# *Letting Go of the Words* — study ledger

## Access and scope

- **Author:** Janice (Ginny) Redish
- **Full title:** *Letting Go of the Words: Writing Web Content That Works*
- **Edition/year:** Second edition, 2012
- **Publisher:** Morgan Kaufmann, an imprint of Elsevier
- **ISBN:** 978-0-12-385930-3
- **Access:** User-supplied local PDF; SHA-256 `1d3873ff99da041fe2e11f108bb98f1c551f9e4591d733b679378065b995b574` matches the intake receipt.
- **Technical form:** 366-page, tagged, unencrypted PDF; metadata says Internet Archive and “Recoded by LuraDocument PDF v2.68.” The PDF remains outside Git.
- **Authenticity boundary:** Title/copyright matter supports bibliographic identity and the contents support structural completeness, but recoding metadata prevents this ledger from claiming publisher-file authenticity.
- **Contents boundary:** The supplied contents enumerate 15 chapters, five interludes, references, an index of cited websites, and a subject index. Contents labels support navigation only; unread sections cannot support detailed claims.
- **Locator policy:** Front matter uses roman numerals: Foreword occupies PDF pages 23–24, acknowledgments PDF pages 25–26, and “Introducing Letting Go of the Words” PDF pages 27–29. Chapter 1 occupies printed pages 1–15/PDF pages 31–45, Chapter 2 occupies printed pages 17–36/PDF pages 47–66, Interlude 1 occupies printed pages 37–44/PDF pages 67–74, Chapter 3 occupies printed pages 45–71/PDF pages 75–101, Chapter 4 occupies printed pages 73–88/PDF pages 103–118, Chapter 5 occupies printed pages 89–100/PDF pages 119–130, Chapter 6 occupies printed pages 101–123/PDF pages 131–153, Chapter 7 occupies printed pages 125–149/PDF pages 155–179, Interlude 2 occupies printed pages 151–155/PDF pages 181–185, Chapter 8 occupies printed pages 157–163/PDF pages 187–193, Chapter 9 occupies printed pages 165–186/PDF pages 195–216 with an unnumbered summary continuation on PDF page 217, Interlude 3 occupies printed pages 189–195/PDF pages 219–225, Chapter 10 occupies printed pages 197–226/PDF pages 227–256, Chapter 11 occupies printed pages 227–251/PDF pages 257–281, Interlude 4 occupies printed pages 253–258/PDF pages 283–288, Chapter 12 occupies printed pages 259–270/PDF pages 289–300, Chapter 13 occupies printed pages 271–287/PDF pages 301–317, Chapter 14 occupies printed pages 289–302/PDF pages 319–332, Interlude 5 occupies printed pages 303–306/PDF pages 333–336, and Chapter 15 occupies printed pages 307–315/PDF pages 337–345. PDF page 346 is a scan-only separator; the bibliography occupies printed pages 317–322/PDF pages 347–352, the subject index printed pages 323–330/PDF pages 353–360, the website-example index printed pages 331–332/PDF pages 361–362, the author biography printed page 333/PDF page 363, and the back cover PDF page 366. PDF pages 22, 30, 46, 102, 154, 180, 186, 194, 218, 226, 282, 318, 364, and 365 are scan-only separators or decorative blanks.
- **Rights/provenance:** The copyright page reserves reproduction and transmission rights. No book prose, screenshots, page images, examples, or fixtures are stored here; this ledger contains original synthesis and locators.
- **Read:** Complete supplied volume: title/copyright matter, contents, Foreword, acknowledgments, introduction, Chapters 1–15, Interludes 1–5, bibliography, both indexes, author biography, and back cover.
- **Not yet read:** None in the supplied volume. Back matter supports only bounded navigation, provenance, intellectual-lineage, and publisher-positioning claims.

## Source-position records

These records preserve the author's and foreword writer's practitioner positions while separating source, edition, evidence, implementation, rights, and authority boundaries. They are not approved `content.md` behavior.

### `LGW-C01` — exact-copy identity and recoding status bound every claim

- **Locator:** Title/copyright matter and PDF metadata, PDF pages 1–10.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The supplied volume identifies Janice Redish's *Letting Go of the Words: Writing Web Content That Works*, second edition, ©2012, published by Morgan Kaufmann/Elsevier, ISBN 978-0-12-385930-3.
- **Candidate implication:** Bind every record to book ID, exact-copy hash, edition, printed/PDF locator, reading cutoff, and technical provenance.
- **Boundary:** Matching title and ISBN do not prove that an Internet Archive recode is the publisher's original file or that scan, tags, reading order, images, and pagination are defect-free.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-exact-copy-provenance; recoded-file-authenticity-unproven`.

### `LGW-C02` — contents establish reading structure, not substantive evidence

- **Locator:** Contents, PDF pages 11–21.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The book presents 15 chapters and five interludes spanning planning, strategy, design, page types, organization, writing, links, illustrations, revision, style guidance, and evaluation.
- **Candidate implication:** Track reading and records by printed/PDF boundary and preserve interludes as separately locatable evidence.
- **Boundary:** Headings and detailed contents cannot support a method, recommendation, or conclusion until the relevant pages are substantively read.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-structural-navigation-only; no-unread-section-claims`.

### `LGW-C03` — book material and screenshots remain rights-bound

- **Locator:** Copyright and trademark notices, PDF page 9; acknowledgments, PDF pages 25–26.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Reproduction and transmission rights are reserved; product and service names are used to comment on displayed screens without endorsement, and many contributors permitted material or images.
- **Candidate implication:** Store only original synthesis and locators; require independent permission, source, date, state, locale, privacy, trademark, and accessibility review for any future example or fixture.
- **Boundary:** The publisher's permissions, trademark rationale, and contributor arrangements do not transfer to this repository or to model/provider use.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-rights-boundary; no-copy-fixture-or-training-authority`.

### `LGW-C04` — second-edition praise is paracontent, not validation

- **Locator:** Praise and Foreword, PDF pages 3–4 and 23–24.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Reviewers and Steve Krug strongly recommend the book and describe the second edition as a thorough rethink incorporating SEO, content strategy, accessibility, mobile, and social media.
- **Candidate implication:** Treat praise as reception and edition-context evidence; evaluate every method independently and compare with newer sources.
- **Boundary:** Testimonials, teaching adoption, reputation, enthusiasm, or a foreword do not establish correctness, universality, currency, accessibility, or causal benefit.
- **Promotion status:** `paracontent-only; no-prestige-as-evidence`.

### `LGW-C05` — the author explicitly scopes examples as contextual and sometimes speculative

- **Locator:** Book introduction, PDF page 28.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Screenshots and proposed revisions illustrate principles; sites change quickly, and revisions for organizations the author did not consult are acknowledged as best guesses.
- **Candidate implication:** Record each example as a dated hypothesis with missing organizational, user, technical, legal, domain, and outcome context.
- **Boundary:** An old example may teach a concept but cannot prove current behavior, transferability, factual accuracy, brand fit, or improvement. A speculative rewrite is not validated design.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-historical-example-boundary; revision-as-hypothesis-only`.

### `LGW-C06` — durable principles still require current implementation evidence

- **Locator:** Book introduction, PDF page 28.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The book focuses on writing and design strategy rather than tools because technology changes quickly and good-writing principles are presented as transcending it.
- **Candidate implication:** Separate durable semantic or research candidates from implementation contracts that must be refreshed for platform, component, search, accessibility, privacy, and locale.
- **Boundary:** No principle is technology-independent once behavior, rendering, input, output, discovery, personalization, or assistive technology affects meaning and access.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-principle-implementation-separation; current-runtime-validation-required`.

### `LGW-C07` — UX, usability, plain language, and reader focus overlap but are not synonyms

- **Locator:** Book introduction, PDF page 28.
- **Documented practitioner position:** User-experience design is described as focusing on people's goals, needs, work, and environments; reader-focused writing, usability, and plain language are grouped as names for aspects of the same aim.
- **Candidate implication:** Preserve relationships among research, content quality, task usability, accessibility, interaction behavior, plain language, and whole experience without collapsing their constructs or evidence.
- **Boundary:** A plain sentence can sit in an unusable or harmful flow; usability can omit usefulness, rights, safety, or affected parties; and UX is broader than reader focus.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-related-construct-separation; no-umbrella-equivalence`.

### `LGW-C08` — the book is a nonlinear reference, not an executable checklist

- **Locator:** Book introduction, PDF pages 28–29.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Readers may follow the proposed sequence, jump to relevant chapters, revisit the book as a reference, or use its contents as a checklist.
- **Candidate implication:** Preserve dependency, applicability, prerequisite, evidence, conflict, authority, and validation metadata when projecting methods into a workflow.
- **Boundary:** Nonlinear use can omit necessary research or risk controls, while a checklist can create ceremonial compliance. Publication order does not establish dependency or release authority.
- **Promotion status:** `candidate-reference-and-review-view; governed-dependencies-required`.

### `LGW-C09` — task and answer needs are a strong content starting point

- **Locator:** Chapter 1, printed page 1; PDF page 31.
- **Documented practitioner position:** People primarily come to sites and apps for information that answers questions or helps complete tasks; navigation, search, design, and technology support that content.
- **Candidate implication:** Start with actor, goal, question, task, state, consequence, information need, success condition, and next step, then design discovery and presentation around them.
- **Boundary:** People may also explore, play, connect, create, deliberate, monitor, express identity, or comply under constraint. Content is not always the sole or primary value, and support layers are not subordinate in safety or access.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-task-and-answer-first-model; nonexclusive-purpose-taxonomy-required`.

### `LGW-C10` — “content equals conversation” is a design metaphor, not a literal relationship

- **Locator:** Book introduction, PDF page 27; Chapter 1, printed pages 1–3; PDF pages 31–33.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Every site or app use is framed as a conversation initiated by the visitor, and content creators should anticipate and satisfy that conversation.
- **Candidate implication:** Use conversational analysis to model likely questions, turns, disclosures, expectations, repair, and follow-up while retaining actual actors and system state.
- **Boundary:** Static pages, automated systems, institutions, and models do not literally listen, understand, reciprocate, or converse. The metaphor can conceal asynchronous production, organizational agency, surveillance, or absent response capability.
- **Promotion status:** `candidate-conversation-analysis-lens; no-human-reciprocity-claim`.

### `LGW-C11` — “phone, not file cabinet” usefully challenges document dumping but overgeneralizes

- **Locator:** Chapter 1, printed pages 3–4; PDF pages 33–34.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Sites should answer people's questions rather than expose organizational documents for visitors to rummage through; the phone is proposed as a better metaphor than a file cabinet.
- **Candidate implication:** Decompose source documents into task-oriented answers and services while preserving authoritative originals, citations, effective dates, record status, downloads, and archival access where needed.
- **Boundary:** People often need complete records, contracts, research, evidence, policies, datasets, or printable documents. A conversational layer must not paraphrase away legal force, provenance, context, or public-record access.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-document-decomposition-candidate; authoritative-record-preservation-required`.

### `LGW-C12` — self-service is a channel choice with equity and service consequences

- **Locator:** Chapter 1, printed page 3; PDF page 33.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The web is described as asking people to serve themselves instead of calling or writing.
- **Candidate implication:** Specify which tasks can be completed independently, which require assisted service, and how people move between accessible channels with context preserved.
- **Boundary:** Self-service can transfer labor, exclude people, conceal support cuts, or be unsafe for urgent, complex, disputed, vulnerable, or inaccessible situations. A human alternative and escalation may be essential.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-self-service-boundary; equivalent-channel-and-escalation-required`.

### `LGW-C13` — user and organization outcomes must remain separate

- **Locator:** Chapter 1, printed pages 3–6; PDF pages 33–36.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Effective content should satisfy visitor conversations, encourage continued or return use, and meet sales, information, membership, or other business goals.
- **Candidate implication:** Model task/answer success, comprehension, access, autonomy, safety, trustworthiness, organization outcome, and affected-party outcome independently with noncompensatory constraints.
- **Boundary:** Continued use, return, sales, membership, and engagement can conflict with the person's goal or result from lock-in, pressure, or failure. Business value cannot validate harm.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-outcome-separation; no-engagement-or-business-compensation`.

### `LGW-C14` — scanning is a context-dependent behavior, not a universal reading law

- **Locator:** Chapter 1, printed pages 4–5; PDF pages 34–35.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Busy visitors often skim navigation, search results, and information pages until they find the answer or task content they came for; they read more when doing, learning, or enjoying relevant material.
- **Candidate implication:** Support orientation, scent, hierarchy, headings, summaries, progressive detail, and resumption while preserving coherent sustained reading where the task requires it.
- **Boundary:** A cited 2006 average and writer observation do not describe every population, disability, language, device, domain, or reading purpose. Designing only for scanning can fragment complex or protective meaning.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-scan-and-read-mode-support; no-universal-short-attention-assumption`.

### `LGW-C15` — information overload can be a product and governance failure

- **Locator:** Chapter 1, printed page 5; PDF page 35.
- **Documented practitioner position:** People read less when busy, when content is irrelevant, or when overwhelmed by too much information.
- **Candidate implication:** Diagnose necessity, relevance, duplication, hierarchy, timing, subscriptions, personalization, defaults, policy, and ownership before shortening prose.
- **Boundary:** People may need detailed information for learning, consent, audit, safety, or comparison. “Overload” cannot justify hiding material facts or blaming readers for organizational accumulation.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-information-burden-diagnostic; material-detail-preserved`.

### `LGW-C16` — questions should shape topic content without becoming an FAQ dump

- **Locator:** Chapter 1, printed page 6; PDF page 36.
- **Documented practitioner position:** For each topic, writers should identify what people want to know and answer clearly and concisely throughout the content rather than turn the whole site into an FAQ collection.
- **Candidate implication:** Map evidence-backed questions to concepts, tasks, states, page purpose, hierarchy, search vocabulary, and follow-up paths; maintain one authoritative answer across projections.
- **Boundary:** Assumed questions are hypotheses, and question phrasing alone does not create findable information architecture. Some questions must be reframed when based on a false premise or unsafe request.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-question-to-content-mapping; evidence-and-canonical-answer-required`.

### `LGW-C17` — “grab and go” is one service mode, not the universal quality target

- **Locator:** Chapter 1, printed page 6; PDF page 36.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Short sections, clear headings, key messages first, compact sentences and paragraphs, lists, tables, meaningful links, and illustrations help people quickly take what they need.
- **Candidate implication:** Represent summary, hierarchy, chunk, list, table, link, illustration, and detail as purpose-specific projections over complete semantic content.
- **Boundary:** Shortness and extraction can remove qualifications, sequence, comparison context, evidence, or narrative coherence. Each format requires accessibility, responsive, localization, and comprehension testing.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-layered-task-content; no-brevity-or-fragmentation-default`.

### `LGW-C18` — satisfaction should precede optional commercial expansion

- **Locator:** Chapter 1, printed pages 6–7; PDF pages 36–37.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Clear, useful content and related information can encourage return, while cross-selling or upselling should follow satisfaction of the visitor-initiated purpose.
- **Candidate implication:** Complete required tasks and recovery before clearly separated optional promotion; preserve refusal, relevance, privacy, frequency, and outcome guardrails.
- **Boundary:** Satisfying one need does not create consent for profiling, promotion, contact, or manipulation. Return behavior is not proof of satisfaction, and necessary services should not exploit completion moments.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-primary-goal-before-promotion; optionality-and-consent-required`.

### `LGW-C19` — SEO guidance is explicitly time-bound

- **Locator:** Chapter 1, printed page 7; PDF page 37.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Useful content using people's search words is presented as a durable SEO foundation, while algorithm-specific advice is acknowledged as changing and requiring current sources.
- **Candidate implication:** Separate audience vocabulary and discoverability intent from provider-specific indexing, rendering, metadata, structured-data, quality, and policy contracts with retrieval date and source.
- **Boundary:** “Great content” does not guarantee indexing or ranking, and search demand does not authorize surveillance, keyword stuffing, misleading scope, or optimization over user outcomes. No 2012 engine claim is current evidence.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-search-guidance-expiry; current-primary-documentation-required`.

### `LGW-C20` — internal search depends on more than matching user words

- **Locator:** Chapter 1, printed page 8; PDF page 38.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Clear writing using visitor vocabulary is described as key to both internal and external search success.
- **Candidate implication:** Govern query language, synonyms, entities, taxonomy, metadata, permissions, locale, ranking, zero results, correction, analytics, privacy, and content freshness alongside visible wording.
- **Boundary:** Vocabulary alignment cannot repair missing indexing, inaccessible results, poor ranking, stale content, permission defects, or unsupported languages. Search logs reflect system behavior and are sensitive evidence, not direct intent.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-vocabulary-search-input; retrieval-system-and-privacy-contract-required`.

### `LGW-C21` — accessibility is a right and implementation property, not a market segment

- **Locator:** Chapter 1, printed page 8; PDF page 38.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The author urges universal usability, cites 2012 disability estimates and legal obligations, and also frames disabled people as customers with money to spend.
- **Candidate implication:** Treat accessibility as a noncompensatory lifecycle obligation spanning content, semantics, components, media, input/output modes, research, testing, procurement, support, and remedy.
- **Boundary:** Population counts, named laws, URLs, and “universal guidelines” are time- and jurisdiction-bound; a commercial case must not determine access. “Accessible to all” is not proven by writing or intent.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-accessibility-obligation; dated-statistic-law-and-market-boundary`.

### `LGW-C22` — financial-service examples require current truth, security, and consent evidence

- **Locator:** Chapter 1, Case Study 1-1, printed pages 9–11; PDF pages 39–41.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Mint is praised for anticipating questions about purpose, benefit, signup, data aggregation, security, and transaction capability with short layered answers.
- **Candidate implication:** Surface data sources, access mode, permissions, processing purpose, security facts, movement authority, limitations, revocation, incidents, and support before financial linkage.
- **Boundary:** “Bank-level,” named seals, encryption figures, “free,” time-to-signup, and read-only claims are historical and require present substantiation. Reassuring copy cannot create security or informed consent.
- **Promotion status:** `historical-financial-example; current-security-privacy-and-consent-proof-required`.

### `LGW-C23` — adoption and asset figures do not establish content causality

- **Locator:** Chapter 1, Case Study 1-1, printed page 11; PDF page 41.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Mint's conversational approach is linked to rapid user growth, assets managed, purchase tracking, and acquisition by Intuit.
- **Candidate implication:** Treat commercial figures as dated organization claims and require an explicit causal design, comparison, confound analysis, user outcomes, and guardrails before attributing effects to content.
- **Boundary:** Growth, assets, transaction volume, and acquisition can result from product capability, distribution, incentives, market conditions, or other factors and may increase privacy or reliance risk.
- **Promotion status:** `historical-outcome-claim; no-content-causality-or-benefit-inference`.

### `LGW-C24` — product pages should resolve decision questions with structured facts

- **Locator:** Chapter 1, Case Study 1-2, printed pages 12–13; PDF pages 42–43.
- **Documented practitioner position:** An eBags product page is praised for answering appearance, dimensions, weight, price, reviews, delivery, returns, and alternatives with little prose.
- **Candidate implication:** Model product identity, variants, media, dimensions, units, price basis, availability, fulfillment, returns, reviews, comparisons, and provenance as structured, accessible, current decision content.
- **Boundary:** Dense visual layouts, strike-through pricing, reviews, recommendations, urgency, and historical claims may mislead or exclude. Few words do not prove comprehension, accessibility, accuracy, or support reduction.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-structured-decision-content; commerce-truth-access-and-provenance-required`.

### `LGW-C25` — public-participation instructions are an access and timing contract

- **Locator:** Chapter 1, Case Study 1-3, printed pages 13–15; PDF pages 43–45.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Meeting instructions should appear before the deadline, identify how and where to obtain a request card, use direct steps, and separate time limits and agenda conditions.
- **Candidate implication:** Specify eligibility, channel, location, deadline, sequence, required information, accommodation, language access, remote alternative, confirmation, speaking order, time limits, exceptions, and appeal before participation closes.
- **Boundary:** A clearer rewrite cannot supply missing facts, change an exclusionary process, ensure legal notice, or support people unable to attend or use the prescribed card. Domain and accessibility review remain necessary.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-procedural-instruction-contract; policy-access-and-missing-fact-escalation-required`.

### `LGW-C26` — missing information must be resolved before prose is finalized

- **Locator:** Chapter 1, Case Study 1-3, printed page 14; PDF page 44.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The first revision step is to obtain the missing answer about where a participation card is available, then restructure and reposition the instructions.
- **Candidate implication:** Represent unknown, owner, blocking severity, question, source, resolution, approval, and expiry; prevent unsupported details from being filled by fluent drafting.
- **Boundary:** A writer cannot infer operational facts from style guidance. Some unknowns reveal policy, service, data, or implementation defects requiring accountable decisions.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-unknown-escalation; no-fluent-fabrication`.

### `LGW-C27` — Chapter 1 offers a task-content lens, not a complete content model

- **Locator:** Chapter 1, printed pages 1–15; PDF pages 31–45.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The chapter connects goals, questions, scanning, reading, conversation, search, accessibility, marketing, structured decision information, and procedural revision.
- **Candidate implication:** Preserve task/question analysis, semantic hierarchy, discoverability, accessibility, structured facts, unknown resolution, and outcome separation as source-scoped candidates for cross-book synthesis.
- **Boundary:** The chapter underdevelops multi-actor state, authoritative records, consent, security, privacy, localization, service operations, causality, runtime semantics, and harm. Current external standards remain unverified because the Developer Index quota was exhausted.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-task-content-foundation; incomplete-and-nonexecuting-model`.

### `LGW-C28` — planning is continuous and applies at multiple content scopes

- **Locator:** Chapter 2, printed page 17; PDF page 47.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Writers should plan before drafting and continue planning through revision at site, page, app, article, message, and component scope.
- **Candidate implication:** Maintain purpose, actor, task, question, state, source, constraint, decision, unknown, and validation records throughout discovery, drafting, implementation, release, and maintenance.
- **Boundary:** A brief cannot freeze learning, and continuous planning must not become endless unowned reconsideration. Scope changes require explicit impact, authority, and version control.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-continuous-multiscope-planning; decision-and-version-governance-required`.

### `LGW-C29` — why, who, and desired interaction are distinct planning dimensions

- **Locator:** Chapter 2, printed page 17; PDF page 47.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Every content effort should ask why it exists, who it serves, and what conversation those people want to have.
- **Candidate implication:** Separate organization purpose, affected actor, user goal, task, question, interaction state, desired outcome, and evidence rather than storing one undifferentiated brief.
- **Boundary:** “Conversation” remains a metaphor and may omit bystanders, subjects, administrators, regulators, or people unable to initiate contact. The organization is not a single speaker.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-purpose-actor-interaction-separation; multi-actor-and-agency-model-required`.

### `LGW-C30` — organization goals should be translated into user-visible behavior without scripting desire

- **Locator:** Chapter 2, printed page 18; PDF page 48.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Goals such as selling shoes or increasing subscriptions are reframed as actions visitors take or engagement that leads them to subscribe.
- **Candidate implication:** Express organization goals as proposed user behaviors, then test whether the behavior is voluntary, necessary, informed, valuable, reversible, and consistent with the person's own goal.
- **Boundary:** Rephrasing a conversion target in user terms does not make it user-centered. “Should do” and “feel engaged” can legitimize manipulation, withheld functionality, coercive defaults, or optimization of attachment.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-goal-reframing-boundary; voluntary-user-value-and-autonomy-required`.

### `LGW-C31` — a specific purpose statement is a hypothesis, not authorization

- **Locator:** Chapter 2, printed pages 18–19; PDF pages 48–49.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Writers should move beyond “inform, persuade, educate” and complete a specific statement identifying who should be able to do what or what effect the writing should have.
- **Candidate implication:** Store actor, intended capability or decision, context, consequence, success evidence, exclusions, risk, owner, and approval for each purpose.
- **Boundary:** Specificity can make a harmful goal easier to optimize. Persuasion, forgiveness, behavior change, or organizational effects require ethical, factual, legal/domain, and affected-party review.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-specific-purpose-schema; no-purpose-as-permission`.

### `LGW-C32` — discoverability and accessibility are noncompensatory requirements, not optional goals

- **Locator:** Chapter 2, printed page 19; PDF page 49.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Organic search visibility and universal usability are recommended as purposes for most or all sites.
- **Candidate implication:** Treat relevant discovery and accessibility as separately governed qualities with current technical sources, affected populations, tests, exceptions, and remediation.
- **Boundary:** Ranking is not universally desirable for private, sensitive, duplicate, temporary, or restricted content. “Good business” cannot ground accessibility rights, and “everyone” cannot be proven by an intention statement.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-discovery-access-separation; privacy-and-accessibility-governance-required`.

### `LGW-C33` — each content unit needs a traceable purpose and lifecycle

- **Locator:** Chapter 2, printed page 19; PDF page 49.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Overall properties and every part, article, message, or app element should have a reason and an intended result.
- **Candidate implication:** Require semantic ID, scope, purpose, audience/affected roles, source, owner, authority, state, channel, locale, review, expiry, dependency, and outcome for governed content units.
- **Boundary:** Not every atom needs a unique business metric, and shared infrastructure, legal records, accessibility metadata, or consistency patterns may support multiple purposes. Purpose cannot be invented merely to preserve content.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-content-purpose-lineage; shared-and-obligatory-content-supported`.

### `LGW-C34` — find, understand, act, time, and effort are separate usability claims

- **Locator:** Chapter 2, printed page 20; PDF page 50.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Success is defined as finding needed content, understanding it, acting appropriately, and doing so within acceptable time and effort; this is linked to usability and plain language.
- **Candidate implication:** Measure discovery, comprehension, decision quality, action correctness, completion, time, effort, accessibility, confidence, recovery, and downstream outcome independently.
- **Boundary:** “Act appropriately” embeds an authority judgment and may conflict with autonomy; acceptable effort varies; plain language does not prove finding, action, or whole-experience usability.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-multidimensional-task-quality; no-plain-language-equivalence-or-compensatory-score`.

### `LGW-C35` — internal experience is not user evidence

- **Locator:** Chapter 2, printed page 20; PDF page 50.
- **Documented practitioner position:** “We are not our users”; creators should not generalize their technology adoption, social-media behavior, vocabulary, or preferences to typical visitors.
- **Candidate implication:** Label internal intuition, stakeholder assertion, domain expertise, participant evidence, behavioral telemetry, and production outcome as distinct evidence types.
- **Boundary:** Researchers and team members may also belong to relevant populations, while “typical user” can erase minority or high-risk needs. Difference does not justify treating participants as exotic or homogeneous.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-internal-assumption-boundary; situated-and-inclusive-evidence-required`.

### `LGW-C36` — interpretation depends on prior knowledge and context

- **Locator:** Chapter 2, printed pages 20–21; PDF pages 50–51.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Readers actively interpret words through their own knowledge, experience, vocabulary, and meanings rather than passively receiving a writer's message.
- **Candidate implication:** Test concepts and terms across roles, expertise, locale, culture, disability, preceding context, and realistic tasks; preserve alternate labels and definitions where evidence supports them.
- **Boundary:** Interpretation is not unlimited subjectivity: domain facts, system states, and legal meanings still require authority. Writers cannot know individual mental models merely from group membership.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-interpretive-context-model; domain-authority-and-user-evidence-required`.

### `LGW-C37` — direct research should complement, not erase, other evidence

- **Locator:** Chapter 2, printed pages 21–23; PDF pages 51–53.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Observation, listening, and direct conversation with current or potential visitors are preferred over assumptions or information filtered through colleagues, alongside mission, feedback, service, marketing, analytics, social, surveys, interviews, field visits, and testing.
- **Candidate implication:** Triangulate methods by research question, population, context, evidence strength, access, consent, privacy, analysis, negative cases, and decision scope.
- **Boundary:** Direct contact is still sampled, reactive, interpreted, and power-laden; indirect operational evidence can reveal events participants cannot recall or safely discuss. No method provides a “true picture” alone.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-mixed-method-triangulation; no-direct-contact-as-unfiltered-truth`.

### `LGW-C38` — mission identifies a duty claim, not the served population by itself

- **Locator:** Chapter 2, printed page 21; PDF page 51.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Teams may begin audience inquiry by asking whom the organization's mission says it should serve and what it should help them accomplish.
- **Candidate implication:** Compare stated mission with law/policy, actual eligibility, service behavior, usage, exclusion, unmet need, affected parties, and community evidence.
- **Boundary:** Missions can be vague, promotional, outdated, contested, or structurally exclusionary. They do not prove whom the service reaches or whose interests control.
- **Promotion status:** `candidate-mission-as-assertion; service-and-exclusion-evidence-required`.

### `LGW-C39` — feedback and support records require provenance and privacy controls

- **Locator:** Chapter 2, printed pages 21–22; PDF pages 51–52.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Contact messages, page feedback, reviews, recommendations, blogs, and customer-service questions are suggested as evidence about audiences and needs.
- **Candidate implication:** Record channel, consent/notice, purpose, access, sensitivity, identity status, sampling, transformation, retention, redaction, coding, representativeness, and deletion for operational language evidence.
- **Boundary:** Writers may disclose secrets, health, financial, employment, or third-party data; complainants and support users are not representative by default; public availability does not equal permission for reuse or training.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-operational-feedback-governance; no-public-or-support-data-free-use`.

### `LGW-C40` — stakeholder teams provide scoped perspectives, not user truth

- **Locator:** Chapter 2, printed pages 21–22; PDF pages 51–52.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Customer service, marketing, usability, and UX colleagues can contribute what they know about questions, target groups, and prior research.
- **Candidate implication:** Preserve source role, contact proximity, method, incentives, date, population, evidence artifact, interpretation, conflict, and authority for every stakeholder contribution.
- **Boundary:** Marketing targets, support volume, research summaries, and colleague memory answer different questions and can reflect organizational filters or incentives. Agreement does not establish validity.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-stakeholder-evidence-scoping; no-proxy-or-consensus-as-user-truth`.

### `LGW-C41` — search logs reveal entered queries, not stable intent or identity

- **Locator:** Chapter 2, printed pages 22 and 29; PDF pages 52 and 59.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Site-search terms are recommended for learning what visitors seek and the words they use, while the author notes that queries reveal “what” rather than “who.”
- **Candidate implication:** Treat query text as sensitive event evidence with timestamp, surface, locale, filters, result set, reformulation, click/action, zero-result state, retention, aggregation, and uncertainty.
- **Boundary:** Queries can be incomplete, adversarial, accidental, proxy-entered, constrained by interface vocabulary, or contain personal data. They do not directly reveal intent, preferred terminology, comprehension, identity, or desired outcome.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-search-query-evidence-boundary; privacy-context-and-outcome-linkage-required`.

### `LGW-C42` — analytics events cannot explain cause by themselves

- **Locator:** Chapter 2, printed page 22; PDF page 52.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Analytics can show acquisition, paths, dwell, exits, devices, browsers, and search words, but a quick exit may mean either success or failure.
- **Candidate implication:** Separate event, metric, construct, interpretation, hypothesis, qualitative evidence, experiment, confound, segment, guardrail, and causal confidence.
- **Boundary:** Dwell, exit, path, and device data may be incomplete or privacy-sensitive; third-party referrer/search data change over time. No behavioral event proves comprehension, emotion, satisfaction, or intent.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-event-interpretation-separation; no-analytics-as-causal-or-emotional-proof`.

### `LGW-C43` — social listening and community participation need ethical boundaries

- **Locator:** Chapter 2, printed page 22; PDF page 52.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Teams are encouraged to participate in relevant online groups, ask questions, and track conversations mentioning their organization, brand, or site.
- **Candidate implication:** Require community norms, researcher identity, notice/consent where appropriate, platform terms, vulnerability review, quotation permission, de-identification, retention, safety, and noninterference.
- **Boundary:** A searchable or public conversation is not automatically research consent. Monitoring can become surveillance, chill speech, expose pseudonymous people, or extract marginalized-community knowledge.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-social-listening-boundary; community-consent-privacy-and-safety-required`.

### `LGW-C44` — surveys need construct, sampling, consent, and burden controls

- **Locator:** Chapter 2, printed page 22; PDF page 52.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Short questionnaires may ask visitors about themselves, their reason for visiting, and whether they found what they needed.
- **Candidate implication:** Define research question, construct, sampling frame, trigger, eligibility, consent, necessity, response options, accessibility, locale, burden, incentive, nonresponse, privacy, analysis, and decision scope.
- **Boundary:** Self-report, intercept timing, survivor bias, leading wording, and optional response limit inference. Asking “about themselves” can collect sensitive demographics without decision value.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-survey-governance; no-short-questionnaire-as-neutral-evidence`.

### `LGW-C45` — interviews and field observation require participant protection

- **Locator:** Chapter 2, printed pages 22–23; PDF pages 52–53.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Contextual and critical-incident interviews, observation in workplaces or service locations, and visits to grantees are recommended for understanding real tasks, environments, technology, values, and vocabulary.
- **Candidate implication:** Govern recruitment, consent, recording, third parties, workplace power, sensitive tasks, safety, compensation, withdrawal, minimization, access, notes, interpretation, and reporting.
- **Boundary:** Observation changes behavior and can expose customers, patients, coworkers, children, or confidential operations. A visitor's story is situated evidence, not a full process record or permission to reproduce language.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-contextual-research-value; participant-third-party-and-workplace-protection-required`.

### `LGW-C46` — content testing must include resolved information and application tasks

- **Locator:** Chapter 2, printed pages 22–23; PDF pages 52–53.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Usability studies should examine information and application pages, not navigation alone, using participant-generated or assigned tasks and inquiry about needs and content use.
- **Candidate implication:** Test discovery, comprehension, decision, action, recovery, accessibility, trust calibration, and downstream effects in realistic content, state, role, device, and environment.
- **Boundary:** Assigned tasks may cue behavior, participant accounts are self-report, and a study session cannot prove production reliability, long-term learning, policy fairness, or rare harm.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-content-inclusive-task-testing; production-and-longitudinal-boundaries-required`.

### `LGW-C47` — audience groups should be decision-relevant and person-centered

- **Locator:** Chapter 2, printed page 23; PDF page 53.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Groups may reflect self-identification, role, expertise, frequency, location, or journey intent; departments and institutions should not be treated as users.
- **Candidate implication:** Define each segment by the content or interaction decision it changes, membership evidence, overlap, variability, excluded cases, lifecycle, and affected-person relationship.
- **Boundary:** Labels such as patient, professional, tourist, browser, or booker can flatten intersectional roles and changing intent. Organizations may still be legal actors while humans perform their tasks.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-decision-relevant-human-segmentation; overlap-and-institutional-role-preserved`.

### `LGW-C48` — characteristic categories are research prompts, not profile fields by default

- **Locator:** Chapter 2, printed pages 23–29; PDF pages 53–59.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Teams are encouraged to consider quotes, expertise, emotion, values, technology, social/cultural environment, language, and demographics for each group.
- **Candidate implication:** Require a concrete decision, necessity, source, uncertainty, sensitivity, bias risk, retention, and prohibited-use rule before collecting or inferring any characteristic.
- **Boundary:** A broad planning checklist can normalize surveillance, stereotyping, and sensitive profiling. Many content decisions can be made through task, environment, and access evidence without personal traits.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-characteristic-minimization; no-profile-field-by-checklist`.

### `LGW-C49` — participant phrases must remain attributed evidence, not invented voice

- **Locator:** Chapter 2, printed pages 24 and 29; PDF pages 54 and 59.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Key phrases and unaltered visitor vocabulary are recommended for keeping busyness, goals, information needs, and audience words visible in content work.
- **Candidate implication:** Store quotation status, speaker consent, context, date, method, transcription, transformation, aggregation, sensitivity, representativeness, and permitted use separately from synthesized terminology.
- **Boundary:** Illustrative speech bubbles may be author/team inventions, and verbatim reuse can expose identity or appropriate dialect. One participant's wording is not a canonical label.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-participant-language-provenance; no-invented-or-verbatim-appropriated-voice`.

### `LGW-C50` — expertise requires layered concepts, not universal simplification

- **Locator:** Chapter 2, printed pages 24–25; PDF pages 54–55.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Vocabulary and detail should reflect subject expertise; travel jargon can be avoided, while patient and professional cancer information may be offered in separate versions accessible to both.
- **Candidate implication:** Model canonical concept, plain explanation, domain term, alias, definition, role, depth, cross-link, locale, and authority so people can move between layers.
- **Boundary:** Expert and novice are task-specific and fluid; separate versions can drift or segregate access. “Simple words work best” cannot override precise clinical, legal, technical, or safety terminology.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-expertise-layering; canonical-parity-and-domain-precision-required`.

### `LGW-C51` — emotion should be observed as context, not assigned as identity

- **Locator:** Chapter 2, printed pages 25–26; PDF pages 55–56.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Teams are asked to anticipate states such as anxiety, anger, impatience, skepticism, pressure, or frustration for injured workers, reporters, and people troubleshooting products.
- **Candidate implication:** Prefer known situational facts and self-report; represent possible interpretations with uncertainty, prohibit emotion-targeted exploitation, and use a neutral safe default.
- **Boundary:** Role or task does not prove emotion, and rhetorical questions invite stereotype. Software cannot know inner state from a segment, behavior, or error; stress-sensitive clarity does not require labeling the person.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-emotion-inference-boundary; state-evidence-neutral-default-and-nonexploitation-required`.

### `LGW-C52` — values claims require direct, plural, and conflict-aware evidence

- **Locator:** Chapter 2, printed page 27; PDF page 57.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Knowing what visitors value—time, price transparency, policy comprehension, health trust—may guide selection and emphasis.
- **Candidate implication:** Distinguish stated priority, observed tradeoff, situational constraint, organization interpretation, and normative value; preserve variation and conflict across affected groups.
- **Boundary:** Task behavior does not reveal stable personal values, and values can be sensitive or manipulated. Organizational convenience must not be recast as what users value.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-value-evidence-separation; no-behavior-to-stable-value-inference`.

### `LGW-C53` — technology context must be capability-based and current

- **Locator:** Chapter 2, printed pages 27–28; PDF pages 57–58.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Connection speed and cost, screen size, device, browser, interruption, duration, location, companions, and proxy work can affect content; the author avoids fixed statistics because technology changes.
- **Candidate implication:** Model current capabilities and constraints—viewport, input/output, bandwidth, offline state, data cost, assistive technology, interruption, privacy, environment, and resumption—without tying support solely to device labels.
- **Boundary:** Analytics see reported technical events, not all needs; browser and resolution statistics exclude blocked or failed sessions. Responsive content cannot be inferred from screen size alone.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-context-capability-model; current-inclusive-and-failure-aware-evidence-required`.

### `LGW-C54` — culture, language, and environment are contextual, not deterministic traits

- **Locator:** Chapter 2, printed page 28; PDF page 58.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Planning should consider location, company, language familiarity, culture, social participation, interruption, prolonged use, and serving another person.
- **Candidate implication:** Represent locale, language preference and proficiency where voluntarily supplied, jurisdiction, environment, role, collaboration, interruption, and channel as separate contextual fields.
- **Boundary:** Country, culture, or language cannot predict preference, comprehension, literacy, or behavior. Proxy work may involve consent, authorization, confidentiality, and identity risks beyond content style.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-context-of-use; no-cultural-or-language-determinism`.

### `LGW-C55` — demographics must not stand in for needs or capabilities

- **Locator:** Chapter 2, printed pages 28–29; PDF pages 58–59.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Age and other demographics may affect writing and design, but older populations are described as diverse in aptitude, attitude, and ability, and aggregate percentages leave important countergroups.
- **Candidate implication:** Use direct task, capability, preference, barrier, and outcome evidence where possible; require necessity, consent, sensitivity, fairness, subgroup analysis, and expiry for demographic data.
- **Boundary:** Age, family status, education, disability, and other traits are not reliable proxies for literacy, technology use, cognition, or desired tone. “Aptitude,” “attitude,” and “ability” can encode deficit framing.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-demographic-proxy-boundary; direct-need-and-fairness-evidence-required`.

### `LGW-C56` — persona composites need traceable evidence and uncertainty

- **Locator:** Chapter 2, printed pages 29–32; PDF pages 59–62.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Personas combine characteristics of real people into named, pictured individuals so teams can remember research, and they should remain faithful to collected data rather than resemble creators.
- **Candidate implication:** Link every persona attribute, task, quote, need, barrier, and scenario to source evidence, prevalence or variation, uncertainty, conflicts, exclusions, version, owner, and permitted decision scope.
- **Boundary:** A coherent fictional individual can imply correlations the research never observed, erase negative cases, and turn sampled data into false population truth. Memorability is not validity.
- **Promotion status:** `candidate-evidence-projection-persona; attribute-lineage-and-false-coherence-controls-required`.

### `LGW-C57` — persona names and images add rights and stereotype risk

- **Locator:** Chapter 2, printed pages 30–31; PDF pages 60–61.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Respectful names and resonant pictures are recommended; stock, shared, friend, or family photos require suitable licenses or permission, and known individuals should be avoided to preserve the fiction.
- **Candidate implication:** Prefer minimal necessary representation; record image source, license, consent, likeness risk, demographic signal, stereotype review, expiry, and removal, or use nonportrait alternatives.
- **Boundary:** Permission does not prevent stereotyping or contextual misuse. A photo can falsely imply that one appearance represents a group, while a fictional name can invite cultural caricature or reidentification.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-persona-media-rights-and-stereotype-boundary; nonportrait-option-required`.

### `LGW-C58` — demographic realism can manufacture causal stories

- **Locator:** Chapter 2, printed pages 30–32; PDF pages 60–62.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Age, family, education, employment, income, location, household, quotes, and narrative are used to make personas feel real and identify represented groups.
- **Candidate implication:** Include only attributes that change an authorized decision and explicitly mark synthetic joins, range, variability, source, and prohibited inference.
- **Boundary:** Decorative biography encourages stereotypes and can imply unsupported relationships among poverty, family, disability, caution, education, or technology. “Realness” is not a research quality criterion.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-decorative-demographic-rejection; decision-necessity-and-synthetic-join-disclosure-required`.

### `LGW-C59` — one disabled persona cannot carry accessibility coverage

- **Locator:** Chapter 2, printed page 32; PDF page 62.
- **Documented practitioner position:** A persona using a screen reader or magnifier is suggested as a reminder to make everything accessible to everyone.
- **Candidate implication:** Include disabled people throughout research and scenarios while maintaining independent standards, component tests, multiple disability/input/output coverage, procurement, monitoring, and remediation.
- **Boundary:** A vision-centered persona can tokenize disability, imply one “special need,” and make conformance depend on team empathy or memory. Accessibility is not a persona trait or edge case.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-disability-persona-tokenism-boundary; independent-disability-inclusive-governance-required`.

### `LGW-C60` — persona quantity follows decision coverage, not example counts

- **Locator:** Chapter 2, printed page 32; PDF page 62.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Examples use six, eight, or nine personas, including primary groups, secondary groups, and even a software robot.
- **Candidate implication:** Derive the smallest sufficient set of evidence projections from distinct tasks, needs, risks, contexts, and design decisions; preserve nonhuman actors separately from affected people.
- **Boundary:** Historical counts are not heuristics. Too many personas fragment attention; too few erase difference; treating software as a human persona can confuse actor, agent, system, owner, and accountability.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-decision-coverage-based-persona-set; no-numeric-or-human-agent-conflation`.

### `LGW-C61` — personas are discussion artifacts, not team members or decision authorities

- **Locator:** Chapter 2, printed pages 32–33; PDF pages 62–63.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Teams display, name, email, and speak about personas as if they were members in order to keep audiences visible during work.
- **Candidate implication:** Use personas as explicitly fictional research projections in critique, always linking back to evidence, participant voices, unresolved variation, and accountable human decision owners.
- **Boundary:** Anthropomorphizing an artifact can replace actual participation, let facilitators ventriloquize users, and give a memorable fiction more authority than contradictory evidence.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-persona-authority-boundary; no-fictional-user-ventriloquism`.

### `LGW-C62` — traffic change cannot validate persona practice

- **Locator:** Chapter 2, printed page 33; PDF page 63.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Visible persona use is associated with a reported 47% annual traffic increase for a music site.
- **Candidate implication:** Record the claim as a dated anecdote and require design, baseline, attribution, confound, source, population, user outcome, and guardrail evidence before drawing a causal conclusion.
- **Boundary:** Traffic may reflect campaigns, search changes, content supply, measurement changes, or market events and does not establish comprehension, inclusion, benefit, or persona causality.
- **Promotion status:** `historical-anecdote-only; no-traffic-as-persona-validation`.

### `LGW-C63` — scenarios should expose state, actors, constraints, and consequences

- **Locator:** Chapter 2, printed pages 33–35; PDF pages 63–65.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Short or long stories connect a persona's goals, interruptions, vocabulary, financial constraints, confidence, collaboration, and common reasons for using content.
- **Candidate implication:** Model initiating actor, affected actors, trigger, prior state, goal, task, environment, interruption, permission, information need, decision, risk, success, failure, recovery, and evidence for each scenario.
- **Boundary:** Narrative vividness can hide unsupported details, stereotyped causality, rare cases, policy constraints, and alternative paths. Scenarios are hypotheses and test frames, not requirements or population truth.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-scenario-state-model; no-narrative-as-evidence-or-requirement`.

### `LGW-C64` — secondary and low-frequency actors still require consequence-based coverage

- **Locator:** Chapter 2, printed page 34; PDF page 64.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Major personas receive several scenarios, while reporters, investors, and other secondary groups may receive mini-personas and scenarios.
- **Candidate implication:** Prioritize scenario depth by consequence, rights, vulnerability, frequency, strategic importance, uncertainty, and service obligation rather than traffic alone.
- **Boundary:** “Secondary” can erase people with low volume but high stakes, accessibility needs, oversight roles, or legal rights. A mini-persona cannot justify reduced protection.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-consequence-based-scenario-coverage; no-frequency-only-prioritization`.

### `LGW-C65` — scenario absence is not sufficient reason to delete content

- **Locator:** Chapter 2, printed pages 34–36; PDF pages 64–66.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Everything on a site should relate to a plausible visitor scenario; otherwise it consumes space and may distract in search results.
- **Candidate implication:** Require a documented purpose or obligation for content and investigate orphaned material against tasks, records, law/policy, accessibility, safety, operations, history, APIs, search, and rare-event needs before disposition.
- **Boundary:** No known scenario may reflect incomplete research. Records, disclosures, emergency information, evidence, governance, machine consumers, or future recovery may be required despite low demand. Deletion needs authority, retention, redirect, archive, and impact review.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-scenario-pruning-boundary; obligation-retention-and-impact-review-required`.

### `LGW-C66` — Chapter 2 provides research scaffolding, not a governed evidence system

- **Locator:** Chapter 2, printed pages 17–36; PDF pages 47–66.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The chapter moves from purpose and audience inquiry through mixed evidence, grouping, characteristics, personas, and scenarios to guide content decisions.
- **Candidate implication:** Preserve the progression while adding research question, method, consent, privacy, sampling, evidence type, analysis, uncertainty, sensitive inference, authority, decision, validation, lifecycle, and affected-party controls.
- **Boundary:** The chapter's broad collection prompts and vivid composites can encourage surveillance, stereotyping, false coherence, and unsupported decisions. Current external research guidance remains unverified because the Developer Index quota was exhausted.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-planning-research-scaffold; full-evidence-governance-required`.

### `LGW-C67` — field popularity and search-result counts are historical context only

- **Locator:** Interlude 1, printed page 37; PDF page 67.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The interlude describes rapid growth of the “content strategy” label through conferences, meetups, books, job titles, and 1.5 million Google results around 2012.
- **Candidate implication:** Record disciplinary vocabulary with date, source, community, meaning, and predecessor practices; evaluate methods rather than adopting terminology from popularity.
- **Boundary:** Search-result counts are unstable and methodologically weak, while conferences, titles, and publications do not establish consensus, maturity, efficacy, or current scope.
- **Promotion status:** `historical-field-context-only; no-popularity-as-authority`.

### `LGW-C68` — content strategy must connect user needs and organization intent without making business goals supreme

- **Locator:** Interlude 1, printed pages 37–38; PDF pages 67–68.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Content strategy is defined as thinking strategically about content, aligning it with the business plan and goals, and combining audience focus with organizational understanding.
- **Candidate implication:** Model organization goals, user goals, affected-party rights, public/service duties, content purpose, constraints, conflicts, and noncompensatory requirements explicitly.
- **Boundary:** Business alignment can institutionalize manipulation, exclusion, surveillance, unsafe policy, or short-term optimization. Strategy must also support legal/domain truth, accessibility, privacy, safety, labor, preservation, and remedy.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-strategic-alignment; no-business-goal-supremacy`.

### `LGW-C69` — consistency does not independently create credibility or truth

- **Locator:** Interlude 1, printed page 37; PDF page 67.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Consistent messages are said to build credibility, strengthen brand, keep writers on purpose, and prevent clutter from outdated or inaccurate content.
- **Candidate implication:** Separate semantic consistency, terminology consistency, expression coherence, accuracy, evidence, freshness, credibility judgments, and brand outcomes.
- **Boundary:** A coordinated falsehood can be consistently expressed, and justified variation may be required by role, locale, channel, law, or state. Credibility is not proof of trustworthiness.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-consistency-credibility-boundary; truth-and-context-control`.

### `LGW-C70` — governance spans creation through removal and preservation

- **Locator:** Interlude 1, printed page 38; PDF page 68.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Governed content is planned, coordinated, regularly reviewed, assigned to someone, maintained, and removed when outdated.
- **Candidate implication:** Define intake, source, authoring, review, approval, publication, distribution, monitoring, correction, versioning, deprecation, redirect, archive, retention, legal hold, deletion, and remedy.
- **Boundary:** “Someone in charge” is too vague, and outdated content may still be an authoritative historical record. Removal requires dependency, rights, retention, citation, route, and affected-user review.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-content-lifecycle-governance; preservation-dependency-and-deletion-controls-required`.

### `LGW-C71` — message architecture must preserve propositions and authority

- **Locator:** Interlude 1, printed pages 38 and 40; PDF pages 68 and 70.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Organizations should define key messages, prioritize communication goals, and align each content item's message with the wider strategy.
- **Candidate implication:** Represent proposition, source, evidence, scope, priority, audience, channel, conflicts, owner, approver, effective period, and required disclosure separately from expression.
- **Boundary:** A “key message” can be promotional framing rather than verified fact. Priority cannot suppress material costs, risks, uncertainty, alternatives, dissent, or legal/domain statements.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-message-proposition-governance; no-priority-as-omission-authority`.

### `LGW-C72` — media choices require equivalent meaning and rights controls

- **Locator:** Interlude 1, printed pages 38–40; PDF pages 68–70.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Strategy should deliberately select text, illustration, charts, graphs, tables, forms, PDFs, video, podcasts, blogs, forums, and social media with appropriate visual, audio, style, and tone guidelines.
- **Candidate implication:** Bind each semantic purpose to suitable modalities, alternatives, captions/transcripts, data source, responsive behavior, accessibility, localization, rights, privacy, performance, and fallback.
- **Boundary:** A media mix is not accessible or effective by intention; PDF, audio, video, chart, form, and social content have distinct implementation and preservation contracts.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-multimodal-content-strategy; equivalence-rights-and-runtime-validation-required`.

### `LGW-C73` — roles need typed authority, separation, and escalation

- **Locator:** Interlude 1, printed pages 38–39; PDF pages 68–69.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Strategy should identify who writes, edits, illustrates, produces, publishes, manages social media, decides future content, maintains the strategy, and what skills they need.
- **Candidate implication:** Define requester, source owner, author, editor, domain reviewer, legal/privacy/security/accessibility/localization reviewer, approver, publisher, operator, incident owner, archivist, and accountable executive with escalation and separation of duties.
- **Boundary:** Job title, background, access to a CMS, or “being in charge” does not establish competence or authority. Small teams may combine roles but cannot erase required review functions.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-typed-content-roles; competence-authority-and-separation-required`.

### `LGW-C74` — process and training are production controls

- **Locator:** Interlude 1, printed page 39; PDF page 69.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Teams should decide how contributors work together, which systems they use, and what strategy, process, and technology training they receive.
- **Candidate implication:** Govern intake, handoff, review, service levels, permissions, training, competency evidence, documentation, exceptions, audit logs, support, and continuous learning.
- **Boundary:** Training cannot compensate for unsafe permissions, unusable tools, understaffing, conflicting incentives, or missing authority. Attendance is not competence or adoption evidence.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-content-operations-control; no-training-as-systemic-repair`.

### `LGW-C75` — strategy should guide tooling while technical constraints remain first-class

- **Locator:** Interlude 1, printed page 39; PDF page 69.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The CMS should support content strategy rather than drive or constrain it.
- **Candidate implication:** Derive requirements from semantic models, workflows, roles, channels, locales, accessibility, privacy, security, versioning, preview, APIs, migration, observability, and preservation before tool selection.
- **Boundary:** Strategy cannot ignore feasibility, platform contracts, vendor risk, cost, interoperability, performance, or legacy data. Tool and strategy must co-evolve through explicit tradeoffs rather than a one-way hierarchy.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-strategy-led-tooling; bidirectional-feasibility-and-risk-review-required`.

### `LGW-C76` — accessibility must be embedded in governance, not remembered as an audience need

- **Locator:** Interlude 1, printed page 39; PDF page 69.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Content strategists are reminded to plan for usability and accessibility for all visitors.
- **Candidate implication:** Bind accessibility requirements, disabled participation, semantic structure, components, media alternatives, procurement, testing, exceptions, monitoring, incidents, and remediation to every lifecycle stage.
- **Boundary:** A reminder, persona, or broad “all” goal does not establish conformance. Current standards and affected-user evidence remain necessary.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-accessibility-in-governance; no-reminder-as-assurance`.

### `LGW-C77` — cross-functional alignment needs conflict and decision governance

- **Locator:** Interlude 1, printed page 39; PDF page 69.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Brand, business, documentation, marketing, social media, web content, and other strategists should coordinate content as part of organization-wide strategy.
- **Candidate implication:** Record shared objectives, domain boundaries, conflicts, dependencies, evidence, affected parties, decision owner, dissent, approval, and escalation across functions.
- **Boundary:** Alignment and consistency can suppress specialist objections or public/user interests. Collaboration does not establish truth, permission, safety, or a resolved tradeoff.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-cross-functional-strategy; conflict-dissent-and-authority-required`.

### `LGW-C78` — channel strategy requires semantic continuity, not identical content

- **Locator:** Interlude 1, printed page 40; PDF page 70.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Web, print, email, advertising, blogs, social platforms, and other channels should support each other and organizational messages while deliberately varying media, style, and tone.
- **Candidate implication:** Maintain canonical propositions and state while adapting length, interaction, accessibility, privacy, timing, locale, legal disclosure, and recovery to each channel.
- **Boundary:** Identical wording can fail channel needs, while variation can create semantic drift. Not every channel should carry every message, and cross-channel tracking or contact requires consent and minimization.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-cross-channel-semantic-continuity; channel-specific-contracts-required`.

### `LGW-C79` — social publishing is an operated service with safety obligations

- **Locator:** Interlude 1, printed pages 40–41; PDF pages 70–71.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Strategy should define bloggers, comments, moderation, responses, guests, platforms, groups, resources, posting frequency, permitted topics, and register.
- **Candidate implication:** Specify account authority, identity, consent, moderation policy, response scope, accessibility, privacy, records, security, abuse/harassment response, crisis escalation, staffing, hours, retention, deletion, and exit.
- **Boundary:** Posting cadence and colloquial style are minor beside community safety and operational capacity. Monitoring or engagement can become surveillance, endorsement, or an unfulfillable service promise.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-social-service-operations; safety-privacy-records-and-escalation-required`.

### `LGW-C80` — SEO strategy needs current provider contracts and user guardrails

- **Locator:** Interlude 1, printed page 41; PDF page 71.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Content strategy should include SEO guidelines, relevant keywords, ongoing monitoring, and updates.
- **Candidate implication:** Version current primary-source requirements for crawl/index controls, rendering, metadata, structured data, quality, duplication, migration, privacy, accessibility, and performance; measure discovery and task outcomes.
- **Boundary:** Keywords, ranking, and traffic do not authorize misleading framing, duplicate pages, surveillance, or optimization against user needs. The 2012 guidance is not a current technical contract.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-search-strategy-lifecycle; current-provider-and-user-outcome-validation-required`.

### `LGW-C81` — professional background is not content-strategy competence

- **Locator:** Interlude 1, printed page 41; PDF page 71.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Copywriters, information architects, journalists, marketers, technical communicators, UX specialists, designers, and others can expand into content strategy.
- **Candidate implication:** Define competencies, evidence, scope, authority, supervision, continuing learning, and collaboration needs independently of title or origin profession.
- **Boundary:** Adjacent experience does not establish research, modeling, accessibility, localization, governance, legal/domain, analytics, or operational competence.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-title-agnostic-entry; competency-and-scope-evidence-required`.

### `LGW-C82` — the seven steps are an iterative review projection, not a universal lifecycle

- **Locator:** Interlude 1, printed pages 41–42; PDF pages 71–72.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Inventory, messaging/media/tone, style guidance, content-led design, audit/action, testing, and future planning can overlap and apply at multiple scopes.
- **Candidate implication:** Preserve the steps as a human-facing view over discovery, modeling, governance, delivery, evaluation, and maintenance with explicit dependencies and risk gates.
- **Boundary:** The method omits or compresses research ethics, requirements, source authority, structured modeling, localization, accessibility implementation, privacy, security, release, incidents, archival retention, and migration.
- **Promotion status:** `candidate-seven-step-review-view; nonexhaustive-and-nonexecuting`.

### `LGW-C83` — a URL/page inventory is only one projection of the content system

- **Locator:** Interlude 1, printed page 42; PDF page 72.
- **Documented practitioner position:** A spreadsheet may track URL, title, description, inbound/outbound links, creation/update dates, and owner.
- **Candidate implication:** Add semantic ID, content type, proposition, source, component/state, structured fields, locale, channel, audience/permissions, dependencies, variants, version, status, rights, sensitivity, retention, accessibility, and runtime references.
- **Boundary:** URLs and pages miss content reused through components, APIs, search indexes, notifications, apps, documents, personalization, and generated views. A spreadsheet snapshot is not canonical runtime state.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-inventory-starting-view; structured-cross-channel-runtime-lineage-required`.

### `LGW-C84` — link graphs are dependencies, not merely spreadsheet columns

- **Locator:** Interlude 1, printed page 42; PDF page 72.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Inventories should record pages linking to and from each URL.
- **Candidate implication:** Model inbound/outbound navigation, embeds, canonical references, redirects, API consumers, search, citations, campaigns, localization, permissions, and external dependencies with validation and impact analysis.
- **Boundary:** A crawler cannot see every dynamic, authenticated, personalized, client-rendered, offline, or external dependency. Link presence does not prove relevance, safety, or accessibility.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-dependency-graph-candidate; incomplete-crawl-and-impact-boundary`.

### `LGW-C85` — ownership must distinguish source, decision, operation, and accountability

- **Locator:** Interlude 1, printed page 42; PDF page 72.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Inventory and audit columns include current owner, change responsibility, writer, editor, due date, and workflow status.
- **Candidate implication:** Separate source-of-truth owner, subject-matter authority, content steward, author/editor, approver, publisher, operator, incident responder, archivist, and accountable decision owner.
- **Boundary:** Department names and nominal owners can hide vacancies, conflicts, access limits, or nonexistent review capacity. Ownership does not confer unilateral deletion or factual authority.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-typed-ownership; availability-authority-and-escalation-required`.

### `LGW-C86` — audit dispositions require evidence and reversible governance

- **Locator:** Interlude 1, printed pages 42–44; PDF pages 72–74.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Audits assign delete, move, combine, separate, or edit fates, prioritize them, record comments and owners, and track work to publication.
- **Candidate implication:** Store proposed disposition, rationale, evidence, affected actors, dependencies, risk, rights/retention, redirect/archive plan, approvers, implementation, verification, rollback, and final state separately.
- **Boundary:** An auditor's recommendation is not authority. Deletion, consolidation, splitting, and movement can break legal records, citations, search, bookmarks, permissions, translations, APIs, or task continuity.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-audit-disposition-workflow; reversible-approved-impact-review-required`.

### `LGW-C87` — priority must include consequence, not only high/middle/low importance

- **Locator:** Interlude 1, printed page 42; PDF page 72.
- **Documented practitioner position:** High, middle, or low importance helps sequence work on audit fates.
- **Candidate implication:** Prioritize by user/service impact, severity, likelihood, rights, safety, accessibility, privacy, security, legal/domain obligation, traffic, dependency, effort, reversibility, confidence, and remediation urgency.
- **Boundary:** A single ordinal importance score hides incomparable concerns and can let popularity outrank rare severe harm.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-coarse-priority-boundary; multidimensional-risk-and-obligation-model-required`.

### `LGW-C88` — workflow state must extend beyond “published”

- **Locator:** Interlude 1, printed page 42; PDF page 72.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Audit work may move through not started, in process, review, and published states.
- **Candidate implication:** Include proposed, researched, drafted, domain-reviewed, accessibility/localization/privacy/security/legal-reviewed as applicable, approved, staged, released, verified, monitored, corrected, deprecated, archived, and removed.
- **Boundary:** Publication is not completion or proof of runtime parity. Asynchronous channels, caches, localization, rollback, incidents, and stale derivatives require post-release state.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-lifecycle-state-expansion; no-published-as-terminal-success`.

### `LGW-C89` — an organic style guide is a maintained decision system

- **Locator:** Interlude 1, printed page 43; PDF page 73.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Teams can start with current decisions and grow a style guide in response to contributor and editor questions.
- **Candidate implication:** Version each rule, rationale, source, scope, examples, exceptions, locale, accessibility, conflicts, owner, approval, effective date, review, deprecation, and implementation mapping.
- **Boundary:** Organic growth can accumulate contradictions and local conventions. Contributor demand does not validate a rule, and a prose guide does not enforce semantic or runtime behavior.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-living-guidance-system; structured-lifecycle-and-conflict-governance-required`.
