### `LGW-C90` — real content must participate in design from the beginning

- **Locator:** Interlude 1, printed page 43; PDF page 73.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Workable designs should focus on content and use real content from the earliest stages.
- **Candidate implication:** Prototype representative semantic types, extremes, states, locales, permissions, errors, accessibility needs, dynamic data, and adverse cases alongside layout and behavior.
- **Boundary:** One polished sample can underrepresent length, scripts, uncertainty, failure, privacy, or high-risk variants. Early real content still needs rights and sensitive-data controls.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-content-inclusive-design; representative-state-and-locale-coverage-required`.

### `LGW-C91` — audit quality requires more than goals, messages, organization, questions, and SEO

- **Locator:** Interlude 1, printed pages 43–44; PDF pages 73–74.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Audit decisions examine strategic goals, messages, organization, visitor questions, testing, analytics, SEO, search, and conversion evidence to delete, move, combine, split, edit, or create content.
- **Candidate implication:** Add truth/source, completeness, accessibility, localization, privacy, security, consent, legal/domain authority, structured reuse, performance, rights, freshness, service behavior, incidents, affected-party outcomes, and remedy.
- **Boundary:** Goal fit and discoverability cannot compensate for false, unsafe, inaccessible, coercive, or unauthorized content. Audit criteria have distinct authority and cannot collapse into one score.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-multidimensional-content-audit; noncompensatory-quality-and-risk-controls-required`.

### `LGW-C92` — conversion optimization is not a neutral audit input

- **Locator:** Interlude 1, printed page 44; PDF page 74.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Conversion analysis may help move lookers to bookers, browsers to shoppers, and readers to subscribers.
- **Candidate implication:** Treat conversion as one organization outcome subject to informed choice, equal refusal, material disclosure, privacy, fairness, regret, cancellation, support, accessibility, and harm guardrails.
- **Boundary:** People may be researching, comparing, declining, or unable to act; nonconversion is not failure. Optimization can produce coercive or deceptive content even when metrics improve.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-conversion-optimization-boundary; autonomy-and-downstream-guardrails-required`.

### `LGW-C93` — testing a product does not isolate or fully validate its content strategy

- **Locator:** Interlude 1, printed page 44; PDF page 74.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Usability testing a site is described as testing its content strategy or lack of one.
- **Candidate implication:** Map each research task and observation to strategy proposition, information architecture, content, component behavior, visual design, performance, policy, and service operation with scoped causal claims.
- **Boundary:** A usability session tests a resolved experience under a protocol; it cannot isolate strategy or prove production reliability, governance adoption, long-term maintenance, accessibility conformance, business causality, or rare harm.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-strategy-test-scope-boundary; construct-and-layer-attribution-required`.

### `LGW-C94` — future planning needs triggers, capacity, and continuity

- **Locator:** Interlude 1, printed page 44; PDF page 74.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Goals, messages, audiences, information, and media change, so strategy needs regular review and realistic commitments based on time and people.
- **Candidate implication:** Define review cadence and event triggers, staffing, budget, service levels, succession, training, vendor dependency, backlog, incident capacity, continuity, deprecation, and shutdown plans.
- **Boundary:** A calendar review can miss urgent change, while ambitious cadence can create low-quality or exploitative labor. Capacity limits must change scope or service promises, not be hidden by automation or stale content.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-strategy-lifecycle-capacity; event-trigger-and-continuity-governance-required`.

### `LGW-C95` — Interlude 1 is a governance foundation, not a complete content architecture

- **Locator:** Interlude 1, printed pages 37–44; PDF pages 67–74.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The interlude connects business and audience strategy to content types, channels, roles, systems, inventory, guidance, design, audit, testing, logistics, and maintenance.
- **Candidate implication:** Retain this operational frame while adding semantic models, source authority, structured reuse, variants, permissions, localization, accessibility implementation, privacy, security, rights, retention, releases, observability, incidents, migration, archives, and remedy.
- **Boundary:** Page inventories, messaging, consistency, conversion, and periodic review cannot govern a contemporary content system alone. Current external technical documentation remains unverified because the Developer Index quota was exhausted.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-content-governance-foundation; full-cross-layer-architecture-required`.

### `LGW-C96` — rapid visual judgment is context evidence, not a universal design verdict

- **Locator:** Chapter 3, printed page 45; PDF page 75.
- **Documented practitioner position:** People are said to judge visual appeal within milliseconds, and an adverse first impression may prevent engagement before words are read.
- **Candidate implication:** Evaluate immediate orientation, legitimacy cues, readability, accessibility, perceived purpose, and willingness to proceed separately from later comprehension and task outcomes.
- **Boundary:** One 2006 study and a visual-appeal construct do not prove trustworthiness, usability, or behavior across cultures, disabilities, devices, domains, and necessary services. Attractive deception can win a first impression.
- **Promotion status:** `candidate-first-impression-construct; no-visual-appeal-as-quality-or-trust-proof`.

### `LGW-C97` — content and design are one interaction specification

- **Locator:** Chapter 3, printed pages 45–47; PDF pages 75–77.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Layout, spacing, typography, color, content type, words, media, and interaction must be developed together rather than pouring final copy into a finished design.
- **Candidate implication:** Bind semantic purpose, content, component, behavior, visual treatment, accessibility, locale, data, state, and validation in shared versioned specifications.
- **Boundary:** Collaboration alone does not resolve authority or prove quality; content and design must also align with product behavior, policy, data, engineering, research, operations, and affected-user evidence.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-content-design-integration; cross-layer-versioned-contract-required`.

### `LGW-C98` — role collaboration requires shared vocabulary and bounded expertise

- **Locator:** Chapter 3, printed page 46; PDF page 76.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Designers, content specialists, and hybrid practitioners should understand enough of one another's disciplines to collaborate on color, space, typography, and readability.
- **Candidate implication:** Define shared decision objects, review questions, responsibility, competence, escalation, and specialist signoff while enabling cross-disciplinary contribution.
- **Boundary:** An overview does not qualify a writer to certify visual accessibility or a designer to approve domain meaning. “Wearing both hats” cannot erase independent review for high-risk work.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-cross-disciplinary-literacy; specialist-authority-and-escalation-preserved`.

### `LGW-C99` — content-type inventories must include state and behavior

- **Locator:** Chapter 3, printed pages 46–47; PDF pages 76–77.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Teams should list content types and determine how, when, and where each appears across page types, screens, apps, social channels, media, and interactive elements.
- **Candidate implication:** Model semantic type, component, trigger, state, actor/permission, source, data, behavior, channel, locale, accessibility, persistence, fallback, and lifecycle.
- **Boundary:** Page-type and placement inventories omit runtime variants, personalization, errors, loading, offline states, APIs, and assistive-technology behavior unless explicitly represented.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-content-type-state-model; no-static-page-placement-only-view`.

### `LGW-C100` — responsive content requires semantic priority, not device-specific copies

- **Locator:** Chapter 3, printed page 47; PDF page 77.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Content and presentation should be planned for large and small screens, then coordinated with apps and social media.
- **Candidate implication:** Preserve canonical meaning while adapting hierarchy, disclosure, layout, input, interaction, and media to available space, capabilities, preferences, locale, and task state.
- **Boundary:** “Classic web, tablet, mobile” is a dated device taxonomy. Viewport does not reveal input, posture, bandwidth, disability, urgency, or intent, and separate copies can drift.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-responsive-semantic-priority; capability-based-cross-channel-parity-required`.

### `LGW-C101` — character limits are downstream constraints, not content requirements by themselves

- **Locator:** Chapter 3, printed page 47; PDF page 77.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Catalog descriptions, headlines, headings, same-page links, images, videos, and interactive elements should be planned with available space and expected lengths.
- **Candidate implication:** Test representative, minimum, maximum, empty, localized, right-to-left, error, and dynamic content; store hard technical limits separately from preferred editorial ranges.
- **Boundary:** Designing to one language or average string length causes truncation and semantic loss. A visual slot cannot authorize removal of necessary meaning.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-content-extreme-testing; hard-limit-and-editorial-range-separation-required`.

### `LGW-C102` — real content prototypes must be representative and rights-safe

- **Locator:** Chapter 3, printed pages 47–48; PDF pages 77–78.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Meaningful content should replace lorem ipsum throughout design so content and presentation can be evaluated together.
- **Candidate implication:** Use original synthetic or authorized examples spanning realistic types, lengths, states, locales, sensitivities, permissions, errors, accessibility needs, and edge cases.
- **Boundary:** One “real” happy-path example can create false confidence, and production data may expose secrets or personal information. Placeholder text can still serve narrowly labeled geometry experiments.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-representative-content-prototyping; rights-privacy-and-state-coverage-required`.

### `LGW-C103` — universal usability requires adaptability without claiming universality

- **Locator:** Chapter 3, printed pages 48–50; PDF pages 78–80.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Accessibility and flexibility should be designed from the beginning to meet widely varying needs rather than retrofitted later.
- **Candidate implication:** Support standards-based semantics, user-agent preferences, zoom/reflow, text spacing, contrast, keyboard, switch, speech, touch, captions/transcripts, screen readers, magnification, reduced motion, and personalization.
- **Boundary:** No product meets “everyone's needs” absolutely. Coverage, exceptions, current standards, disabled participation, testing, and remediation must be explicit.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-adaptive-accessibility-from-start; no-universal-access-claim`.

### `LGW-C104` — page-level text-size controls are optional, not the primary accessibility mechanism

- **Locator:** Chapter 3, printed page 48; PDF page 78.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Every site is advised to expose an obvious text-size widget because many people do not know browser controls and the widget signals care and increases trust.
- **Candidate implication:** First respect browser/OS zoom, text size, font, contrast, and accessibility preferences; add in-product controls only when they work consistently, remain discoverable, and provide tested additional value.
- **Boundary:** A page widget can conflict with user settings, persist through cookies, affect only some text, or imply accessibility without reflow. Care and trust cannot be inferred from control presence.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-text-widget-default-boundary; native-preference-and-reflow-first`.

### `LGW-C105` — all content and controls must survive resizing and reflow

- **Locator:** Chapter 3, printed pages 48–49; PDF pages 78–79.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Navigation, side content, dropdowns, forms, and main text should all enlarge and remain usable rather than resizing only the central article.
- **Candidate implication:** Test zoom, text-only scaling, dynamic type, text spacing, orientation, reflow, overlays, fixed regions, form controls, error messages, and focus visibility across complete tasks.
- **Boundary:** Enlarged text is not enough if content clips, overlaps, reorders incorrectly, requires two-dimensional scrolling, or loses functionality. Current thresholds remain externally unverified.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-all-content-resize-and-reflow; complete-task-validation-required`.

### `LGW-C106` — alternate input and output modes need implemented equivalence

- **Locator:** Chapter 3, printed pages 49–50; PDF pages 79–80.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The chapter mentions color customization, keyboard-only use, captions, audible output, skip links, logical tab order, and multimedia access.
- **Candidate implication:** Define equivalent names, roles, states, relationships, order, keyboard/switch/speech/touch operation, captions/transcripts, audio description where needed, announcements, and fallbacks per component.
- **Boundary:** An accessibility page describing support does not prove it, and 2012 Flash/JavaScript examples are historical. Reading order, focus order, and tab order are distinct.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-multimodal-equivalence; runtime-component-and-assistive-technology-testing-required`.

### `LGW-C107` — disability is not a “special need” category

- **Locator:** Chapter 3, printed pages 49–50; PDF pages 79–80.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Examples provide customization help for visitors described as having “special needs” across vision, hearing, motor, and cognitive areas.
- **Candidate implication:** Use disability-respecting language, remove deficit framing, design interoperable defaults, and involve disabled people across research, governance, and testing.
- **Boundary:** Categorizing people as exceptional shifts responsibility away from product barriers and omits intersectional, temporary, situational, and self-defined needs.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-special-needs-framing-rejection; disability-inclusive-social-barrier-model-required`.

### `LGW-C108` — preference persistence is a privacy and state contract

- **Locator:** Chapter 3, printed page 49; PDF page 79.
- **Documented practitioner position:** An accessibility page stores color and text-size preferences in a cookie and links to privacy information.
- **Candidate implication:** Define necessity, scope, consent basis where required, storage location, retention, cross-device behavior, account linkage, reset, expiry, security, and no-tracking use for accessibility preferences.
- **Boundary:** A privacy link alone does not govern data. Accessibility preferences may reveal disability-related information and should not become advertising, profiling, or eligibility signals.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-accessibility-preference-privacy; minimization-purpose-and-reset-required`.

### `LGW-C109` — color must not be the sole carrier of meaning

- **Locator:** Chapter 3, printed pages 50–51; PDF pages 80–81.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Features, functions, and information should remain distinguishable when people cannot perceive intended color differences.
- **Candidate implication:** Pair color with text, shape, pattern, position-independent labels, icon semantics, borders, or other programmatically determinable cues and test all states.
- **Boundary:** Adding one redundant visual cue may still fail nonvisual access, forced colors, low vision, cognitive interpretation, or localization. Meaning must exist in semantics as well as appearance.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-redundant-color-meaning; programmatic-and-multimodal-equivalence-required`.

### `LGW-C110` — color-vision statistics and gender framing are dated evidence

- **Locator:** Chapter 3, printed page 50; PDF page 80.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The chapter cites percentages for males and women with color deficiency and uses a deuteranopia simulation.
- **Candidate implication:** Avoid designing from demographic prevalence; test color distinctions against current measurable requirements, simulations as preliminary tools, and people with varied color vision.
- **Boundary:** Binary gender wording, historical percentages, and simulations do not define individual perception or coverage. Low prevalence cannot justify exclusion.
- **Promotion status:** `historical-color-vision-statistic; no-gender-proxy-or-simulation-as-validation`.

### `LGW-C111` — cultural color meaning requires contextual research without determinism

- **Locator:** Chapter 3, printed page 51; PDF page 81.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Color connotations and political associations vary across countries and cultures, so teams should avoid recycled factoids and test with intended populations.
- **Candidate implication:** Research color meaning by locale, community, domain, current event, context, task, and actual combination; preserve uncertainty and accessible alternatives.
- **Boundary:** Culture and country do not predict individual interpretation, and participant preference cannot override contrast, safety, or established domain conventions without review.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-contextual-color-research; no-cultural-determinism-or-factoid-rule`.

### `LGW-C112` — brand palettes are subordinate to readability and state meaning

- **Locator:** Chapter 3, printed page 52; PDF page 82.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Teams are advised to work within established brand colors unless serious problems exist.
- **Candidate implication:** Validate brand tokens for contrast, forced colors, themes, state distinctions, charts, focus, links, errors, and disabled states; create accessible alternatives and escalate failures.
- **Boundary:** Brand authority cannot define what counts as a serious accessibility problem. Visual consistency never compensates for unreadable or ambiguous content.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-brand-color-subordination; accessible-token-and-escalation-required`.

### `LGW-C113` — light/dark polarity is a user-context choice, not a universal reading rule

- **Locator:** Chapter 3, printed page 52; PDF page 82.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Dark-on-light is presented as best for sustained reading, with bold light-on-dark reserved for short interface text.
- **Candidate implication:** Support tested light, dark, high-contrast, forced-color, and user-preferred themes with correct tokens and media behavior.
- **Boundary:** Polarity needs vary with low vision, photophobia, environment, display, font, size, and personal preference. Bold text and short duration do not guarantee readability.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-color-polarity-universal-boundary; user-preference-and-theme-testing-required`.

### `LGW-C114` — backgrounds must preserve foreground legibility across states

- **Locator:** Chapter 3, printed pages 52–53; PDF pages 82–83.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Patterned, vivid, or competing backgrounds can obscure words and illustrations and should not defeat reading.
- **Candidate implication:** Test foreground/background contrast and interference across imagery, gradients, overlays, video, themes, responsive crops, loading, failure, focus, selection, and user styles.
- **Boundary:** “Clear” is not a measurable contract, and removing texture alone does not ensure access. Text over media often needs adaptive treatment or separation.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-foreground-background-separation; all-state-measured-validation-required`.

### `LGW-C115` — contrast is measurable and state-specific

- **Locator:** Chapter 3, printed page 53; PDF page 83.
- **Documented practitioner position:** High contrast between text and background is required; dark-on-dark, light-on-light, and some saturated combinations are shown as difficult.
- **Candidate implication:** Store foreground, background, text size/weight, component role, interaction state, theme, forced-color behavior, and current conformance target; test manual and automated results.
- **Boundary:** “High” and “vibrating” are not sufficient thresholds, and screenshots cannot validate computed or rendered contrast. Current standards remain externally unverified in this slice.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-measured-contrast-contract; current-standard-and-rendered-state-validation-required`.

### `LGW-C116` — mobile-first space pressure must not become semantic deletion

- **Locator:** Chapter 3, printed pages 53–54; PDF pages 83–84.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Designing for small screens exposes limited space and encourages teams to remove words rather than squeeze desktop content into a dense mobile layout.
- **Candidate implication:** Prioritize propositions, tasks, actions, and progressive detail across viewports while preserving material facts, accessibility, recoverability, and canonical parity.
- **Boundary:** Small screens do not reduce legal, safety, consent, or decision needs. “Mobile first” is not “mobile only,” and visual density can be reduced through behavior and hierarchy rather than deleting meaning.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-small-space-semantic-priority; no-viewport-based-material-omission`.

### `LGW-C117` — pattern consistency supports recognition but does not create trustworthiness

- **Locator:** Chapter 3, printed pages 54–55; PDF pages 84–85.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Repeated positions and styles help people form mental models, find content faster, and feel confidence and trust.
- **Candidate implication:** Maintain predictable semantic component patterns while allowing evidence-backed variation by state, role, locale, accessibility, and task.
- **Boundary:** Consistent deceptive patterns remain harmful, and responsive or personalized layouts may move elements. Familiarity and confidence do not prove truth, safety, or successful outcome.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-recognition-patterns; no-consistency-to-trust-inference`.

### `LGW-C118` — grids are responsive relationships, not fixed coordinates

- **Locator:** Chapter 3, printed pages 55–56; PDF pages 85–86.
- **Documented practitioner position:** A small set of aligned starting positions and clear grids reduce clutter and support varied content templates.
- **Candidate implication:** Define alignment, grouping, reading order, breakpoints, reflow, density, zoom, localization, and component constraints as responsive relationships.
- **Boundary:** Visual alignment can diverge from semantic and focus order; rigid grids fail long translations, magnification, vertical scripts, personalization, and dynamic content.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-responsive-grid-relationships; semantic-order-and-content-extreme-testing-required`.

### `LGW-C119` — active space is a grouping and comprehension signal

- **Locator:** Chapter 3, printed pages 56–57; PDF pages 86–87.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Space within content helps distinguish paragraphs, list items, headings, images, and chunks, while excessive or misplaced space can imply false completion.
- **Candidate implication:** Model proximity, grouping, rhythm, density, hierarchy, target separation, and reading sequence together with programmatic structure and responsive reflow.
- **Boundary:** More space is not automatically clearer and can increase scrolling, separation, or magnification burden. Visual grouping cannot replace headings, lists, regions, and relationships in semantics.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-space-as-relationship; semantic-grouping-and-responsive-burden-validation-required`.

### `LGW-C120` — safety information needs hazard structure, not only more whitespace

- **Locator:** Chapter 3, printed page 57; PDF page 87.
- **Documented practitioner position:** A dense pet-safety list is criticized for lacking space, ordering, headings, and a task-oriented title.
- **Candidate implication:** Organize hazards by source, severity, urgency, prevention, exposure signs, immediate action, prohibited action, emergency contact, and evidence; make the structure accessible and printable.
- **Boundary:** Layout cannot validate veterinary accuracy or triage. Search-oriented headings and short chunks must not fragment dosage, timing, exception, or emergency meaning.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-safety-content-structure; domain-authority-and-emergency-action-required`.

### `LGW-C121` — cross-viewport reduction requires canonical parity and change governance

- **Locator:** Chapter 3, printed page 57; PDF page 87.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Mobile content should keep only what is needed and use short pieces, lists, visuals, and active space.
- **Candidate implication:** Define core versus supplementary propositions, ensure equivalent access to full detail, track divergence, and test tasks across viewport, orientation, input, locale, offline state, and assistive technology.
- **Boundary:** Teams may label organization-inconvenient facts “unneeded.” A link to desktop content is not equivalent if inaccessible, costly, or context-breaking.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-mobile-pruning-boundary; canonical-proposition-and-equivalent-detail-required`.

### `LGW-C122` — perceived page endings require content continuity cues

- **Locator:** Chapter 3, printed pages 57–58; PDF pages 87–88.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Horizontal rules or large blank blocks can create “false bottoms” that cause people to stop despite a scrollbar; teams should test across screens and browsers.
- **Candidate implication:** Preserve visible continuation, avoid viewport-height traps, and test lazy loading, carousels, accordions, sticky regions, overlays, infinite scroll, pagination, zoom, and orientation.
- **Boundary:** Scrollbars and device/resolution matrices have changed since 2012. Removing separators universally can harm grouping; the issue is misleading completion, not the mere presence of lines or space.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-content-continuity-cues; no-universal-separator-ban`.

### `LGW-C123` — heading proximity must align with semantic hierarchy

- **Locator:** Chapter 3, printed pages 58–59; PDF pages 88–89.
- **Documented practitioner position:** More space above than below a heading visually groups it with following content; headings should not look like links or float ambiguously.
- **Candidate implication:** Align visual proximity, heading level, accessible name, region/list/table structure, link styling, reading order, and responsive behavior.
- **Boundary:** CSS spacing alone does not create a heading or fix hierarchy. A heading may introduce multiple components, and scripts or layouts can use different spatial conventions.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-visual-semantic-heading-alignment; no-CSS-only-structure`.

### `LGW-C124` — repeated “learn more” links need destination-specific purpose

- **Locator:** Chapter 3, printed pages 58–59; PDF pages 88–89.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Repeated generic links are criticized for screen-reader navigation, and revised links include destination context.
- **Candidate implication:** Make link purpose distinguishable in its accessible context, align visible and accessible names, and preserve concise patterned presentation where appropriate.
- **Boundary:** Appending words mechanically can create verbosity or speech-input mismatch. Card semantics, programmatic context, localization, and repeated destination handling require component testing.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-link-purpose; accessible-context-and-speech-alignment-required`.

### `LGW-C125` — alignment guidance must account for writing system and content genre

- **Locator:** Chapter 3, printed pages 59–60; PDF pages 89–90.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Centered prose is said to lack a stable line-start anchor and left-aligned ragged-right text is recommended for all web writing, with exceptions such as invitations or poetry.
- **Candidate implication:** Choose alignment by writing direction, script, genre, line length, layout, locale convention, table role, and reading task; test legibility and reflow.
- **Boundary:** Left alignment is not universal for right-to-left, vertical, bidirectional, centered-display, or culturally specific content. “All web writing” overstates an English/Roman-script convention.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-left-alignment-universal-boundary; script-genre-and-locale-aware-layout-required`.

### `LGW-C126` — typography guidance is explicitly writing-system scoped

- **Locator:** Chapter 3, printed pages 60–62; PDF pages 90–92.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The font primer distinguishes serif and sans serif within the Roman alphabet and acknowledges that other writing systems require different discussion.
- **Candidate implication:** Specify font coverage, shaping, fallback, script, language, punctuation, numerals, weight, style, variable axes, rendering, and locale review for every supported writing system.
- **Boundary:** Latin-centric anatomy, family categories, and familiarity claims cannot be generalized to Arabic, CJK, Indic, African, Indigenous, or other scripts.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-writing-system-scope-boundary; multilingual-font-engineering-required`.

### `LGW-C127` — sans serif is not a universal legibility winner

- **Locator:** Chapter 3, printed pages 61–63; PDF pages 91–93.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Despite acknowledging inconsistent reading-speed and comprehension findings, the chapter recommends familiar sans serif fonts based on preference, convention, low-vision advice, and perceived modernity.
- **Candidate implication:** Select typefaces through character differentiation, spacing, rendering, script coverage, weight, size, user override, task, population, and tested outcomes rather than serif category alone.
- **Boundary:** Preference and familiarity are not comprehension, while “old-fashioned” is aesthetic judgment. Disability needs vary and current evidence must be verified.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-sans-serif-default-boundary; feature-and-outcome-based-font-selection-required`.

### `LGW-C128` — user font preferences and fallback behavior are part of the content contract

- **Locator:** Chapter 3, printed pages 62–63; PDF pages 92–93.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Browser settings may override site fonts, and unavailable unusual fonts fall back to what a computer has.
- **Candidate implication:** Test user styles, fallback stacks, webfont loading/failure, privacy, performance, glyph coverage, metrics, layout shift, zoom, forced colors, and dyslexia/low-vision preferences.
- **Boundary:** Modern font delivery differs from 2012 assumptions, but author control remains incomplete. A design that works only with one font is fragile.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-font-fallback-and-user-control; loading-failure-and-layout-resilience-required`.

### `LGW-C129` — default text size should follow tested access needs, not age proxies

- **Locator:** Chapter 3, printed page 63; PDF page 93.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Teams should write less rather than shrink type and consider that creators may be younger or have better vision than visitors.
- **Candidate implication:** Use accessible scalable tokens, user preferences, zoom/reflow, device distance, density, font metrics, locale, and disabled-user testing; never trade necessary content for smaller text.
- **Boundary:** Age does not predict vision, and “cater to them” is othering. Larger text alone does not resolve contrast, spacing, hierarchy, magnification, or cognitive access.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-legible-scalable-default; no-age-proxy-or-word-count-tradeoff`.

### `LGW-C130` — line length is a contextual range, not a fixed universal number

- **Locator:** Chapter 3, printed pages 63–64; PDF pages 93–94.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Very long lines may strain return sweeps, very short lines may break phrases, and 50–70 characters or 8–10 words is offered as a medium default for web and tablet.
- **Candidate implication:** Test measure with font, size, spacing, script, language, viewport, zoom, columns, content genre, reading mode, and user preference; allow fluid reflow.
- **Boundary:** Character and word counts vary dramatically across scripts and typography; cited student studies and paper research do not establish a universal range or comprehension optimum.
- **Promotion status:** `candidate-medium-measure-range; script-context-and-user-testing-required`.

### `LGW-C131` — all-caps guidance should preserve semantics and avoid unsupported metrics

- **Locator:** Chapter 3, printed pages 64–65; PDF pages 94–95.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Extended capitals are described as slower, larger, boring, ignored, and perceived as shouting; mixed case, bold, color, or size is recommended for hierarchy.
- **Candidate implication:** Avoid long all-caps prose, preserve source casing where semantically required, and use semantic headings/emphasis with accessible visual treatment.
- **Boundary:** The chapter's 30% space and 15% speed figures are unsourced here and not universal. Acronyms, identifiers, scripts without case, legal marks, and user-entered text require exceptions; bold/color alone may not convey hierarchy.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-extended-all-caps-caution; numeric-and-cross-script-boundary`.

### `LGW-C132` — underline and italics are contextual conventions, not absolute semantics

- **Locator:** Chapter 3, printed page 65; PDF page 95.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Underlining is reserved for links, while italics are limited to titles or light emphasis and discouraged for paragraphs and headings.
- **Candidate implication:** Keep link affordance perceivable beyond color, use semantic emphasis/citation elements, support user styles, and test hierarchy, contrast, font fallback, locale, and accessibility.
- **Boundary:** Not all links must be underlined in every context, and nonlinks may be conventionally underlined in annotations or forms. Italic availability and meaning vary by script and language.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-affordance-and-emphasis-semantics; contextual-style-and-script-rules-required`.

### `LGW-C133` — incremental visual redesign does not validate comprehension or truth

- **Locator:** Chapter 3, Case Study 3-1, printed pages 66–70; PDF pages 96–100.
- **Documented practitioner position:** A crane article is successively changed through contrast, alignment, paragraphing, font, headings, lists, reorganization, shortening, and an image to become more inviting and easier to use.
- **Candidate implication:** Treat each transformation as a testable variant with proposition diff, factual/source review, hierarchy, image purpose/alternative, responsive behavior, accessibility, locale, and task-based evaluation.
- **Boundary:** The case offers no participant results, source validation, rights/alt-text detail, or preservation audit; it also contains apparent scan/typographic errors. Visual improvement cannot prove comprehension, factual equivalence, or accessibility.
- **Promotion status:** `candidate-incremental-redesign-method; semantic-diff-and-user-validation-required`.

### `LGW-C134` — Chapter 3 is a useful design-content primer, not a current component standard

- **Locator:** Chapter 3, printed pages 45–71; PDF pages 75–101.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The chapter connects early collaboration and real content to adaptable access, color, space, typography, responsive presentation, and an incremental redesign.
- **Candidate implication:** Retain the integration, representative-content, resizing, redundant meaning, grouping, continuity, legibility, and evaluation principles while mapping them to semantic components and current multimodal contracts.
- **Boundary:** Many prescriptions are visual, English/Roman-script, device-era, demographic, or heuristic. Current external technical documentation remains unverified because the Developer Index quota was exhausted.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-content-design-foundation; current-semantic-responsive-accessibility-standard-required`.

### `LGW-C135` — the home page is one entry state, not the universal beginning

- **Locator:** Chapter 4, printed page 73; PDF page 103.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The chapter acknowledges search, links, blogs, and multiple routes but focuses on home pages as the opening for information- and task-rich sites across screen sizes.
- **Candidate implication:** Model entry source, route, referrer, campaign, notification, deep link, shared link, bookmark, search result, app intent, authentication state, locale, permission, and recovery for every landing surface.
- **Boundary:** Many journeys begin on destination pages, modal routes, app screens, messages, or restored state and never visit a home page. “Starting well” is a route-level contract, not one page template.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-multi-entry-journey; no-home-page-as-universal-start`.

### `LGW-C136` — the six home-page functions are contextual review questions

- **Locator:** Chapter 4, printed page 74; PDF page 104.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Home pages should support discoverability, identity, tone/trust, scope orientation, immediate task start, and onward navigation with few words.
- **Candidate implication:** Evaluate discovery, authentic identity, purpose/scope, current state, primary actions, search/navigation, access, help, privacy, and recovery according to product and entry context.
- **Boundary:** Not every private, authenticated, single-purpose, campaign, app, or necessary-service entry needs all six functions, and shortness cannot omit material information.
- **Promotion status:** `candidate-entry-page-review-view; contextual-and-nonexhaustive`.

### `LGW-C137` — 2012 SEO rules and traffic figures are not current contracts

- **Locator:** Chapter 4, printed pages 74–75; PDF pages 104–105.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Keywords in titles, URLs, headings, and copy, inbound links, and high organic rank are presented as primary findability mechanisms; a 2009 top-result traffic share is cited.
- **Candidate implication:** Verify current primary provider documentation for crawl/index controls, rendering, metadata, structured data, duplication, quality, performance, accessibility, migration, and measurement.
- **Boundary:** Keyword presence and inbound links do not guarantee rank; provider algorithms, result formats, privacy, personalization, AI answers, and zero-click behavior change. The traffic percentage cannot be generalized.
- **Promotion status:** `historical-search-guidance; current-provider-and-query-outcome-evidence-required`.

### `LGW-C138` — search vocabulary alignment must preserve canonical meaning

- **Locator:** Chapter 4, printed pages 74–75; PDF pages 104–105.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Site terminology should match searchers' words so pages can be discovered, while repeated keyword stuffing is rejected.
- **Candidate implication:** Map queries, synonyms, aliases, misspellings, entities, domain terms, locale variants, and user language to canonical concepts without distorting propositions.
- **Boundary:** Query volume does not establish preferred, respectful, accurate, or safe terminology. Matching harmful or false-premise searches may require explanation rather than repetition.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-query-to-concept-mapping; no-keyword-as-canonical-language`.

### `LGW-C139` — “black hat” and “white hat” language should not become taxonomy

- **Locator:** Chapter 4, printed pages 74–75; PDF pages 104–105.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Deceptive keyword techniques and accepted SEO practices are labeled “black hat” and “white hat.”
- **Candidate implication:** Use descriptive categories such as deceptive manipulation, policy violation, permitted optimization, and user-serving discoverability.
- **Boundary:** Color-coded moral terminology can reproduce racialized associations and obscures which behavior, provider rule, user harm, or enforcement status is at issue.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-color-morality-taxonomy-rejection; behavior-specific-language-required`.

### `LGW-C140` — “remarkable content” does not guarantee discovery or quality

- **Locator:** Chapter 4, printed page 75; PDF page 105.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Content worthy of comments and links is presented as a durable route to strong search performance.
- **Candidate implication:** Separate content usefulness and quality from crawlability, indexing, query match, authority signals, distribution, competition, accessibility, and user outcomes.
- **Boundary:** Sensational, polarizing, false, or harmful content may attract remarks and links, while high-quality niche or private information may not. Popularity is not truth or benefit.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-remarkability-boundary; no-popularity-as-quality-or-discovery-proof`.

### `LGW-C141` — entry identity requires authentic provenance, not branding alone

- **Locator:** Chapter 4, printed page 75; PDF page 105.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Logo, name, and tagline should quickly confirm whose site this is and whether the visitor arrived where expected.
- **Candidate implication:** Expose service/operator identity, authoritative domain or app provenance, environment, account/tenant, locale, security context, current route, and support/reporting paths where relevant.
- **Boundary:** Logos and brand styling are easily spoofed and can mislead in phishing, embedded, white-label, reseller, government, or federated contexts. Identity is not equivalent to brand loyalty.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-authentic-entry-identity; provenance-and-security-context-required`.

### `LGW-C142` — taglines are compressed claims requiring evidence and scope

- **Locator:** Chapter 4, printed page 75; PDF page 105.
- **Documented practitioner position:** A short memorable tagline is preferred to mission paragraphs for encapsulating the organization's message and helping people understand what the site offers.
- **Candidate implication:** Treat taglines as proposition variants with source, substantiation, scope, qualifiers, locale, audience, approval, effective date, and link to fuller purpose.
- **Boundary:** Memorability can amplify vague, comparative, or misleading claims; a short phrase cannot replace eligibility, limitations, material terms, or public-service mandate.
- **Promotion status:** `candidate-compressed-identity-proposition; substantiation-and-layered-detail-required`.

### `LGW-C143` — tone and visual personality do not establish credibility or trustworthiness

- **Locator:** Chapter 4, printed pages 76–77; PDF pages 106–107.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Visual style and content personality are said to create trust, credibility, and confidence, illustrated by contrasting historical IRS designs.
- **Candidate implication:** Separate expression fit and perceived credibility from verified authority, accuracy, security, privacy, competence, service performance, and remedy.
- **Boundary:** Official or friendly appearance can create unsafe reliance, and necessary services do not earn legitimacy through style. Historical screenshots cannot establish causal trust effects.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-personality-trust-boundary; verified-trustworthiness-signals-required`.

### `LGW-C144` — voice should not “never break character” when truth or risk requires recession

- **Locator:** Chapter 4, printed page 77; PDF page 107.
- **Documented practitioner position:** A cohesive brand is encouraged to maintain character across content, visuals, affordances, features, and communication goals.
- **Candidate implication:** Preserve truthful identity while allowing brand expression to recede under error, urgency, safety, legal/domain precision, accessibility, consent, and crisis.
- **Boundary:** Unbroken character can make grave, uncertain, or harmful situations seem performative and can subordinate semantic state to brand consistency.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-never-break-character-rejection; risk-based-brand-recession-required`.

### `LGW-C145` — voice governance needs evidence, participation, and decision authority

- **Locator:** Chapter 4, printed page 78; PDF page 108.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Owners may choose personality, while larger organizations coordinate with marketing, communications, web services, and strategy groups rather than changing brand arbitrarily.
- **Candidate implication:** Record voice scope, research, propositions, constraints, affected-user evidence, specialist reviews, conflicts, decision owner, approval, exceptions, and lifecycle.
- **Boundary:** Organizational effort, hierarchy, or brand ownership does not validate a choice. “The whole organization's face” can erase distinct services, mandates, locales, and power relationships.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-voice-decision-governance; no-hierarchy-or-investment-as-validation`.

### `LGW-C146` — entry scope needs a concise information architecture projection

- **Locator:** Chapter 4, printed pages 78–79; PDF pages 108–109.
- **Documented practitioner position:** A useful home page makes site purpose obvious through links, short descriptions, and verb-led calls to action without saying too much or too little.
- **Candidate implication:** Project canonical services, topics, status, primary tasks, audiences where necessary, help, and recent/urgent conditions through tested hierarchy and information scent.
- **Boundary:** “Instantly clear” and the right amount are audience/task claims; links and short descriptions cannot repair incoherent scope, overlapping services, hidden eligibility, or unstable taxonomy.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-entry-scope-projection; information-architecture-and-comprehension-testing-required`.

### `LGW-C147` — card sorting is one taxonomy input, not information architecture truth

- **Locator:** Chapter 4, printed page 78; PDF page 108.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Collaboration with information architects and participant card sorting are suggested for grouping content.
- **Candidate implication:** Combine sorting with tasks, search, navigation tests, domain models, permissions, lifecycle, analytics, support evidence, accessibility, and known-item finding.
- **Boundary:** Card labels, sample, method, context, participant knowledge, and facilitation shape results; agreement does not prove findability, domain correctness, or safe grouping.
- **Promotion status:** `candidate-card-sort-input; triangulated-architecture-validation-required`.

### `LGW-C148` — dialect and colloquiality require audience, locale, and power review

- **Locator:** Chapter 4, printed page 79; PDF page 109.
- **Documented practitioner position:** “Wanna talk?” is praised for conveying a local folksy personality consistently across a provider's site.
- **Candidate implication:** Treat colloquial spelling, dialect, slang, and informality as locale- and community-scoped expression requiring authenticity, comprehension, accessibility, translation, and stereotype review.
- **Boundary:** Performed dialect can caricature class, region, race, or community and may reduce clarity or seriousness. Organization voice should not appropriate speech merely to appear relatable.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-performed-dialect-boundary; authenticity-and-stereotype-review-required`.

### `LGW-C149` — primary-task prioritization must preserve minority and high-consequence access

- **Locator:** Chapter 4, printed pages 79–82; PDF pages 109–112.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Home pages should emphasize primary personas and tasks rather than giving everything equal weight, illustrated by Tricare's shift from news and regions to plan/member questions.
- **Candidate implication:** Prioritize using task frequency, consequence, urgency, rights, vulnerability, accessibility, service duty, seasonality, and recovery while retaining paths for secondary and exceptional needs.
- **Boundary:** Primary personas can erase rare, overseas, nonstandard, disabled, emergency, provider, staff, or oversight journeys. Equal visual weight is not required, but equivalent service access may be.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-entry-task-priority; minority-and-high-consequence-path-preservation-required`.

### `LGW-C150` — guided personalization at entry requires necessity and privacy controls

- **Locator:** Chapter 4, Case Study 4-1, printed pages 80–82; PDF pages 110–112.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Tricare asks role, location/ZIP, and plan to provide tailored health-care information and route new versus existing participants.
- **Candidate implication:** Justify each question, minimize precision, explain purpose, protect health/military/location data, support refusal or generic paths, preserve corrections, and enforce permissions and retention.
- **Boundary:** Three “simple” questions can reveal sensitive identity and eligibility and may exclude people who do not know their plan or fit listed roles. Copy cannot make unnecessary collection safe.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-entry-personalization-privacy; minimization-alternative-and-security-required`.

### `LGW-C151` — historical satisfaction gains do not prove redesign causality

- **Locator:** Chapter 4, Case Study 4-1, printed page 81; PDF page 111.
- **Documented practitioner position:** A 20-point satisfaction increase is reported after the redesigned Tricare site launched.
- **Candidate implication:** Require measure definition, instrument, baseline, sampling, timing, comparison, confounds, subgroup outcomes, task measures, accessibility, and production changes before attribution.
- **Boundary:** A before/after association cannot isolate home-page content, personas, forms, visual design, broader product changes, service changes, or population shifts.
- **Promotion status:** `historical-outcome-claim; no-redesign-causality-without-study-evidence`.

### `LGW-C152` — starting a major task at entry can reduce navigation cost

- **Locator:** Chapter 4, printed pages 82–83; PDF pages 112–113.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Common forms such as travel booking can begin on the home page rather than after several navigational steps.
- **Candidate implication:** Offer direct task initiation when prerequisites, identity, scope, consequences, and alternatives are clear; preserve draft state and enable later completion or route changes.
- **Boundary:** Fewer clicks are not inherently easier, and an entry form can overwhelm, exclude, or prematurely commit. Task complexity and risk may require orientation first.
- **Promotion status:** `candidate-direct-task-entry; prerequisite-risk-and-recovery-review-required`.

### `LGW-C153` — “high on the page” is a responsive salience requirement, not a fold coordinate

- **Locator:** Chapter 4, printed page 83; PDF page 113.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Important forms should be immediately findable and not pushed below the initial viewport by large images; the author notes variation across screens and scrolling behavior.
- **Candidate implication:** Give primary actions clear hierarchy and early discoverability across viewport, zoom, reflow, orientation, locale, authentication, personalization, and assistive navigation.
- **Boundary:** There is no stable fold, and source order, landmarks, headings, search, focus, or command access may matter more than pixel position. Salience must not crowd out required context.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-responsive-primary-action-salience; no-fixed-fold-rule`.

### `LGW-C154` — unnecessary early forms are a data-minimization and consent failure

- **Locator:** Chapter 4, printed pages 83–84; PDF pages 113–114.
- **Documented practitioner position:** People should not be forced to provide unwanted, premature, excessive, or difficult information before receiving the value they came for.
- **Candidate implication:** Remove, delay, make optional, or provide alternatives for fields lacking necessity; explain purpose and consequences, separate marketing consent, and support anonymous/guest access where feasible.
- **Boundary:** The chapter frames the tradeoff partly as abandonment and bad data; rights, safety, equity, and informed consent apply even when conversion does not decline.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-progressive-data-minimization; no-conversion-only-justification`.

### `LGW-C155` — linked promises must be fulfilled before registration or promotion

- **Locator:** Chapter 4, printed pages 83–84; PDF pages 113–114.
- **Documented practitioner position:** A social link should not promise content and then interpose a registration form so the organization can market later.
- **Candidate implication:** Preserve destination/action parity, disclose gates before activation, deliver promised value, and request separately consented follow-up only after an equal decline path.
- **Boundary:** Referral traffic does not authorize identity collection, contact, tracking, or friend-network exploitation. A technically skippable gate can still be coercive or inaccessible.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-link-promise-and-gate-parity; separate-consent-and-equal-decline-required`.

### `LGW-C156` — fake data is a diagnostic signal, not user misconduct

- **Locator:** Chapter 4, printed page 84; PDF page 114.
- **Documented practitioner position:** People may enter false information to pass an unwanted form, producing costly bad data.
- **Candidate implication:** Treat implausible, duplicate, abandoned, or low-quality input as evidence to review necessity, trust, clarity, validation, alternatives, incentives, and coercion before tightening controls.
- **Boundary:** Aggressive verification can increase exclusion, surveillance, and harm. Pseudonymous or protective responses may be legitimate, especially in sensitive contexts.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-bad-data-as-form-diagnostic; no-punitive-verification-default`.

### `LGW-C157` — leaving the site or declining to advance is not necessarily failure

- **Locator:** Chapter 4, printed page 84; PDF page 114.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Home pages should help people move forward quickly rather than leave the site.
- **Candidate implication:** Distinguish successful answer-and-exit, external handoff, comparison, refusal, browser back, task completion, unresolved exit, and abandonment through outcome evidence.
- **Boundary:** Preventing exit is a dark pattern; people retain the right to leave, verify elsewhere, decline, or use another channel. Retention is not task success.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-site-exit-boundary; no-retention-as-success-proxy`.

### `LGW-C158` — navigation is broader than search or clicking a link

- **Locator:** Chapter 4, printed page 84; PDF page 114.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The chapter says sites offer two ways to move: search or choose a link.
- **Candidate implication:** Model links, buttons, tabs, menus, breadcrumbs, browser history, deep links, commands, gestures, keyboard/screen-reader landmarks, in-page navigation, filters, app routing, and external handoffs by semantic purpose.
- **Boundary:** The binary reflects a page-centric 2012 model and conflates navigation with controls. Correct role, state, focus, history, URL, and recovery behavior matter.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-two-way-navigation-correction; semantic-routing-and-control-taxonomy-required`.

### `LGW-C159` — search needs a complete retrieval and accessibility contract

- **Locator:** Chapter 4, printed pages 84–85; PDF pages 114–115.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Large sites should expose a consistently placed search box near the top rather than hide it at the bottom.
- **Candidate implication:** Specify search scope, label, query handling, suggestions, submission, results, ranking, filters, permissions, zero results, privacy, keyboard/speech operation, landmarks, focus, and responsive placement.
- **Boundary:** Search presence does not guarantee usefulness, and “any large site” may not need local search if scope is small or external discovery suffices. Position alone does not establish accessibility.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-discoverable-search; full-retrieval-component-and-privacy-contract-required`.

### `LGW-C160` — screen-reader access cannot be modeled as listening from page top

- **Locator:** Chapter 4, printed page 85; PDF page 115.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Search near the top is said to help screen-reader users because software starts at the top and otherwise they may listen to the whole page first.
- **Candidate implication:** Provide landmarks, headings, skip links, named search regions/forms, logical reading/focus order, shortcuts where appropriate, and tested navigation modes.
- **Boundary:** Screen-reader users navigate by landmarks, headings, links, forms, search, rotor/element lists, and virtual cursor—not necessarily linear listening. Visual top position is insufficient.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-screen-reader-linear-model-correction; semantic-navigation-required`.

### `LGW-C161` — link labels should connect user language to exact destination scope

- **Locator:** Chapter 4, printed pages 86–87; PDF pages 116–117.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Links should use visitor vocabulary rather than cute program names, article cleverness, internal structures, or ambiguous categories.
- **Candidate implication:** Bind visible label and accessible name to destination concept, task, scope, object, state, and route; support domain aliases and localization.
- **Boundary:** User words are evidence, not automatic labels; a familiar term may be inaccurate, contested, unsafe, or broader than the destination. Clever names can remain secondary brand metadata.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-link-information-scent; canonical-destination-and-accessible-name-required`.

### `LGW-C162` — ambiguous navigation exposes information-architecture defects

- **Locator:** Chapter 4, printed page 86; PDF page 116.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Overlapping tabs such as loans, rates, services, and accounts make a car-loan seeker uncertain where to begin.
- **Candidate implication:** Test category boundaries, cross-listing, task links, search, breadcrumbs, route recovery, and destination scope against realistic known-item and exploratory tasks.
- **Boundary:** Copy changes alone cannot repair overlapping domain concepts or organization-driven architecture. Redundancy may help if canonical routes and analytics remain coherent.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-ambiguous-navigation-diagnostic; domain-and-architecture-repair-required`.

### `LGW-C163` — mobile priorities should follow context, not a mobile persona

- **Locator:** Chapter 4, printed pages 86–88; PDF pages 116–118.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Mobile versions should strip to the primary conversations of people using mobile devices, with flight status prioritized over booking in the Lufthansa example.
- **Candidate implication:** Prioritize by current task, urgency, location, connectivity, input, authenticated state, saved context, notification source, and user choice while preserving access to full capabilities.
- **Boundary:** Device class does not determine intent; people book on phones and check status on desktops. “Strip down” can create inferior or inaccessible mobile service.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-mobile-persona-boundary; context-aware-priority-and-capability-parity-required`.

### `LGW-C164` — entry states also require authentication, tenant, and session clarity

- **Locator:** Chapter 4, printed pages 73–88; PDF pages 103–118.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The chapter covers identity, account login, task initiation, and tailored information but does not develop authenticated entry states.
- **Candidate implication:** Expose signed-in status, account/tenant, role, environment, session expiry, permissions, shared-device privacy, reauthentication, logout, switching, and recovery where relevant.
- **Boundary:** This is an inferred coverage gap, not a detailed method from the chapter. A branded home page can conceal wrong-account or wrong-tenant action with serious consequences.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-authenticated-entry-gap; identity-session-and-permission-contract-required`.

### `LGW-C165` — entry prioritization needs dynamic status and emergency governance

- **Locator:** Chapter 4, printed pages 73–88; PDF pages 103–118.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Examples contrast news, hot topics, top tasks, promotions, and current information on home pages.
- **Candidate implication:** Govern incidents, outages, emergencies, deadlines, eligibility changes, maintenance, fraud alerts, and action-required states by severity, affected scope, timing, persistence, accessibility, expiry, and remedy.
- **Boundary:** Static “top tasks” and brand messages can become unsafe during changing conditions. News is not usually primary, but urgent status may temporarily override normal hierarchy.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-dynamic-entry-priority; incident-and-expiry-governance-required`.

### `LGW-C166` — Chapter 4 provides entry-orientation patterns, not a modern routing model

- **Locator:** Chapter 4, printed pages 73–88; PDF pages 103–118.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The chapter connects discoverability, identity, voice, scope, primary tasks, forms, search, links, and mobile prioritization into a concise home-page model.
- **Candidate implication:** Retain authentic identity, orientation, direct-task, data-minimization, search, information-scent, and priority principles while expanding them across every route and state.
- **Boundary:** The model remains home-page-, SEO-, brand-, device-, and conversion-centered and underdevelops deep links, authentication, permissions, privacy, accessibility semantics, dynamic incidents, and exit autonomy. Current external technical documentation remains unverified because the Developer Index quota was exhausted.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-entry-orientation-foundation; modern-route-state-and-rights-model-required`.

### `LGW-C167` — pathway pages are transit surfaces with a selection purpose

- **Locator:** Chapter 5, printed pages 89–90; PDF pages 119–120.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Pathway or landing/gallery pages primarily help visitors scan, select, and move toward information, products, services, or tasks rather than consume destination content.
- **Candidate implication:** Give every routing surface an explicit entry context, choice set, destination relation, selection task, and successful onward state.
- **Boundary:** A route may also need scope, status, eligibility, warning, consent, or orientation. Calling prose a distraction cannot authorize omission of material information.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-routing-surface-purpose; protective-context-preserved`.

### `LGW-C168` — information scent should connect a choice to the visitor's goal

- **Locator:** Chapter 5, printed page 90; PDF page 120.
- **Documented practitioner position:** People forage for links whose wording strongly suggests that the destination will satisfy their need.
- **Candidate implication:** Specify each route label by user vocabulary, destination purpose, expected result, scope, and state; validate prediction against the actual destination.
- **Boundary:** Scent is a metaphor and a promising label is not evidence that the destination works. Misleadingly strong scent can manufacture clicks while breaking trust.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-label-destination-prediction; runtime-parity-required`.

### `LGW-C169` — find mode and read mode require different content density

- **Locator:** Chapter 5, printed pages 90–91; PDF pages 120–121.
- **Documented practitioner position:** People navigating are usually in find mode: they scan choices and perhaps short descriptions but avoid dense welcome or marketing paragraphs.
- **Candidate implication:** Classify route content as choice-critical, protective, optional orientation, or destination detail; foreground the first two and defer the rest.
- **Boundary:** Modes overlap, and some people must read before choosing. Legal, safety, privacy, cost, access, or irreversible consequences cannot be deferred merely to accelerate movement.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-find-read-mode-distinction; material-choice-information-required`.

### `LGW-C170` — observed nonreading is a design signal, not user failure

- **Locator:** Chapter 5, Case Study 5-1, printed pages 91–93; PDF pages 121–123.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Test participants repeatedly selected a disabled alphabet letter rather than read the explanatory paragraph on a vital-records routing page.
- **Candidate implication:** When observed action conflicts with prose, test label, affordance, hierarchy, availability, instruction placement, and route structure before blaming attention or literacy.
- **Boundary:** One historical test with 16 described participants does not establish prevalence or causality, and the ledger does not inherit participant data or reuse rights.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-behavior-over-author-assumption; limited-historical-study`.

### `LGW-C171` — instructions should expose the actual selection variable

- **Locator:** Chapter 5, Case Study 5-1, printed pages 91–93; PDF pages 121–123.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Replacing an indirect alphabet control with a short instruction and open state/territory list made the required choice—where the event occurred—clear.
- **Candidate implication:** Name the decision rule at the choice point and present values in a representation that maps directly to it.
- **Boundary:** Open lists, maps, search, filters, and select controls have different scale, accessibility, localization, error, and maintenance properties; the historical redesign is not a universal component rule.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-selection-variable-exposure; component-validation-required`.

### `LGW-C172` — public-service routing must clarify distributed authority

- **Locator:** Chapter 5, Case Study 5-1, printed pages 91–93; PDF pages 121–123.
- **Documented practitioner position:** U.S. birth certificates are obtained from the relevant state or territory rather than a national registry, so the route must explain which jurisdiction controls the record.
- **Candidate implication:** Model responsible authority, jurisdiction, eligibility, handoff destination, source status, service boundary, and recovery when a service routes across organizations.
- **Boundary:** A clear handoff does not guarantee that the receiving office is authoritative, current, accessible, safe, or able to complete the task; external-route verification is required.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-cross-authority-routing; verified-handoff-contract-required`.

### `LGW-C173` — domain terms should be bridged to the words people seek

- **Locator:** Chapter 5, Case Study 5-1, printed pages 92–93; PDF pages 122–123.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The official term “vital records” should remain where institutionally necessary but be connected to familiar items such as birth, death, marriage, and divorce certificates.
- **Candidate implication:** Preserve canonical terminology while adding evidence-backed aliases, examples, definitions, and search mappings.
- **Boundary:** Familiar words may be legally imprecise, culturally variable, outdated, or jurisdiction-specific. Aliases must not silently broaden or narrow eligibility or record type.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-canonical-term-alias-bridge; scope-parity-required`.

### `LGW-C174` — content creep can reintroduce solved routing friction

- **Locator:** Chapter 5, Case Study 5-1, printed page 93; PDF page 123.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The improved state list persisted, but unnecessary explanatory text accumulated again over time.
- **Candidate implication:** Assign route-content ownership, purpose tags, review triggers, usage evidence, and expiry; require additions to justify their effect on choice and obligation.
- **Boundary:** Less text is not inherently better, and later additions may reflect changed law, risk, access, or service conditions. Governance must distinguish clutter from newly material content.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-routing-content-governance; no-automatic-deletion`.

### `LGW-C175` — pathway pages share overview and selection functions with tables of contents

- **Locator:** Chapter 5, printed page 94; PDF page 124.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Like a table of contents, a pathway page should show what is offered and help people choose where to go.
- **Candidate implication:** Test both set comprehension and destination selection, including whether choices are mutually intelligible and collectively adequate.
- **Boundary:** The table-of-contents analogy underrepresents dynamic state, filtering, permissions, personalization, dependencies, and actions that occur without navigation.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-overview-and-selection-dual-purpose; dynamic-route-model-required`.

### `LGW-C176` — prominent imagery and congratulatory prose can delay the next task

- **Locator:** Chapter 5, Case Study 5-2, printed pages 94–96; PDF pages 124–126.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Eye-tracking participants skipped a large image and congratulatory homeowner text and moved directly to insurance-information links; the team moved links upward.
- **Candidate implication:** Prioritize the next evidence-backed task over decorative media or organization-authored ceremony and validate the resolved hierarchy behaviorally.
- **Boundary:** Gaze does not alone establish comprehension or value, and one scenario does not prove that imagery or acknowledgment is always irrelevant. Rights, context, emotion, and accessibility still matter.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-next-task-salience; limited-historical-eye-tracking-evidence`.

### `LGW-C177` — descriptions should supply only decision-changing information

- **Locator:** Chapter 5, printed pages 96–98; PDF pages 126–128.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Brief descriptions help when labels alone do not let visitors distinguish options; examples add price, maker, color, rating, service, or feature details.
- **Candidate implication:** Derive route-description fields from the questions and attributes that change selection, comparison, eligibility, cost, risk, or next action.
- **Boundary:** “Short” is not a semantic criterion, and commercial attributes can be incomplete, sponsored, stale, or inaccessible. Material facts and uncertainty must not be omitted for compactness.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-decision-supporting-description; material-fact-governance-required`.

### `LGW-C178` — organization jargon breaks routing when it lacks a user-language bridge

- **Locator:** Chapter 5, printed page 97; PDF page 127.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Pathways written only in internal terminology fail visitors who do not know the organization's categories.
- **Candidate implication:** Test taxonomy and labels with representative people and map observed language to canonical concepts rather than exposing the org chart.
- **Boundary:** User vocabulary is heterogeneous and can be inaccurate or harmful; internal terms may be required for professional, regulatory, or record precision.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-user-language-routing; canonical-precision-preserved`.

### `LGW-C179` — pictures alone rarely carry sufficient selection meaning

- **Locator:** Chapter 5, printed pages 97–98; PDF pages 127–128.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Product galleries often need names, prices, ratings, variants, specifications, or other text because images may not reveal meaningful differences.
- **Candidate implication:** Treat images as one representation of a product or route, paired with structured, textual, programmatic, comparable attributes.
- **Boundary:** The needed attributes vary by task and person; visual and textual representations can both mislead. Completeness, accuracy, locale, unit, currency, availability, and update time require governance.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-multimodal-selection-content; structured-attribute-contract-required`.

### `LGW-C180` — image-based routing requires meaningful nonvisual equivalence

- **Locator:** Chapter 5, printed page 98; PDF page 128.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Gallery routes must provide descriptions for blind and low-vision visitors rather than abandon them mid-journey.
- **Candidate implication:** Define accessible names and descriptions from image purpose and decision-relevant content, preserving equivalent selection and action.
- **Boundary:** Alternative text alone does not make a gallery operable; semantics, grouping, focus, comparison, keyboard/touch behavior, updates, errors, and testing with disabled people remain necessary.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-nonvisual-route-equivalence; alt-text-alone-insufficient`.

### `LGW-C181` — fragments can be valid microcontent when semantics remain complete

- **Locator:** Chapter 5, printed page 98; PDF page 128.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Route descriptions can use fragments or bullets because visitors need compact help choosing rather than continuous prose.
- **Candidate implication:** Permit fragments for labels and structured attributes when component context supplies the missing grammar and the proposition remains unambiguous.
- **Boundary:** Fragments can lose actor, action, condition, negation, time, or scope and may localize or synthesize poorly. High-consequence content may require complete sentences.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-purpose-specific-fragments; proposition-completeness-required`.

### `LGW-C182` — route quality is not an arbitrary click-count target

- **Locator:** Chapter 5, printed pages 98–99; PDF pages 128–129.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The three-click rule is rejected; a smooth, predictable path can outperform a shallower organization-centered route even with an extra step.
- **Candidate implication:** Evaluate task success, time, prediction accuracy, wrong turns, recovery, confidence, access, and consequence rather than minimizing clicks in isolation.
- **Boundary:** Extra steps can still impose cost, abandonment, exposure, or coercion, especially in repeated, urgent, mobile, assisted, or accessibility-dependent tasks.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-path-quality-over-click-count; burden-and-consequence-bounds-required`.

### `LGW-C183` — user-task architecture can improve outcomes despite greater depth

- **Locator:** Chapter 5, printed page 99; PDF page 129.
- **Documented practitioner position:** A cited redesign organized around user tasks raised reported success from 72% to 95% and reduced average task time from 132 to 50 seconds while adding about one click.
- **Candidate implication:** Compare architecture variants through preregistered task and population measures, treating depth as one explanatory variable rather than the objective.
- **Boundary:** The summarized historical study does not provide enough method, uncertainty, task, population, or confound detail here to support a transferable effect claim.
- **Promotion status:** `corroborating-historical-outcome-example; independent-replication-required`.

### `LGW-C184` — each route step should minimize avoidable interpretation effort

- **Locator:** Chapter 5, printed page 99; PDF page 129.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Visitors should not need to stop and puzzle over what to do at each pathway step.
- **Candidate implication:** Test label clarity, option distinction, hierarchy, prerequisite visibility, state feedback, and expected destination with representative tasks.
- **Boundary:** Deliberation is sometimes protective and necessary for consent, finance, health, law, safety, or irreversible action. “Do not make people think” cannot justify pressure or concealment.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-avoidable-interpretation-reduction; protective-deliberation-preserved`.

### `LGW-C185` — repeated backtracking is a diagnostic signal, not inherently failure

- **Locator:** Chapter 5, printed page 99; PDF page 129.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Repeatedly entering and backing out of plausible paths—“pogo-sticking”—signals weak labels or architecture and can cause frustration.
- **Candidate implication:** Instrument wrong turns and recovery with privacy safeguards; inspect whether routes support comparison, history, state preservation, undo, and alternate paths.
- **Boundary:** Back navigation can be intentional comparison or healthy exploration. Removing it would worsen autonomy; the response is diagnosis and recovery, not trapping forward progress.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-backtracking-diagnostic; navigation-autonomy-required`.

### `LGW-C186` — first-position choice is a behavioral risk requiring fair ordering

- **Locator:** Chapter 5, printed pages 99–100; PDF pages 129–130.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Busy visitors often satisfice by choosing the first option that appears adequate, so order materially affects selection.
- **Candidate implication:** Establish an explicit ordering policy based on task relevance, urgency, consequence, rights, accessibility, and evidence; test across visual and sequential access.
- **Boundary:** Position effects vary, and order cannot substitute for clarity. Observed satisficing does not authorize exploiting limited attention.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-order-as-choice-architecture; fairness-and-evidence-required`.

### `LGW-C187` — organization-preferred ordering can become manipulative choice architecture

- **Locator:** Chapter 5, printed page 100; PDF page 130.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The chapter advises placing the option the organization wants selected first.
- **Candidate implication:** Reject organization preference as sufficient authority; permit preferential ordering only when it demonstrably advances the person's informed goal without hiding alternatives or shifting harm.
