- **Boundary:** Commercial, support-cost, enrollment, data-collection, or policy goals can conflict with user welfare, affected parties, legal duties, and meaningful choice.
- **Promotion status:** `reject-unbounded-preferred-option-ordering; informed-user-interest-and-fairness-required`.

### `LGW-C188` — age-based clicking claims should not enter product logic

- **Locator:** Chapter 5, printed pages 99–100; PDF pages 129–130.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The chapter generalizes that younger visitors act sooner while older visitors review more choices.
- **Candidate implication:** Replace age inference with direct evidence about task, familiarity, urgency, risk tolerance, reading/input mode, and observed behavior.
- **Boundary:** The chapter supplies no adequate basis here for age segmentation, and such generalization can stereotype, exclude, or mask cohort, accessibility, expertise, and context effects.
- **Promotion status:** `reject-age-as-click-behavior-proxy; direct-capability-and-task-evidence-required`.

### `LGW-C189` — sequential access makes source order part of route meaning

- **Locator:** Chapter 5, printed page 100; PDF page 130.
- **Documented practitioner position:** People listening to a page may select the first plausible option they hear, making order consequential beyond visual layout.
- **Candidate implication:** Align DOM, accessibility-tree, reading, focus, visual, and localized order; provide headings, landmarks, bypass, and concise distinguishable link names.
- **Boundary:** Screen-reader users are not one behavior type, and first-choice behavior is not inevitable. Current standards and assistive-technology testing remain required.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-sequential-order-contract; current-accessibility-validation-required`.

### `LGW-C190` — Chapter 5 offers durable routing heuristics but not a complete route-state contract

- **Locator:** Chapter 5, printed pages 89–100; PDF pages 119–130.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The chapter combines information scent, low-density pathways, overview and selection, concise descriptions, meaningful imagery, path smoothness, recovery signals, and ordering.
- **Candidate implication:** Retain these as routing candidates within a model covering route identity, state, permissions, provenance, locale, accessibility, search, history, deep links, analytics, external handoff, and recovery.
- **Boundary:** The 2012 treatment underdevelops authenticated and dynamic routes, permission-filtered choices, localization, current component semantics, privacy-respecting measurement, and ethical ordering. Developer Index verification remains unavailable because its quota is exhausted.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-routing-foundation; modern-route-state-and-ethics-model-required`.

### `LGW-C191` — information needs should not inherit document boundaries automatically

- **Locator:** Chapter 6, printed pages 101–103; PDF pages 131–133.
- **Documented practitioner position:** People often want the instruction or answer relevant now rather than an entire manual, handbook, report, or benefits binder.
- **Candidate implication:** Model canonical propositions, tasks, questions, conditions, and evidence independently from the documents that originally contain them, then project appropriate units for each use.
- **Boundary:** Documents can be authoritative records, signed instruments, complete evidence, narratives, datasets, or required disclosures. Decomposition must preserve provenance, scope, sequence, legal force, and access to the whole.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-semantic-decomposition; authoritative-document-preservation-required`.

### `LGW-C192` — “right information, amount, time, person, medium” needs explicit governance

- **Locator:** Chapter 6, printed page 102; PDF page 132.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Content strategy should deliver the right information in the right amount at the right time to the right person in the right medium.
- **Candidate implication:** Represent information need, actor or role, interaction state, timing, modality, completeness, source, and delivery constraints as separately testable fields.
- **Boundary:** “Right” is a decision requiring evidence and authority, not an intrinsic property. Personalization can infer sensitive attributes, restrict access, or hide material facts.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-contextual-delivery-frame; authority-privacy-and-completeness-required`.

### `LGW-C193` — the index-card metaphor supports modularity but can fragment meaning

- **Locator:** Chapter 6, printed pages 102–104; PDF pages 132–134.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Online information can be divided into findable “cards” because digital storage does not impose the physical bundling constraints of paper.
- **Candidate implication:** Create cohesive semantic units with identity, purpose, dependencies, context, canonical source, version, and relations rather than treating pages as indivisible records.
- **Boundary:** Small units can scatter qualifications, increase navigation, duplicate facts, and break reading continuity. A page or card is a projection, not necessarily the canonical content boundary.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-modular-content-candidate; semantic-cohesion-and-lineage-required`.

### `LGW-C194` — decomposition succeeds only when units remain discoverable and connected

- **Locator:** Chapter 6, printed pages 103–104; PDF pages 133–134.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Separately useful topics need pathway pages and wording that matches the terms visitors search for.
- **Candidate implication:** Couple decomposition with taxonomy, aliases, search indexing, route labels, inbound context, related links, and destination parity.
- **Boundary:** Search terms and pathways do not guarantee completeness, ranking, permissions, freshness, or recovery; fragmentation may make the correct unit harder to find.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-decomposition-discovery-coupling; retrieval-validation-required`.

### `LGW-C195` — organization axes are alternatives to test, not a universal taxonomy

- **Locator:** Chapter 6, printed pages 104–113; PDF pages 134–143.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Content may be organized by questions, topic or task, product type, information type, people, life event, or time/sequence.
- **Candidate implication:** Record candidate grouping axis, applicability, audience evidence, overlaps, cross-links, exceptional items, and selection outcomes; support multiple projections over shared concepts.
- **Boundary:** The seven axes are neither exhaustive nor mutually exclusive. Geography, jurisdiction, status, risk, goal, eligibility, channel, ownership, and other dimensions may govern a domain.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-testable-organization-axis-set; nonexclusive-multiaxial-model-required`.

### `LGW-C196` — real questions can provide recognizable entry points

- **Locator:** Chapter 6, printed pages 104–105; PDF pages 134–135.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Evidence-backed questions in the visitor's voice can organize content and help people recognize where their need is addressed.
- **Candidate implication:** Link each displayed question to its evidence, canonical intent, answer scope, route, locale, and follow-up states.
- **Boundary:** Question wording may be invented, unrepresentative, false-premised, unsafe, or too specific to scale. A question heading cannot substitute for a coherent taxonomy or accurate answer.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-question-led-entry; evidence-and-answer-parity-required`.

### `LGW-C197` — topic and task distinctions should preserve policy and action semantics

- **Locator:** Chapter 6, printed pages 105–108; PDF pages 135–138.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Topics group information while tasks answer action-oriented questions, although the distinction can be fuzzy.
- **Candidate implication:** Type content as concept, fact, policy, eligibility, procedure, task, decision, or mixed unit and express the relations among them.
- **Boundary:** Relabeling a topic as a task can imply available action or authorization that does not exist; task-first design must not suppress governing policy, rationale, exception, or appeal.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-topic-task-typing; policy-action-separation-required`.

### `LGW-C198` — search analytics are one vocabulary signal, not audience truth

- **Locator:** Chapter 6, printed page 105; PDF page 135.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Site-search analytics can reveal what people seek and which words they use for topics and tasks.
- **Candidate implication:** Treat queries as privacy-governed behavioral traces and triangulate them with research, support, task outcomes, zero results, reformulations, and domain terminology.
- **Boundary:** Logs exclude unsearched needs, failed expression, nonusers, blocked queries, and external search; frequency does not establish importance, correctness, or permission to retain sensitive text.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-query-vocabulary-input; privacy-and-triangulation-required`.

### `LGW-C199` — product-type grouping works only when people know and share the categories

- **Locator:** Chapter 6, printed page 106; PDF page 136.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Product type can organize commerce when visitors know what category they seek and the site's grouping matches theirs.
- **Candidate implication:** Validate category recognition, boundaries, alternate names, comparison attributes, and routes for people who begin with a problem rather than a product class.
- **Boundary:** Merchandising taxonomies may reflect inventory, contracts, sponsorship, or organization ownership rather than user goals, and products may belong in several categories.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-product-taxonomy-condition; problem-led-and-multicategory-paths-required`.

### `LGW-C200` — duplicative placement can support differing mental groupings without duplicating truth

- **Locator:** Chapter 6, printed page 106; PDF page 136.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Offering a product or topic under more than one category can help visitors whose groupings differ.
- **Candidate implication:** Support multiple routes and aliases to one canonical entity rather than maintaining independent copies.
- **Boundary:** Repeated placement can confuse hierarchy, analytics, breadcrumbs, canonical URLs, inventory, translation, and maintenance if identity and lineage are not explicit.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-multiple-route-single-source; canonical-identity-required`.

### `LGW-C201` — information type is useful when relations cross its boundaries

- **Locator:** Chapter 6, printed pages 106–109; PDF pages 136–139.
- **Documented practitioner position:** News, events, policies, procedures, advice, products, releases, and research may be separated by type but should link laterally when a visitor needs the related item.
- **Candidate implication:** Type information explicitly and model bidirectional semantic relations such as governed-by, implements, explains, evidences, announces, supersedes, and offers.
- **Boundary:** Generic “related” links can be noisy or commercial, and bidirectionality may be inappropriate for security, permissions, archival state, or asymmetric authority.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-typed-content-and-relations; relation-semantics-and-access-control-required`.

### `LGW-C202` — policy eligibility and procedural action must not be conflated

- **Locator:** Chapter 6, printed pages 107–108; PDF pages 137–138.
- **Documented practitioner position:** “Can I?” questions concern policy or eligibility while “How do I?” questions concern procedure; linked but separate pages may serve them.
- **Candidate implication:** Bind procedures to current policy authority, eligibility predicates, effective dates, exceptions, required evidence, consequences, and appeal or assistance.
- **Boundary:** A procedure without policy context may lead people into an inapplicable task, while a policy without an actionable route may deny service in practice.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-policy-procedure-separation; executable-linkage-required`.

### `LGW-C203` — next-step links should follow user intent across organizational silos

- **Locator:** Chapter 6, printed pages 108–109; PDF pages 138–139.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Each page should anticipate where visitors reasonably want to go next, even when the relevant content belongs to another category, team, or medium.
- **Candidate implication:** Define next-step intent, destination owner, prerequisite, handoff state, permissions, return path, and verification for every consequential transition.
- **Boundary:** Predictive links can become promotional detours, surveillance, or clutter. The organization may not control external destinations, and an inferred next step is not consent.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-cross-silo-journey-linking; handoff-and-intent-controls-required`.

### `LGW-C204` — editorial and marketing coordination cannot make business support the sole content test

- **Locator:** Chapter 6, printed pages 108–109; PDF pages 138–139.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Editorial and product-marketing teams should coordinate so content relates to products and supports business goals.
- **Candidate implication:** Coordinate canonical facts, calendars, campaigns, service readiness, disclosure, expiry, and ownership while measuring user and affected-party outcomes separately.
- **Boundary:** Advice, research, support, public information, and safety content may need independence from product promotion. Business alignment cannot override truth, rights, editorial integrity, or user welfare.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-cross-team-coordination; reject-business-goal-as-sole-authority`.

### `LGW-C205` — role-based organization requires immediate and nonstigmatizing self-recognition

- **Locator:** Chapter 6, printed pages 109–111; PDF pages 139–141.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Dividing by people can work when visitors recognize the relevant group, accept its label, and can select the role that fits the current visit.
- **Candidate implication:** Validate role labels, definitions, overlap, change, refusal, generic entry, and correction with represented participants.
- **Boundary:** Self-identification may disclose sensitive identity or eligibility and can exclude people who reject, misunderstand, or do not fit the offered categories.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-role-route-conditions; privacy-inclusion-and-generic-path-required`.

### `LGW-C206` — a person may occupy several roles and switch by task

- **Locator:** Chapter 6, printed pages 110–111; PDF pages 140–141.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The same individual can be an employer and employee or hold several overlapping identities, choosing differently on separate visits.
- **Candidate implication:** Represent role as task- and state-specific rather than a fixed person attribute; support switching without lost context or hidden reclassification.
- **Boundary:** Roles may be simultaneous, contested, delegated, permission-bound, or assigned by law. A user-facing choice does not itself establish authorization.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-contextual-role-model; authorization-and-switching-contract-required`.

### `LGW-C207` — audience division can be local rather than governing the whole experience

- **Locator:** Chapter 6, printed pages 111–112; PDF pages 141–142.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Different audience routes may be useful only within a relevant section, such as professional versus patient information or giver versus recipient tasks.
- **Candidate implication:** Scope segmentation to the decision it improves and preserve shared navigation, canonical meaning, cross-role access, and easy switching.
- **Boundary:** Local segmentation can still create unequal quality, missing disclosures, stereotyped complexity, or accidental permission walls.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-locally-scoped-segmentation; semantic-parity-and-access-required`.

### `LGW-C208` — life-event organization can orient journeys but carries sensitive assumptions

- **Locator:** Chapter 6, printed page 112; PDF page 142.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Life stages such as pregnancy, parenting, or retirement can provide a chronological organizing frame.
- **Candidate implication:** Use event or transition models with optional entry points, nonlinearity, uncertainty, privacy, exceptions, and terminology chosen with affected people.
- **Boundary:** Life-event labels may presume family structure, fertility, gender, age, health, culture, or desired outcomes and can expose highly sensitive status.
- **Promotion status:** `candidate-life-event-routing; sensitive-nonlinear-and-inclusive-model-required`.

### `LGW-C209` — sequence can reveal process without forcing one linear journey

- **Locator:** Chapter 6, printed page 112; PDF page 142.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Time or sequence can show what happens first and next while allowing people to access steps in another order.
- **Candidate implication:** Represent prerequisites, optionality, parallel work, current state, completion evidence, dependencies, branching, resumption, and rollback.
- **Boundary:** Displayed order may be advisory rather than required; forcing it can block experts, exceptional cases, assistive workflows, or people joining mid-process.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-process-orientation; nonlinearity-and-state-required`.

### `LGW-C210` — an organization chart is rarely a usable public taxonomy

- **Locator:** Chapter 6, printed pages 112–113; PDF pages 142–143.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Visitors generally neither know nor care which internal unit owns a topic and should not need that knowledge to satisfy a goal.
- **Candidate implication:** Route by user-recognizable concepts and tasks while retaining internal ownership as metadata and operational responsibility.
- **Boundary:** Organizational identity may be material for authority, jurisdiction, accountability, contact, complaint, records, or regulated service and must remain visible where relevant.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-user-facing-taxonomy-separation; accountable-owner-visibility-preserved`.

### `LGW-C211` — content grain should follow a coherent need, not an arbitrary unit size

- **Locator:** Chapter 6, printed pages 113–116; PDF pages 143–146.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Page size should reflect how much information a visitor needs for one conversation, with unrelated help questions split into independently selectable topics.
- **Candidate implication:** Define unit cohesion through intent, prerequisites, decision, action, consequence, and likely co-use; test split and combined projections.
- **Boundary:** A single “conversation” can span many dependent topics, and splitting may increase cognitive load, latency, lost context, or inconsistent truth.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-intent-cohesion-granularity; whole-journey-validation-required`.

### `LGW-C212` — large works can gain task-oriented projections without losing whole-work access

- **Locator:** Chapter 6, printed pages 115–116; PDF pages 145–146.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Large documents or reports can become a mini-site with a pathway page and separately titled topical pages.
- **Candidate implication:** Project sections from a canonical work with stable citations, version/effective date, full-work view or download, cross-section context, and synchronized corrections.
- **Boundary:** Splitting can change evidentiary meaning, pagination, quotations, signatures, footnotes, appendices, or archival fixity. The projection must not impersonate the record.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-whole-and-section-projections; record-integrity-required`.

### `LGW-C213` — “three or four scrolls” is not a defensible universal limit

- **Locator:** Chapter 6, printed page 116; PDF page 146.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The chapter suggests three or four vertical scrolls as a maximum while acknowledging that people scroll when continuation is apparent.
- **Candidate implication:** Replace fixed scroll counts with task completion, findability, comprehension, position recovery, performance, and preference evidence.
- **Boundary:** A “scroll” varies by viewport, zoom, text size, orientation, input, locale, content state, and assistive technology; splitting solely to meet this number may worsen use.
- **Promotion status:** `reject-fixed-scroll-limit; outcome-based-length-evaluation-required`.

### `LGW-C214` — performance and fragmentation create a two-sided tradeoff

- **Locator:** Chapter 6, printed page 116; PDF page 146.
- **Documented practitioner position:** One heavy page can delay irrelevant content, while many small pages can create repeated waits and make connected information harder to integrate.
- **Candidate implication:** Measure payload, rendering, interaction readiness, cache/offline behavior, navigation latency, request count, continuity, and task-specific co-use for each projection.
- **Boundary:** Connection speed is only one factor; device resources, scripts, media, server behavior, privacy tooling, assistive technology, and platform architecture also determine performance.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-performance-cohesion-tradeoff; current-runtime-measurement-required`.

### `LGW-C215` — print is a distinct projection with its own task contract

- **Locator:** Chapter 6, printed pages 116–118; PDF pages 146–148.
- **Documented practitioner position:** People may want one topic or a whole connected work in print, and sites can combine separately browsed sections into a coherent print view.
- **Candidate implication:** Specify printable scope, selection, order, source URL, title, version/date, page breaks, visible links, alternatives, tables, images, and material state.
- **Boundary:** Print may expose private data, stale dynamic values, hidden sections, inaccessible color, or incomplete interactive content; not every task has a faithful paper equivalent.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-purpose-specific-print-projection; privacy-and-semantic-parity-required`.

### `LGW-C216` — responsive adaptation must preserve meaning rather than merely shrink chunks

- **Locator:** Chapter 6, printed pages 118–119; PDF pages 148–149.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Planning small content units can help full and mobile sites, and key messages can be reused in social channels.
- **Candidate implication:** Preserve canonical propositions, order, qualification, actions, and state across responsive surfaces while varying presentation and density appropriately.
- **Boundary:** Mobile users do not need less truth, and social snippets can detach claims from source, context, consent, corrections, or expiry. Small chunks are not inherently reusable content.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-cross-surface-semantic-units; no-mobile-truncation-or-contextless-reuse`.

### `LGW-C217` — format choice should begin with use, not source-file convenience

- **Locator:** Chapter 6, printed pages 119–121; PDF pages 149–151.
- **Documented practitioner position:** PDF preserves print layout and can suit works intended for offline study or printing, but should not be the default merely because a paper document exists.
- **Candidate implication:** Choose HTML, PDF, structured data, print, download, or mixed projections from task, record, accessibility, offline, citation, signing, annotation, and distribution requirements.
- **Boundary:** Budget and authoring convenience do not waive access, legal, preservation, security, or maintenance obligations; PDF capabilities and readers have changed since 2012.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-purpose-led-format-selection; current-capability-validation-required`.

### `LGW-C218` — HTML and PDF may serve complementary modes

- **Locator:** Chapter 6, printed pages 120–121; PDF pages 150–151.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Journal participants reportedly used HTML for skimming or extracting facts and PDF for careful offline reading or printing, supporting provision of both in some cases.
- **Candidate implication:** Offer synchronized projections when distinct tasks justify them, with explicit format, size, language, accessibility, version, source identity, and update parity.
- **Boundary:** Two formats double potential drift and do not guarantee accessibility or preference coverage. The described studies lack enough detail here for population-wide inference.
- **Promotion status:** `candidate-complementary-format-projections; synchronization-and-current-research-required`.

### `LGW-C219` — form format must follow the actual submission workflow

- **Locator:** Chapter 6, printed page 120; PDF page 150.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Fillable PDFs may fit a process that remains essentially paper-like while allowing electronic completion and submission.
- **Candidate implication:** Model field semantics, validation, save/resume, signatures, attachments, privacy, security, confirmation, records, accessibility, and alternate channels before choosing a form technology.
- **Boundary:** A fillable PDF can be difficult on mobile or assistive technology and may transmit sensitive data insecurely; resemblance to paper is not sufficient justification.
- **Promotion status:** `candidate-fillable-document-workflow; end-to-end-form-contract-required`.

### `LGW-C220` — PDF-only delivery can impose nested navigation and access burdens

- **Locator:** Chapter 6, printed pages 121–122; PDF pages 151–152.
- **Documented practitioner position:** PDF is a poor sole format when people need only a fragment, use small screens, do not print, lack familiarity, face slow downloads, or need accessible information.
- **Candidate implication:** Declare file type, size, language, pages, accessibility status, destination behavior, and HTML alternative before a document link; support direct section routes where possible.
- **Boundary:** These risks are contextual, not proof that every PDF is unusable. HTML can also be inaccessible, slow, fragmented, or impossible to archive.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-pdf-only-risk-model; comparative-format-testing-required`.

### `LGW-C221` — accessible PDF requires structure beyond OCR or visible appearance

- **Locator:** Chapter 6, printed page 122; PDF page 152.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Headings, image alternatives, tags, reading structure, and text recognition matter for assistive use; unprocessed scans are image-only and many historical PDFs lack usable structure.
- **Candidate implication:** Require current conformance checks for tags, order, headings, language, tables, forms, links, alternatives, metadata, reflow/zoom, keyboard use, and assistive-technology journeys.
- **Boundary:** OCR creates text, not correctness or accessibility; automated checkers and tagging alone do not prove equivalent comprehension or operation. Current standards remain unverified due to index quota.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-structured-document-accessibility; human-and-current-technical-validation-required`.

### `LGW-C222` — converting old PDFs is a governed migration, not a deletion instruction

- **Locator:** Chapter 6, printed pages 122–123; PDF pages 152–153.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Paper-oriented PDFs often contain long prose and sparse headings and should be converted into useful web content when their information remains important.
- **Candidate implication:** Inventory PDFs; determine authority, currency, rights, retention, citations, dependencies, accessibility, demand, and disposition; create linked HTML projections with redirect, archive, rollback, and parity verification.
- **Boundary:** Removal can destroy public records, stable citations, signed evidence, offline access, or legal obligations. Conversion can introduce semantic errors, and the source may need permanent preservation.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-document-modernization-candidate; records-and-migration-governance-required`.

### `LGW-C223` — Chapter 6 supplies decomposition heuristics, not a complete content graph

- **Locator:** Chapter 6, printed pages 101–123; PDF pages 131–153.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The chapter joins modular information, alternative grouping axes, related links, purpose-based granularity, responsive/print concerns, and selective document delivery.
- **Candidate implication:** Retain the semantic-unit, multiaxial taxonomy, relation, projection, and format-choice ideas within a governed content graph spanning source, state, locale, permission, rights, accessibility, channel, and lifecycle.
- **Boundary:** Page/card metaphors remain URL-centric; dated length, device, SEO, PDF-reader, and accessibility claims are not implementation contracts. External technical verification is unavailable while the Developer Index quota is exhausted.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-semantic-organization-foundation; modern-content-graph-and-runtime-contract-required`.

### `LGW-C224` — destination pages must confirm route and answer availability quickly

- **Locator:** Chapter 7, printed pages 125–126; PDF pages 155–156.
- **Documented practitioner position:** People often scan a destination page first to confirm that they arrived where expected and that the sought information is present.
- **Candidate implication:** Expose destination identity, scope, key answer or action, state, source, and route continuity early in visual and sequential order.
- **Boundary:** Scanning is not universal, and rapid confirmation cannot replace complete reading where safety, consent, law, or deliberation requires it.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-destination-confirmation; complete-task-context-required`.

### `LGW-C225` — relevance is an evidence decision, not permission to minimize by instinct

- **Locator:** Chapter 7, printed pages 126–128; PDF pages 156–158.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Content should focus on what visitors want or need rather than organizational history, welcome language, or self-focused prose.
- **Candidate implication:** Classify each proposition by user goal, question, task, obligation, consequence, evidence, and placement; remove or defer only through governed disposition.
- **Boundary:** People may not know to ask about eligibility, risk, cost, privacy, rights, exceptions, or downstream effects. Low-interest information can still be materially required.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-relevance-classification; unasked-material-information-preserved`.

### `LGW-C226` — legacy copy should not anchor the problem definition

- **Locator:** Chapter 7, Checklist 7-1, printed pages 127–128; PDF pages 157–158.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Writers should temporarily set existing copy aside, reconsider purpose and architecture, derive questions, then use the old material as one source.
- **Candidate implication:** Separate source inventory from target requirements and compare them through traceable proposition mapping rather than editing the inherited page in place.
- **Boundary:** Setting copy aside must not lose authoritative facts, records, stakeholder commitments, legal language, dependencies, or prior research.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-requirements-before-rewrite; source-lineage-preserved`.

### `LGW-C227` — strategy fit cannot be the only retain-or-drop gate

- **Locator:** Chapter 7, Checklist 7-1, printed page 127; PDF page 157.
- **Documented practitioner position:** A topic that does not fit content, marketing, social, or information-architecture strategy may be dropped.
- **Candidate implication:** Evaluate strategic fit alongside legal duty, rights, safety, public record, accessibility, service dependency, archive, evidence, and affected-party need.
- **Boundary:** Strategy can be incomplete, biased, commercially narrow, or stale. Organizational nonalignment cannot authorize deletion of required or protective content.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-strategy-fit-boundary; disposition-authority-and-obligations-required`.

### `LGW-C228` — personas can focus hypotheses but cannot supply user evidence

- **Locator:** Chapter 7, Checklist 7-1 and Case Study 7-1, printed pages 127–131; PDF pages 157–161.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The process adopts personas' perspectives to generate and order likely questions for a topic.
- **Candidate implication:** Use personas only as source-linked projections; label every generated question as observed, reported, inferred, organization-required, or speculative.
- **Boundary:** A fictional persona neither asks questions nor validates wording, order, relevance, accessibility, or population coverage.
- **Promotion status:** `candidate-persona-question-prompt; direct-evidence-required`.

### `LGW-C229` — question order should follow task dependency and consequence, not imagined dialogue alone

- **Locator:** Chapter 7, Checklist 7-1, printed pages 127–128; PDF pages 157–158.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Questions should be arranged in the order visitors would logically ask them.
- **Candidate implication:** Order by prerequisite, decision point, urgency, consequence, frequency, rights, vulnerability, action sequence, and representative evidence.
- **Boundary:** There may be several valid orders, and chronology, severity, or legal precedence can override conversational plausibility.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-question-ordering-candidate; multifactored-order-policy-required`.

### `LGW-C230` — source reuse requires semantic reconciliation, not cut-and-paste convenience

- **Locator:** Chapter 7, Checklist 7-1, printed page 128; PDF page 158.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Existing text may be cut, pasted, rewritten, and edited into clear answers.
- **Candidate implication:** Map source propositions to target answers with transformation, authority, date, scope, qualifier, conflict, and omitted-context records.
- **Boundary:** Copying can preserve stale facts, incompatible scope, hidden dependencies, legal wording changes, or duplicated canonical content.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-source-to-answer-transformation; semantic-diff-and-authority-required`.

### `LGW-C231` — unanswered user questions are content and service defects

- **Locator:** Chapter 7, Checklist 7-1, printed page 128; PDF page 158.
- **Documented practitioner position:** When likely questions lack answers, writers should find the responsible expert and obtain the information rather than omit the gap.
- **Candidate implication:** Track unresolved questions with owner, authority, evidence, risk, due date, escalation, blocking status, and permissible interim state.
- **Boundary:** An expert response may remain uncertain, disputed, confidential, jurisdiction-dependent, or operationally unsupported; contact alone does not make it publishable truth.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-unresolved-answer-workflow; verification-and-release-gate-required`.

### `LGW-C232` — unused legacy content needs disposition evidence, not automatic discard

- **Locator:** Chapter 7, Checklist 7-1, printed page 128; PDF page 158.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Material not needed or valued by visitors should be discarded from the topic.
- **Candidate implication:** Distinguish remove-from-projection, relocate, archive, retain-as-record, deprecate, redirect, or delete, with approval and rollback.
- **Boundary:** Absence from a question list is not evidence of no need, and deletion can break citations, search, support, compliance, minority access, or institutional memory.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-discard-boundary; governed-disposition-required`.

### `LGW-C233` — organization goals and participant outcomes must remain distinct in contest content

- **Locator:** Chapter 7, Case Study 7-1, printed pages 129–131; PDF pages 159–161.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The contest example separates the sponsor's desire for many valid entries from parents', teachers', and children's questions about eligibility, deadlines, prizes, rules, judging, certification, and submission.
- **Candidate implication:** Represent organizer outcome, participant task, eligibility, requirements, evaluation, consent, data, submission, confirmation, and remedy separately.
- **Boundary:** More entries or fewer invalid entries do not prove fairness, comprehension, accessibility, child safety, privacy, or participant benefit.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-multi-outcome-content-plan; child-and-fairness-governance-required`.

### `LGW-C234` — necessary information may not be user-initiated

- **Locator:** Chapter 7, Case Study 7-1, printed pages 130–131; PDF pages 160–161.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Writers must include facts people may not think to ask, such as an adult certification requirement, and place them before the dependent action.
- **Candidate implication:** Maintain an obligation inventory for prerequisites, attestations, risks, consent, rights, costs, exceptions, evidence, and consequences alongside observed questions.
- **Boundary:** Organization-declared necessity may be excessive, coercive, unlawful, or self-serving; each requirement needs authority, proportionality, and challenge paths.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-unasked-obligation-model; necessity-and-authority-required`.

### `LGW-C235` — child-facing participation content needs safeguards beyond readable rules

- **Locator:** Chapter 7, Case Study 7-1, printed pages 129–131; PDF pages 159–161.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The example anticipates parents, teachers, and older children and presents age, artwork, certification, prize, and submission rules.
- **Candidate implication:** Add child-appropriate explanation, guardian role, data minimization, image/art rights, contact boundaries, safeguarding, accessibility, fairness, retention, and deletion controls.
- **Boundary:** Assuming six-year-olds will rely on adults can exclude unsupported children or obscure whose consent and data rights govern participation.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-child-participation-gap; safeguarding-and-privacy-required`.

### `LGW-C236` — cutting should remove redundancy and self-focus without deleting propositions

- **Locator:** Chapter 7, Checklist 7-2, printed pages 132–134; PDF pages 162–164.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Writers should repeatedly remove flab and organization-centered wording while preserving essential facts and visitor answers.
- **Candidate implication:** Use proposition-level diffs to classify duplication, ceremony, claim, qualification, instruction, disclosure, evidence, and recovery before shortening.
- **Boundary:** Word-count reduction is not a quality measure; compression can remove negation, exception, actor, timing, uncertainty, empathy, or rationale.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-purposeful-reduction; semantic-preservation-required`.

### `LGW-C237` — read-aloud review is a diagnostic, not comprehension validation

- **Locator:** Chapter 7, Checklist 7-2, printed page 132; PDF page 162.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Reading aloud, including by representative visitors, can reveal awkward or unclear wording; recall of answers and key messages matters more than opinion.
- **Candidate implication:** Use read-aloud as one observation alongside paraphrase, task action, teach-back, delayed recall, error detection, confidence, and accessibility testing.
- **Boundary:** Oral fluency differs from silent, screen-reader, low-vision, translated, signed, cognitive, or stress-state use; recall alone does not prove correct action or informed consent.
- **Promotion status:** `candidate-read-aloud-diagnostic; multimethod-comprehension-validation-required`.

### `LGW-C238` — preference and comprehension measure different constructs

- **Locator:** Chapter 7, printed page 132; PDF page 162.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Asking what content said is presented as more useful than asking whether people liked it.
- **Candidate implication:** Separate preference, comprehension, findability, action success, trustworthiness, accessibility, burden, and downstream outcome measures.
- **Boundary:** A paraphrase can be confidently wrong, and affect or perceived dignity can be a legitimate outcome. No single measure validates the experience.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-measure-separation; no-single-comprehension-proxy`.

### `LGW-C239` — urgent healthcare content should prioritize triage and channel suitability

- **Locator:** Chapter 7, appointment example, printed pages 132–134; PDF pages 162–164.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Reorganizing appointment content around urgent versus later needs, hours, numbers, and locations can surface the anxious visitor's next action.
- **Candidate implication:** Model urgency, emergency exclusion, service hours, channel capacity, location, eligibility, wait expectation, accessible contact, fallback, and escalation before polishing appointment copy.
- **Boundary:** A historical speculative rewrite is not clinical or operational validation and could misroute emergencies or promise unavailable same-day care.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-urgency-first-service-content; clinical-and-operational-validation-required`.

### `LGW-C240` — a percentage reduction is descriptive, not evidence of improvement

- **Locator:** Chapter 7, appointment example, printed pages 133–134; PDF pages 163–164.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The proposed appointment rewrite contains roughly 40–48% fewer words and retains a sentence judged reassuring and explanatory.
- **Candidate implication:** Record word-count change only as a transformation metric and validate preserved meaning, reassurance, task success, risk, locale expansion, and service truth separately.
- **Boundary:** Shorter content can perform worse, and author judgment about reassurance is a hypothesis without participant or production evidence.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-word-count-boundary; outcome-validation-required`.

### `LGW-C241` — “bite, snack, meal” describes layered projections over one meaning

- **Locator:** Chapter 7, printed pages 134–136; PDF pages 164–166.
- **Documented practitioner position:** A label or headline can provide the bite, a key message or summary the snack, and the detailed article or report the meal.
- **Candidate implication:** Link teaser, summary, and detail to canonical propositions with coverage, qualification, source, state, locale, and version parity.
- **Boundary:** A catchy bite may overpromise, a snack may omit material context, and a meal may not be complete; tiers must not become funnel manipulation.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-layered-projection-model; semantic-and-expectation-parity-required`.

### `LGW-C242` — each layer must remain independently truthful

- **Locator:** Chapter 7, printed pages 134–136; PDF pages 164–166.
- **Documented practitioner position:** People may stop after the bite or snack, while others continue to the details.
- **Candidate implication:** Define the minimum truthful proposition and required qualifiers for every stopping point; disclose when detail changes interpretation.
- **Boundary:** Progressive detail cannot rely on everyone opening the next layer to discover costs, limitations, sponsorship, uncertainty, or risk.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-stopping-point-truthfulness; material-qualification-at-first-reliance-required`.

### `LGW-C243` — key-message-first is useful when the key message is correctly governed

- **Locator:** Chapter 7, printed pages 136–138; PDF pages 166–168.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The main conclusion should appear in the headline and be elaborated immediately because many readers decide relevance from the opening.
- **Candidate implication:** Identify key message, audience, evidence, authority, scope, uncertainty, consequence, and supporting order before assigning salience.
- **Boundary:** The organization's preferred message may not be the user's needed truth; context, warning, provenance, or uncertainty may need equal or prior prominence.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-key-message-priority; evidence-and-user-need-governance-required`.

### `LGW-C244` — inverted-pyramid order is contextual, not universally superior

- **Locator:** Chapter 7, printed pages 136–138; PDF pages 166–168.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Conclusion-first structure is contrasted with chronological or school-essay narrative and recommended for busy workplace and web readers.
- **Candidate implication:** Choose conclusion-first, sequence-first, comparison-first, evidence-first, or narrative order according to task, risk, genre, expertise, and desired reasoning.
- **Boundary:** Procedures, investigations, pedagogy, informed deliberation, suspense, legal reasoning, and trauma-sensitive communication may require another order.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-conclusion-first-option; genre-and-task-validation-required`.

### `LGW-C245` — scientific key messages require source and uncertainty, not only immediacy

- **Locator:** Chapter 7, printed pages 137–138; PDF pages 167–168.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Two climate-study openings illustrate that stating the finding and quantitative implication early is more informative than beginning with institutions and methods.
- **Candidate implication:** Lead with an accurately scoped finding while retaining source, date, study type, uncertainty, limitations, attribution, and access to methods.
- **Boundary:** A concise finding can overstate causality, certainty, novelty, or consensus; the historical comparison does not validate either article's scientific accuracy.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-research-summary-priority; evidence-and-uncertainty-required`.

### `LGW-C246` — F-pattern evidence cannot become a universal layout law

- **Locator:** Chapter 7, printed pages 138–139; PDF pages 168–169.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Historical eye-tracking and practitioner observation show some participants concentrating on top lines and line beginnings before scanning headings, lists, or keywords.
- **Candidate implication:** Support early orientation and scannable structure, then test actual layouts, tasks, populations, languages, and states.
- **Boundary:** Reading patterns depend on design, task, script direction, expertise, content, device, magnification, and study method; an aggregate heat map does not prescribe universal placement.
- **Promotion status:** `corroborating-historical-attention-pattern; no-universal-f-layout-rule`.

### `LGW-C247` — screen-reader navigation should not be reduced to “scanning with ears”

- **Locator:** Chapter 7, printed pages 138–139; PDF pages 168–169.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Blind visitors may navigate rapidly among headings, links, and paragraphs and decide from short spoken segments whether to continue.
- **Candidate implication:** Provide semantic headings, landmarks, descriptive links, concise accessible names, correct order, bypass, and reliable state announcements.
- **Boundary:** Blind and screen-reader users have diverse strategies, speech rates, cognition, devices, and expertise; the metaphor cannot substitute for current standards or testing.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-sequential-navigation-support; reject-homogeneous-user-assumption`.

### `LGW-C248` — layering should serve differing detail needs without hiding obligations

- **Locator:** Chapter 7, printed pages 140–142; PDF pages 170–172.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Overlays and progressive disclosure can preserve context while offering definitions, technical detail, or optional deeper information.
- **Candidate implication:** Type each layer as optional support, comparison, evidence, prerequisite, warning, disclosure, or action and govern whether it may begin collapsed.
- **Boundary:** Collapsed content can be undiscoverable, unsearched, unprinted, unlinked, or skipped by assistive technology; required facts must not depend on optional expansion.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-layer-purpose-model; material-content-visibility-required`.

### `LGW-C249` — overlays require a complete modal or nonmodal behavior contract

- **Locator:** Chapter 7, printed pages 140–141; PDF pages 170–171.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Small overlays can show traffic or flight details without navigating away from the underlying map or route.
- **Candidate implication:** Specify trigger role/name, relationship, focus entry/return, keyboard and pointer dismissal, escape, outside interaction, reading order, viewport fit, collision, scroll, zoom, and state persistence.
- **Boundary:** “Keeping context” visually may remove context sequentially or trap magnified and keyboard users. Historical rollovers and secondary windows are not current component guidance.
- **Promotion status:** `candidate-context-preserving-overlay; full-accessibility-behavior-contract-required`.

### `LGW-C250` — progressive disclosure must expose control and expanded state semantically

- **Locator:** Chapter 7, Case Study 7-2, printed pages 141–146; PDF pages 171–176.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Injured-worker information opens and closes within the current page so people can reveal phase and question details without losing the surrounding process.
- **Candidate implication:** Use real controls with accessible name, expanded state, controlled-region relation, keyboard operation, predictable insertion, announcement, deep-linking, print/search behavior, and no-script fallback.
- **Boundary:** The chapter's assertion that JavaScript Show/Hide is accessible is insufficient and time-bound; avoiding page reload does not prove focus, semantics, state, or assistive-technology support.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-progressive-disclosure-intent; historical-implementation-claim-unverified`.

### `LGW-C251` — high-consequence claims journeys need state and rights beyond content layers

- **Locator:** Chapter 7, Case Study 7-2, printed pages 142–145; PDF pages 172–175.
- **Documented practitioner position:** A phased injured-worker mini-site explains immediate care, filing, approval or rejection, return to work, closure, employer roles, and worker questions.
- **Candidate implication:** Model claim state, responsible party, eligibility, deadlines, evidence, payment timing, rights, privacy, retaliation concern, language, accommodation, appeal, help, and current status.
- **Boundary:** Clear pages cannot change a harmful or complex process, guarantee payment, or replace legal and case-specific advice; worker vulnerability makes omission especially consequential.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-high-consequence-process-content; legal-service-and-rights-validation-required`.

### `LGW-C252` — persona vividness can encode unsupported demographic stereotypes

- **Locator:** Chapter 7, Case Study 7-2, printed page 142; PDF page 172.
- **Documented practitioner position:** “Joe” is described by age, education, occupation, family status, hobbies, old computer, pain, skepticism, and limited computer use.
- **Candidate implication:** Retain only evidence-linked task, capability, environment, constraint, and risk attributes that change a design decision.
- **Boundary:** Education, occupation, device age, family status, and hobbies do not establish literacy, digital skill, trust, or behavior and can manufacture a deficit narrative.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-stereotype-risk; direct-capability-evidence-required`.

### `LGW-C253` — phase navigation must distinguish tabs, steps, branches, and status

- **Locator:** Chapter 7, Case Study 7-2, printed pages 143–145; PDF pages 173–175.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The example presents claim phases as tabs, including mutually exclusive approved and rejected paths, with one page per phase.
- **Candidate implication:** Separate navigable sections from required steps, completed status, current state, branching outcomes, unavailable phases, and user-controlled tabs using correct semantics.
- **Boundary:** Visual tab styling can falsely imply permission, completion, sequence, or live claim status; users may need direct access to any phase regardless of their case state.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-process-navigation-distinction; state-semantics-required`.

### `LGW-C254` — breaking dense prose should follow semantic structure

- **Locator:** Chapter 7, printed pages 146–149; PDF pages 176–179.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Headings, short sections, sentences, bullets, and tables can make information easier to discover than walls of paragraphs.
- **Candidate implication:** Convert prose into semantic headings, lists, tables, definitions, steps, conditions, and callouts according to relationships—not visual brevity alone.
- **Boundary:** Excessive fragmentation can destroy cohesion, hierarchy, qualification, and reading flow; bullets and tables require accessible structure and may localize poorly.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-semantic-density-reduction; no-fragmentation-default`.

### `LGW-C255` — conversion experiments do not establish user benefit or causal content mechanisms by themselves

- **Locator:** Chapter 7, Case Study 7-3, printed pages 147–149; PDF pages 177–179.
- **Documented practitioner position:** A historical mortgage-lead form experiment reported higher submission rates, with analysis attributing the significant difference to replacing introductory paragraphs with bullets.
- **Candidate implication:** Preserve assignment, variants, population, traffic source, exposure, sample, uncertainty, metric, harms, data use, follow-through, and replication before interpreting a content effect.
- **Boundary:** Submission is an organization outcome and may increase unwanted contact, sensitive-data disclosure, unsuitable loans, or regret. The summary cannot independently prove causality or welfare.
- **Promotion status:** `limited-historical-experiment; noncompensatory-user-outcomes-required`.

### `LGW-C256` — form introductions must disclose data routing and commercial consequences clearly

- **Locator:** Chapter 7, Case Study 7-3, printed pages 147–149; PDF pages 177–179.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The mortgage form explains that submitted information may produce quotes from several lenders and attempts to summarize the service in a short list.
- **Candidate implication:** State recipients, selection logic, purpose, legal basis or consent, contact channels, frequency, retention, credit impact, compensation, security, withdrawal, and alternatives before sensitive submission.
- **Boundary:** “Confidential,” “personalized,” “competitive,” speed, database size, or brevity can reassure without substantiation and cannot replace material privacy and financial disclosures.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-lead-form-disclosure; privacy-financial-and-consent-governance-required`.

### `LGW-C257` — social reuse requires contextual integrity across projections

- **Locator:** Chapter 7, printed page 149; PDF page 179.
- **Documented practitioner position:** A concise social “bite” can link to a site summary and then fuller detail.
- **Candidate implication:** Bind snippets to canonical source, audience, claim, qualification, date, locale, accessibility, campaign, consent, correction, expiry, and destination parity.
- **Boundary:** The cited platforms and 140-character constraint are dated; engagement and clicks do not validate truth, informed action, or appropriate data use.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-social-projection-lineage; dated-platform-guidance-rejected`.

### `LGW-C258` — Chapter 7 offers prioritization patterns, not a complete disclosure system

- **Locator:** Chapter 7, printed pages 125–149; PDF pages 155–179.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The chapter integrates relevance, question-led planning, reduction, layered detail, key-message-first order, progressive disclosure, semantic chunking, and social reuse.
- **Candidate implication:** Retain these as projection and review candidates within a system governing proposition priority, material disclosure, evidence, state, accessibility, locale, permissions, experimentation, and lifecycle.
- **Boundary:** The chapter's scanning, persona, eye-tracking, Show/Hide, scroll, SEO, conversion, and social-platform claims are historical and incomplete. Current technical verification remains unavailable because the Developer Index quota is exhausted.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-information-priority-foundation; modern-disclosure-state-and-evidence-model-required`.

### `LGW-C259` — pull and push describe initiation, not consent

- **Locator:** Interlude 2, printed pages 151–152; PDF pages 181–182.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Direct marketing initiates contact, while many site visits begin when people actively seek a product or answer.
- **Candidate implication:** Record who initiated the interaction, for what purpose, through which channel, and what promotional scope was actually requested or permitted.
- **Boundary:** A visit, search, click, follow, friendship, account, or purchase does not create blanket consent for targeting, profiling, email, social contact, or cross-channel tracking.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-interaction-initiation-distinction; no-initiation-as-consent`.

### `LGW-C260` — subscribed promotion remains a governed recurring relationship

- **Locator:** Interlude 2, printed page 151; PDF page 181.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Email specials, newsletters, and social follows are examples of organization-initiated messages after registration or connection.
- **Candidate implication:** Bind channel, content class, sender, identity, cadence, personalization, consent event, proof, expiry, preference, unsubscribe, and suppression across systems.
- **Boundary:** Registration may be bundled, coerced, stale, or ambiguous, and platform following does not necessarily authorize data enrichment or contact elsewhere.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-recurring-promotion-contract; granular-consent-and-exit-required`.

### `LGW-C261` — task satisfaction should precede optional promotion

- **Locator:** Interlude 2, printed pages 151–153; PDF pages 181–183.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Visitors focused on a self-initiated need often bypass marketing until at least part of that need is satisfied; promotion should join rather than obstruct the task.
- **Candidate implication:** Define a verified task-satisfaction threshold and keep promotion visually, semantically, and behaviorally subordinate, optional, and dismissible before that point.
- **Boundary:** Partial satisfaction may be ambiguous or reversible, and necessary services, crises, accessibility flows, children, debt, health, or financial decisions may prohibit promotion entirely.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-primary-task-before-promotion; protected-context-restrictions-required`.

### `LGW-C262` — self-focused marketing can break destination expectations

- **Locator:** Interlude 2, HughesNet example, printed pages 152–153; PDF pages 182–183.
- **Documented practitioner position:** A visitor seeking home plans and prices encounters dense organization-focused prose and weakly visible routes instead of the expected comparison.
- **Candidate implication:** Match route promise with destination content, surface material plan attributes, and defer company narrative unless it supports the decision.
- **Boundary:** The author's hypothetical visitor and critique do not establish actual user behavior, plan suitability, pricing truth, availability, or improvement.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-route-promise-content-parity; speculative-example-boundary`.

### `LGW-C263` — conversational pronouns do not establish customer-centered marketing

- **Locator:** Interlude 2, HughesNet example, printed pages 152–153; PDF pages 182–183.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The critique contrasts third-person subscriber language with “you,” “your,” and imperative phrasing on a plan page.
- **Candidate implication:** Evaluate whether content answers the person's decision and accurately states offer, cost, constraint, eligibility, and action rather than scoring pronouns.
- **Boundary:** Second person can intensify manipulation, surveillance, blame, or false personalization; grammar is not evidence of relevance, fairness, or service quality.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-pronoun-as-customer-focus-boundary; substantive-decision-support-required`.

### `LGW-C264` — plan comparison requires material-offer completeness

- **Locator:** Interlude 2, HughesNet example, printed page 153; PDF page 183.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The task-oriented page foregrounds plans, prices, brief use descriptions, details, ordering, and adjacent information.
- **Candidate implication:** Compare total cost, term, fees, taxes, limits, performance, availability, equipment, cancellation, renewal, support, accessibility, and substantiated suitability in a consistent structure.
- **Boundary:** Historical displayed prices, “fits your needs,” popularity, and performance language are not current or complete offer evidence.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-plan-comparison-structure; material-commercial-disclosure-required`.

### `LGW-C265` — a marketing moment is a hypothesis about relevance and timing

- **Locator:** Interlude 2, printed pages 153–154; PDF pages 183–184.
- **Documented practitioner position:** A marketing moment is presented as a natural follow-on or complement after the visitor's initial conversation has been partly satisfied.
- **Candidate implication:** Represent promotion purpose, triggering task/state, relevance evidence, sponsor, expected benefit, risk, frequency, expiry, eligibility, and suppression.
- **Boundary:** An organization cannot infer readiness, vulnerability, or consent solely from page state or behavior; “natural” relevance is contestable and can encode profiling.
- **Promotion status:** `candidate-contextual-promotion-timing; evidence-privacy-and-consent-required`.

### `LGW-C266` — cross-sell and upsell must remain distinguishable from required next steps

- **Locator:** Interlude 2, printed pages 153–154; PDF pages 183–184.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Related products or enhanced service can be offered after the primary need is addressed.
- **Candidate implication:** Label optional commercial recommendations, sponsorship, price difference, recurring commitment, relationship to the task, and equal continue/decline paths.
- **Boundary:** Bundling, default selection, salience imbalance, artificial scarcity, sunk-cost pressure, or hiding the original option can turn a recommendation into manipulation.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-optional-commercial-separation; refusal-parity-required`.

### `LGW-C267` — behavioral recommendations need provenance and privacy limits

- **Locator:** Interlude 2, Amazon example, printed page 154; PDF page 184.
- **Documented practitioner position:** “Customers who bought this also bought,” wish lists, shipping offers, and related products are praised as contextually relevant marketing.
- **Candidate implication:** Disclose recommendation basis, sponsorship, personalization, data sources, eligibility, freshness, diversity, safety constraints, and controls where material.
- **Boundary:** Purchase correlation does not establish suitability or public benefit and may reveal sensitive interests, reinforce bias, exploit children, or optimize seller margin.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-recommendation-system-boundary; provenance-privacy-and-safety-required`.

### `LGW-C268` — convenience features can reduce protective deliberation

- **Locator:** Interlude 2, Amazon example, printed page 154; PDF page 184.
- **Documented practitioner position:** One-click ordering, fast delivery, wish lists, and Prime are framed as timely ways to make buying easier.
- **Candidate implication:** Evaluate confirmation, undo, authentication, total cost, recurring membership, shipping promise, shared-device risk, accidental purchase, and cooling-off needs.
- **Boundary:** Fewer steps are not always better; convenience can conceal commitment, encourage impulsivity, or harm people needing budget, caregiver, or fraud safeguards.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-friction-reduction-boundary; protective-deliberation-and-recovery-required`.

### `LGW-C269` — urgency claims require operational truth and anti-pressure controls

- **Locator:** Interlude 2, Amazon example, printed page 154; PDF page 184.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Countdown and next-day-delivery messaging are treated as relevant to someone considering purchase.
- **Candidate implication:** Substantiate inventory, cutoff, timezone, destination, carrier, fees, confidence, update behavior, and expiry; prevent false or resetting urgency.
- **Boundary:** Accurate logistics can still pressure decisions, and high-consequence or vulnerable contexts may require time rather than acceleration.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-urgency-claim; substantiation-and-pressure-guardrails-required`.

### `LGW-C270` — a fact page should expose relevant next actions without manufacturing demand

- **Locator:** Interlude 2, credit-union example, printed pages 154–155; PDF pages 184–185.
- **Documented practitioner position:** A clean rate page may still strand visitors when it lacks a route to learn about or apply for a loan.
- **Candidate implication:** Offer clearly separated next actions such as compare, calculate, check eligibility, understand terms, seek advice, apply, contact, or exit.
- **Boundary:** Rates are financial claims requiring effective date, term, assumptions, fees, eligibility, risk, and comparison context; an application CTA must not eclipse deliberation or independent help.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-fact-to-action-continuity; financial-protection-required`.

### `LGW-C271` — continuation is a user option, not an engagement obligation

- **Locator:** Interlude 2, printed page 155; PDF page 185.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Sites should let visitors choose when they have enough and provide ways to continue rather than ending the conversation prematurely.
- **Candidate implication:** Support continue, save, share, contact, compare, return, finish, decline, leave, and delete/cancel where applicable, preserving state and autonomy.
- **Boundary:** “Never stop” can become endless engagement, obstruction, or refusal to recognize task completion. A clean exit is a successful outcome.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-user-controlled-continuation; successful-exit-parity-required`.

### `LGW-C272` — live chat is a service channel with operational and privacy obligations

- **Locator:** Interlude 2, printed page 155; PDF page 185.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Live chat is offered as one way to continue a visitor's interaction.
- **Candidate implication:** Specify availability, human/bot identity, capability, wait, transcript, data use, retention, security, accessibility, language, escalation, handoff, and closure.
- **Boundary:** A chat invitation can interrupt, track, misrepresent staffing, or collect sensitive data; it is not inherently assistance.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-chat-channel-contract; no-widget-as-service-proof`.

### `LGW-C273` — feedback and recommendations are separate participation and data flows

- **Locator:** Interlude 2, printed page 155; PDF page 185.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Comments, recommendations, and other feedback can extend engagement.
- **Candidate implication:** Define purpose, audience, identity visibility, moderation, consent, rights, reuse, retention, response expectations, abuse handling, and withdrawal for each contribution type.
- **Boundary:** Asking for input does not guarantee influence and may expose contributors, appropriate unpaid labor, or create moderation and safety burdens.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-feedback-flow-governance; participation-not-engagement-decoration`.

### `LGW-C274` — social engagement is not a neutral continuation channel

- **Locator:** Interlude 2, printed page 155; PDF page 185.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Social media is suggested as another place to continue the visitor relationship.
- **Candidate implication:** Disclose platform handoff, publicness, tracking, account requirement, moderation, records, accessibility, and a first-party alternative.
- **Boundary:** Third-party platforms may profile people, exclude nonmembers, expose sensitive interests, change terms, or make necessary support inaccessible.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-social-handoff-boundary; equivalent-first-party-path-required`.

### `LGW-C275` — contact information must align with capability and case continuity

- **Locator:** Interlude 2, printed page 155; PDF page 185.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Contact information should appear within relevant content as well as global navigation.
- **Candidate implication:** Present purpose-specific channel, hours, response expectation, cost, accessibility, language, emergency scope, identity requirements, case-reference continuity, and fallback.
- **Boundary:** Visibility without staffing, authority, privacy, or resolution capacity creates a false path and can increase harm in urgent or disputed situations.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-contextual-contact-path; operational-capability-required`.

### `LGW-C276` — Interlude 2 identifies timing but lacks a modern promotion-governance model

- **Locator:** Interlude 2, printed pages 151–155; PDF pages 181–185.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The interlude advises answering the visitor-initiated need, then offering relevant commercial or service continuations without stranding the journey.
- **Candidate implication:** Retain primary-task priority, route parity, relevant next action, and user-controlled continuation within a promotion model covering consent, privacy, sponsorship, recommendation provenance, protected contexts, pressure, frequency, measurement, and exit.
- **Boundary:** The historical examples normalize behavioral recommendations, one-click purchase, urgency, upsell, social engagement, and endless continuation without adequate rights or harm controls. Current external contracts remain unverified because the Developer Index quota is exhausted.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-timing-foundation; modern-consent-privacy-and-anti-manipulation-model-required`.

### `LGW-C277` — a headline is a portable destination promise

- **Locator:** Chapter 8, printed page 157; PDF page 187.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Online headlines often appear apart from their content as links, especially on small screens, so they must provide enough context to attract appropriate visitors and discourage wrong choices.
- **Candidate implication:** Specify headline intent, destination identity, scope, audience vocabulary, differentiator, expected content, and standalone contexts such as navigation, search, share, history, and notifications.
- **Boundary:** A headline cannot carry every qualification, and “enticing” can conflict with accurate expectation. Supporting metadata may be necessary before consequential selection.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-headline-as-destination-promise; expectation-parity-required`.

### `LGW-C278` — `<h1>`, document title, link text, and visual headline are related but distinct

- **Locator:** Chapter 8, printed pages 157–158; PDF pages 187–188.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The chapter distinguishes the visible first-level heading from the document `<title>` and says typography alone does not create heading semantics.
- **Candidate implication:** Model page title, primary heading, route label, link text, accessible name, social title, and search title separately with intentional equivalence or divergence.
- **Boundary:** One `<h1>` is not by itself proof of a coherent heading hierarchy, and current browser, framework, accessibility, and search behavior remains unverified.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-title-heading-role-separation; current-semantic-contract-required`.

### `LGW-C279` — headline wording should predict the actual content rather than internal meaning

- **Locator:** Chapter 8, printed page 157; PDF page 187.
- **Documented practitioner position:** A strategic-plan title using “roadmap” and “navigation” led pilots to expect road maps rather than organizational planning.
- **Candidate implication:** Test headline interpretation without surrounding context and compare predicted destination with actual scope and genre.
- **Boundary:** The summarized test lacks participant, task, sample, and alternative-title detail; a single ambiguity does not establish universal word bans.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-prediction-test; limited-historical-example`.

### `LGW-C280` — audience vocabulary needs a canonical concept bridge

- **Locator:** Chapter 8, printed pages 158–159; PDF pages 188–189.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Headlines should use words visitors recognize rather than generic document-section labels or organization vocabulary.
- **Candidate implication:** Link observed aliases and query terms to canonical concepts, genre, jurisdiction, and destination scope.
- **Boundary:** User words can be inaccurate, offensive, ambiguous, or fragmented across communities; they cannot silently replace precise domain terminology.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-audience-vocabulary-headlines; canonical-precision-required`.

### `LGW-C281` — generic document-structure labels lack destination specificity

- **Locator:** Chapter 8, printed page 158; PDF page 188.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Labels such as abstract, introduction, literature review, references, and appendices do not tell an external visitor what a linked section is about.
- **Candidate implication:** Combine genre or structural role with topical and task context when sections appear outside the parent work.
- **Boundary:** Within a clearly identified scholarly or legal work, conventional labels may be expected and useful; replacing them can impair citation or genre navigation.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-standalone-section-context; genre-convention-preserved`.

### `LGW-C282` — clarity should outrank wordplay when recognition is required

- **Locator:** Chapter 8, printed page 159; PDF page 189.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Cute or idiomatic headlines can fail link scanning and discovery because they do not reveal the topic.
- **Candidate implication:** Validate literal topic recognition first; allow voice or wordplay only when it preserves destination prediction and accessibility.
- **Boundary:** Literalness is not the only valid goal in editorial, cultural, entertainment, or persuasive contexts, and humor can serve belonging or memory.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-recognition-before-wordplay; context-sensitive-expression-preserved`.

### `LGW-C283` — historical traffic differences do not isolate headline causality

- **Locator:** Chapter 8, printed page 159; PDF page 189.
- **Documented practitioner position:** A clear survey-design title reportedly received 100 times the visitors of an idiomatic title in the same column series.
- **Candidate implication:** Treat traffic as hypothesis-generating and inspect topic demand, ranking, distribution, timing, links, audience, and measurement before attributing effect to wording.
- **Boundary:** Different articles are not a controlled headline experiment, and visits do not prove comprehension, relevance, or benefit.
- **Promotion status:** `limited-observational-traffic-example; no-causal-lift-claim`.

### `LGW-C284` — global readiness requires more than expanding acronyms and idioms

- **Locator:** Chapter 8, printed pages 159–160; PDF pages 189–190.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Local abbreviations, acronyms, and idioms may fail readers elsewhere, illustrated by “L.I.” for Long Island.
- **Candidate implication:** Localize headlines with concept, locale, script, direction, jurisdiction, date/number/unit, cultural, and search context; test transcreation rather than literal reuse.
- **Boundary:** “Global audience” is not one population, and spelling out a term does not resolve different laws, concepts, connotations, reading orders, or information needs.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-localization-awareness; locale-specific-transcreation-required`.

### `LGW-C285` — headline length must follow purpose and rendering, not an eight-word target

- **Locator:** Chapter 8, printed page 160; PDF page 190.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The chapter recommends roughly eight words based on historical click-through and link-selection reports.
- **Candidate implication:** Optimize for accurate recognition, distinction, qualification, localization, wrapping, truncation, speech, and component constraints rather than a fixed word count.
- **Boundary:** Word count varies across languages and scripts and says little about width, complexity, or meaning; the cited 2011 evidence lacks sufficient method here for a universal rule.
- **Promotion status:** `reject-eight-word-target; semantic-and-rendering-validation-required`.

### `LGW-C286` — click-through rate cannot define headline quality

- **Locator:** Chapter 8, printed page 160; PDF page 190.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Historical aggregate figures associate images, eight words, odd-number listicles, punctuation, and questions with higher click-through.
- **Candidate implication:** Evaluate correct-selection rate, wrong clicks, comprehension, task success, trust, satisfaction, accessibility, and downstream harm alongside any click measure.
- **Boundary:** Click optimization can reward curiosity gaps, sensationalism, dark patterns, or low-quality traffic; correlations may reflect content, placement, audience, and publisher differences.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-click-through-boundary; destination-quality-and-harm-measures-required`.

### `LGW-C287` — odd-number and punctuation formulas are not semantic guidance

- **Locator:** Chapter 8, printed page 160; PDF page 190.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The cited aggregate reports higher click-through for odd-number list titles and colon/dash subtitles.
- **Candidate implication:** Use enumeration and subtitles only when they truthfully represent content structure or add necessary context.
- **Boundary:** Formulaic headlines can misstate completeness, manufacture novelty, translate poorly, clutter assistive speech, and prioritize engagement over meaning.
- **Promotion status:** `reject-formulaic-click-patterns; structure-truth-required`.

### `LGW-C288` — statement headlines should state a verified and scoped proposition

- **Locator:** Chapter 8, printed page 161; PDF page 191.
- **Documented practitioner position:** A statement can put the key message first and prompt readers' questions.
- **Candidate implication:** Bind statement headlines to source, date, scope, certainty, actor, condition, and consequence, with essential qualifiers retained.
- **Boundary:** Declarative brevity can overstate predictions, scientific findings, policy status, or causal claims.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-statement-headline-option; proposition-verification-required`.

### `LGW-C289` — question headlines must reflect a genuine audience question without implying an answer

- **Locator:** Chapter 8, printed page 161; PDF page 191.
