- **Documented practitioner position:** Questions can let the visitor's concern lead when they resonate with what people ask.
- **Candidate implication:** Source question wording, identify answer type and scope, and avoid presupposition, fear, accusation, or teaser framing.
- **Boundary:** A question can manipulate uncertainty, imply a false premise, or conceal that the content lacks a direct answer.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-question-headline-option; evidence-and-answer-contract-required`.

### `LGW-C290` — call-to-action headlines must correspond to an available and appropriate action

- **Locator:** Chapter 8, printed page 161; PDF page 191.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Verb phrases can encourage readers to act.
- **Candidate implication:** Specify actor, action, object, eligibility, state, consequence, destination, reversibility, and authorization before using an action headline.
- **Boundary:** Imperatives can pressure, mislead, or imply capability that is unavailable, gated, unsafe, or unauthorized.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-action-headline-option; executable-and-ethical-action-required`.

### `LGW-C291` — headline form should follow semantic purpose rather than style preference

- **Locator:** Chapter 8, printed pages 161–162; PDF pages 191–192.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Statements, questions, and calls to action are offered as useful headline forms.
- **Candidate implication:** Type a headline as proposition, question, task/action, category, event, status, warning, or record title and validate the form against the content.
- **Boundary:** These forms are not exhaustive and may combine; inconsistent form can create false affordance or mistaken certainty.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-headline-semantic-typing; content-form-parity-required`.

### `LGW-C292` — capitalization is a locale- and system-dependent convention

- **Locator:** Chapter 8, printed pages 161–162; PDF pages 191–192.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Sentence capitalization is preferred over all caps or title case for readability, with typography carrying visual hierarchy.
- **Candidate implication:** Store semantic text separately from display transformations and define casing by locale, script, proper-name, acronym, brand, and accessibility requirements.
- **Boundary:** Sentence case is not defined identically across languages; CSS or design transformations can alter pronunciation, copying, search, and localization if applied carelessly.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-no-content-encoded-by-capitals; locale-aware-casing-required`.

### `LGW-C293` — styling cannot substitute for semantic heading structure

- **Locator:** Chapter 8, printed pages 158 and 162; PDF pages 188 and 192.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Bold, color, and size can indicate visual prominence, but first-level heading meaning requires semantic markup.
- **Candidate implication:** Align heading element, accessible hierarchy, visual token, reading order, focus target, anchor, and component API.
- **Boundary:** Correct markup can still be visually weak or hierarchically incoherent, while visual styles may be removed by user preferences; both layers require validation.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-semantic-visual-heading-alignment; neither-layer-sufficient-alone`.

### `LGW-C294` — bare nouns often need a differentiating proposition

- **Locator:** Chapter 8, printed pages 162–163; PDF pages 192–193.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Topic nouns may label broad sections but often lack enough specificity for article or information-page headlines; adding an action or phrase can clarify value.
- **Candidate implication:** Use label-plus-scope, outcome, audience, state, question, or action when sibling choices or standalone contexts require differentiation.
- **Boundary:** Added language can become promotional padding, and concise conventional nouns may be best in stable navigation or expert tools.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-label-plus-differentiator; contextual-economy-required`.

### `LGW-C295` — subtitles must add meaning rather than decorate a label

- **Locator:** Chapter 8, printed page 162; PDF page 192.
- **Documented practitioner position:** A topic label combined with a clarifying phrase may outperform the label alone.
- **Candidate implication:** Type subtitles as scope, result, audience, time, location, status, or action and test whether they reduce ambiguity.
- **Boundary:** A subtitle can repeat the headline, introduce a second promise, disappear in responsive views, or be omitted from accessible names and search displays.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-meaningful-subtitle; cross-context-presence-and-parity-required`.

### `LGW-C296` — supporting descriptions should carry decision-changing context

- **Locator:** Chapter 8, printed page 163; PDF page 193.
- **Documented practitioner position:** When a headline alone is insufficient, a brief description can help visitors choose among links.
- **Candidate implication:** Add descriptions for scope, eligibility, outcome, method, commitment, difference, or next step and keep them programmatically associated with the choice.
- **Boundary:** Descriptions may be truncated, visually separated, repetitious, promotional, or unavailable to assistive technology; material context cannot depend on fragile display.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-headline-description-pair; programmatic-and-responsive-association-required`.

### `LGW-C297` — Chapter 8 provides headline heuristics, not a complete naming system

- **Locator:** Chapter 8, printed pages 157–163; PDF pages 187–193.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The chapter combines audience vocabulary, clarity, global awareness, medium length, semantic forms, label-plus-context, and descriptions.
- **Candidate implication:** Retain prediction, standalone context, semantic typing, vocabulary, localization, and description principles within a naming system covering source, route, state, locale, accessibility, search, component, and testing.
- **Boundary:** The length, click-through, keyword, capitalization, and formula evidence is historical and incomplete; current technical contracts remain unverified because the Developer Index quota is exhausted.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-predictive-naming-foundation; modern-semantic-localization-and-search-model-required`.

### `LGW-C298` — headings support orientation, selection, flow, and manageable scope

- **Locator:** Chapter 9, printed pages 165–167; PDF pages 195–197.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Useful headings help people grasp the gist, locate one section, follow the sequence, understand each section's context, and manage how much information they address.
- **Candidate implication:** Define each heading's section scope, relation to siblings and parent, destination role, and contribution to the page outline.
- **Boundary:** Headings do not repair inaccurate, incomplete, inaccessible, or poorly sequenced content and can create false confidence in a fragmented structure.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-heading-navigation-functions; content-quality-independent`.

### `LGW-C299` — an outline is a diagnostic projection of content structure

- **Locator:** Chapter 9, printed pages 165 and 168–170; PDF pages 195 and 198–200.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Listing headings without body text reveals grouping, hierarchy, order, gaps, repetition, and whether the overall story serves visitors.
- **Candidate implication:** Generate and review a heading-only outline with source propositions, dependencies, user questions, obligations, and state transitions before final prose.
- **Boundary:** A coherent outline can still omit minority needs, exceptions, evidence, or operational constraints; visual coherence is not validation.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-heading-only-structure-review; evidence-coverage-required`.

### `LGW-C300` — heading semantics enable navigation beyond visual scanning

- **Locator:** Chapter 9, printed pages 165–166; PDF pages 195–196.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Properly marked headings allow assistive software to navigate by heading, while bold or large text alone does not expose the structure.
- **Candidate implication:** Implement semantic heading elements in logical order and test heading-list, landmark, reading, keyboard, voice, and magnification journeys.
- **Boundary:** Markup alone does not prove a useful hierarchy, and screen-reader users do not share one scanning strategy. Current technical contracts remain unverified.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-semantic-heading-navigation; current-assistive-validation-required`.

### `LGW-C301` — adding headings can reveal but cannot cure underlying content defects

- **Locator:** Chapter 9, printed pages 167–169; PDF pages 197–199.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Difficulty naming a section suggests mixed or unclear ideas; repeated headings suggest scattered content; both call for reorganization rather than periodic labels.
- **Candidate implication:** Treat heading difficulty and duplication as lint signals for proposition overlap, mixed purpose, missing hierarchy, or inappropriate grain.
- **Boundary:** Some repeated labels are legitimate in comparison, repeated workflows, data tables, or templated states; diagnostics require context.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-heading-as-structural-lint; no-automatic-rewrite`.

### `LGW-C302` — health guidance examples require current clinical authority

- **Locator:** Chapter 9, exercise example, printed pages 166–167; PDF pages 196–197.
- **Documented practitioner position:** An exercise article is reformatted with action-oriented headings while retaining its original advice.
- **Candidate implication:** Separate structural demonstration from clinical content and require current source, population, contraindication, urgency, accessibility, and review for any health guidance.
- **Boundary:** The example includes generalized medical and exercise claims; headings and conversational tone do not make them safe or current.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-health-example-boundary; no-medical-content-reuse`.

### `LGW-C303` — outline order should represent dependencies, not merely a plausible conversation

- **Locator:** Chapter 9, printed pages 168–170; PDF pages 198–200.
- **Documented practitioner position:** An ordered heading list can begin with user questions and be transformed into statements, action phrases, or labels according to the material.
- **Candidate implication:** Order sections by prerequisite, decision, consequence, sequence, urgency, rights, evidence, and representative task use.
- **Boundary:** A writer-generated outline is a hypothesis; several valid paths may coexist, and safety or legal precedence may override conversational order.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-outline-order-candidate; dependency-and-user-validation-required`.

### `LGW-C304` — heading form should reflect the section's semantic role

- **Locator:** Chapter 9, printed pages 169–178; PDF pages 199–208.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Questions can represent visitor turns, statements can expose key messages, verb phrases can name tasks, and nouns can label categories.
- **Candidate implication:** Type headings as question, proposition, action, activity, category, status, warning, evidence, or exception and validate body-section parity.
- **Boundary:** The categories overlap and do not determine HTML level, component role, or visual style; a heading must not impersonate a control.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-section-heading-typing; semantics-level-and-affordance-separated`.

### `LGW-C305` — Q&A style should integrate answers rather than create a duplicate FAQ silo

- **Locator:** Chapter 9, printed pages 170–172; PDF pages 200–202.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Questions can structure many content genres, while a separate FAQ may signal that the main topic page fails to answer people's needs.
- **Candidate implication:** Map questions to canonical answers and surface them in the relevant task/topic context; avoid independently maintained duplicate answers.
- **Boundary:** A cross-topic FAQ, support triage, policy summary, or emerging-issue page may have a distinct purpose; consolidation requires evidence and redirects.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-question-in-context; no-duplicate-answer-silos`.

### `LGW-C306` — question headings need evidence and direct answer parity

- **Locator:** Chapter 9, printed pages 170–176; PDF pages 200–206.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Effective question headings use what visitors actually ask and are followed by content that answers them.
- **Candidate implication:** Bind each question to source evidence, intent, answer proposition, scope, authority, locale, and follow-up.
- **Boundary:** Internally written questions can launder organization messages into apparent user voice; the answer may be partial, disputed, or conditional.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-question-answer-contract; provenance-and-completeness-required`.

### `LGW-C307` — vaccine headings require current public-health evidence and risk communication

- **Locator:** Chapter 9, vaccination example, printed pages 170–172; PDF pages 200–202.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The chapter critiques and proposes question, statement, and action headings for an immunization article.
- **Candidate implication:** Treat the example only as structure; current public-health content requires authoritative evidence, date, population, schedule, contraindications, benefits, risks, uncertainty, and clinical help.
- **Boundary:** Historical copy, analogies, statistics, and proposed revisions are not medical guidance and cannot be promoted into product content.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-public-health-example-boundary; no-clinical-reuse`.

### `LGW-C308` — jurisdiction-routing content must begin with the person's problem

- **Locator:** Chapter 9, Case Study 9-1, printed pages 172–174; PDF pages 202–204.
- **Documented practitioner position:** A bank-complaint page improved by foregrounding the filing task and regulator check rather than asking visitors to understand the agency first.
- **Candidate implication:** Start with complaint goal, institution type, jurisdiction determination, required evidence, channel, expected process, assistance, and correct-regulator handoff.
- **Boundary:** The historical page and agencies may have changed; clear task framing does not establish current jurisdiction, deadlines, appeal, accessibility, or case outcomes.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-problem-first-regulatory-routing; current-authority-verification-required`.

### `LGW-C309` — wrong-authority recovery is part of the answer

- **Locator:** Chapter 9, Case Study 9-1, printed pages 172–174; PDF pages 202–204.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Many complainants do not know how their bank is chartered, so the service must help identify the correct regulator rather than merely define the agency.
- **Candidate implication:** Provide a verified routing tool or handoff with institution identity, jurisdiction, uncertainty, external destination, context transfer, and return/recovery.
- **Boundary:** Requiring users to self-classify complex authority can deny remedy; a generic “appropriate regulator” reference without a link is not an operational path.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-authority-resolution-and-handoff; verified-recovery-required`.

### `LGW-C310` — first/second-person patterns are optional discourse choices, not universal rules

- **Locator:** Chapter 9, printed pages 174–175; PDF pages 204–205.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Q&A may use “I” for the visitor's question, “you” in the answer, and “we” for the organization.
- **Candidate implication:** Choose pronouns by actor clarity, voice, locale, formality, agency, responsibility, and risk; name the actor when pronouns would be ambiguous.
- **Boundary:** Invented “I” can falsely attribute thoughts, “you” can blame or overpersonalize, and “we” can obscure the responsible institution or system.
- **Promotion status:** `candidate-q-and-a-pronoun-pattern; actor-clarity-and-localization-required`.

### `LGW-C311` — concise questions must retain distinguishing context

- **Locator:** Chapter 9, printed pages 175–176; PDF pages 205–206.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Question headings should be short enough to scan and should not carry multiple questions or answer content.
- **Candidate implication:** Minimize repeated framing while preserving object, state, audience, condition, time, and distinction from sibling headings.
- **Boundary:** Shortness can make headings ambiguous outside their section, in heading lists, search, deep links, or translated speech.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-concise-distinct-question; standalone-context-required`.

### `LGW-C312` — keyword prefixes can disambiguate but also create repetitive noise

- **Locator:** Chapter 9, printed page 176; PDF page 206.
- **Documented practitioner position:** A topical keyword before a question may help rapid visual or spoken scanning, but repeating the same keyword in every heading harms scanability.
- **Candidate implication:** Add a differentiating concept only when parent context is unavailable or sibling ambiguity exists; test heading-list and search contexts.
- **Boundary:** Search-engine benefit is dated and unverified; repetitive prefixes increase speech burden, localization length, and cognitive noise.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-contextual-keyword-prefix; reject-repetitive-keyword-insertion`.

### `LGW-C313` — statement headings are claims that require substantiation

- **Locator:** Chapter 9, printed pages 176–177; PDF pages 206–207.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Short statements can expose each section's key message.
- **Candidate implication:** Bind statement heading to the body proposition, evidence, scope, certainty, date, source, and exceptions.
- **Boundary:** Visual salience amplifies unsupported promises; a heading such as a privacy or security assurance cannot be validated by prose alone.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-statement-heading-claim; substantiation-required`.

### `LGW-C314` — privacy headings must not convert aspiration into assurance

- **Locator:** Chapter 9, privacy example, printed page 177; PDF page 207.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Headings such as not selling, keeping information confidential, and protecting information are praised as clear key-message statements.
- **Candidate implication:** Verify every privacy statement against actual collection, purpose, sharing, sale definitions, processors, access, security, retention, rights, incidents, and jurisdiction.
- **Boundary:** “Confidential,” “protect,” “need to perform jobs,” and “do not sell” are incomplete or legally variable claims and can create false trust.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-privacy-claim-boundary; policy-runtime-and-legal-verification-required`.

### `LGW-C315` — imperatives and gerunds distinguish direct action from activity topics

- **Locator:** Chapter 9, printed pages 177–178 and summary continuation PDF page 217.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Repeated “How do I” headings can become imperatives for direct action or gerunds for activities, improving differentiation.
- **Candidate implication:** Use imperatives only for executable user actions and activity labels for explanatory processes; preserve actor, object, state, and result.
- **Boundary:** A gerund may still resemble a link or status, and an imperative can pressure or falsely promise functionality.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-action-activity-heading-distinction; affordance-parity-required`.

### `LGW-C316` — noun labels work when a stable taxonomy supplies the missing relation

- **Locator:** Chapter 9, printed pages 178–181; PDF pages 208–211.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Nouns can work as alphabetical topic labels on a pathway page when visitors seek the same term, but often fail to express sequence or why the section matters.
- **Candidate implication:** Use noun headings for recognized entities or categories with clear parent context; add action, state, consequence, or question when relation matters.
- **Boundary:** Expert and public vocabularies differ, and an observed gaze path from one historical participant does not validate a taxonomy.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-context-bound-noun-labels; taxonomy-evidence-required`.

### `LGW-C317` — action-based restructuring can expose mixed sections and buried exceptions

- **Locator:** Chapter 9, Case Study 9-2, printed pages 179–181; PDF pages 209–211.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Rewriting order-tracking noun headings as actions reveals that one paragraph covers several events and that shared-email gift risk deserves earlier prominence.
- **Candidate implication:** Split operational content by event and user goal; promote exceptions, notification timing, privacy/shared-device risks, and unavailable changes at the decision point.
- **Boundary:** The proposed revision is not tested and the underlying retailer behavior may be outdated; restructuring must follow current order states and policies.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-event-and-action-restructure; production-state-validation-required`.

### `LGW-C318` — notification content must account for shared access and surprise disclosure

- **Locator:** Chapter 9, Case Study 9-2, printed pages 179–180; PDF pages 209–210.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Shipping email may reveal gift contents to someone sharing an address, so the warning should be prominent rather than buried under a generic heading.
- **Candidate implication:** Model shared email/device/account risk, notification contents, preview text, sender, timing, opt-out, alternate contact, redaction, and consequence.
- **Boundary:** Advising the purchaser to warn the recipient transfers privacy burden and may be impossible; safer product and notification design may be required.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-shared-notification-risk; privacy-by-design-required`.

### `LGW-C319` — parallel heading syntax creates a pattern, not semantic equivalence

- **Locator:** Chapter 9, printed page 181; PDF page 211.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Headings at one level are easier to scan when they share grammatical structure, and styles may change between levels.
- **Candidate implication:** Use parallel syntax for comparable sibling roles while verifying that content scope and hierarchy are actually comparable.
- **Boundary:** Forced parallelism can flatten meaningful differences, distort terminology, or create monotonous and repetitive speech.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-sibling-parallelism; semantic-comparability-required`.

### `LGW-C320` — breaking a heading pattern should signal a typed exception

- **Locator:** Chapter 9, printed pages 180–182; PDF pages 210–212.
- **Documented practitioner position:** A deliberately different heading can make an aside, exception, or warning stand out from a sequence.
- **Candidate implication:** Type exception, warning, aside, status, or interruption semantically and provide nonvisual equivalence rather than relying only on grammar or style deviation.
- **Boundary:** Unexpected inconsistency can look like error or hierarchy drift and may disappear in assistive, localized, or plain-text projections.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-purposeful-pattern-break; typed-nonvisual-exception-required`.

### `LGW-C321` — semantic depth should follow content nesting, not an arbitrary level cap

- **Locator:** Chapter 9, printed pages 181–182; PDF pages 211–212.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Typical pages should use one H1, several H2s, rare H3s, and avoid deeper levels by splitting content.
- **Candidate implication:** Use the minimum coherent hierarchy but preserve real nesting; split only when route, context, task, performance, and continuity remain sound.
- **Boundary:** “Few levels” and one-H1 guidance are contextual, and deep technical, legal, educational, or reference content may legitimately need more structure.
- **Promotion status:** `candidate-shallow-hierarchy-preference; semantic-depth-governs`.

### `LGW-C322` — reusable components need contextual heading-level ownership

- **Locator:** Chapter 9, printed pages 181–184; PDF pages 211–214.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Heading levels should communicate hierarchy and be visibly distinguishable.
- **Candidate implication:** Let the composing page or section supply semantic level, enforce valid nesting, and keep component typography decoupled from hard-coded heading rank.
- **Boundary:** A reusable card, modal, accordion, or embedded document can appear at different depths; fixed levels create skipped or duplicated hierarchy.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-contextual-heading-level-api; current-framework-validation-required`.

### `LGW-C323` — mobile presentation must not erase semantic hierarchy

- **Locator:** Chapter 9, printed page 182; PDF page 212.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Mobile is used to argue for shorter pieces and fewer heading levels.
- **Candidate implication:** Preserve canonical section relations across viewport and input/output modes while adapting visual density and navigation.
- **Boundary:** Small screens do not need less meaning or shallower domains; splitting can increase latency, disorientation, and lost context.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-mobile-shallowness-boundary; cross-viewport-semantic-parity-required`.

### `LGW-C324` — heading and link affordances must be distinguishable without color convention alone

- **Locator:** Chapter 9, printed pages 182–183; PDF pages 212–213.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Headings should not use the site's link color or underlining because visitors may try to activate them.
- **Candidate implication:** Define link and heading affordances through semantics, state, typography, decoration, cursor/focus, and interaction—not a universal blue convention.
- **Boundary:** Link appearance varies by product, user style, forced colors, theme, and platform; color and underline rules from 2012 are not universal.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-heading-link-affordance-separation; no-blue-link-universal`.

### `LGW-C325` — visual heading hierarchy must survive user preferences and localization

- **Locator:** Chapter 9, printed pages 182–184; PDF pages 212–214.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Size, spacing, placement, weight, and color can distinguish heading levels, with headings kept close to the content they introduce.
- **Candidate implication:** Test hierarchy across zoom, text spacing, reflow, forced colors, themes, font substitution, long translations, RTL, vertical scripts, and user CSS.
- **Boundary:** Three-point, 150%, 125%, left-alignment, and rule-placement suggestions are contextual heuristics, not universal tokens or standards.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-visual-semantic-hierarchy; reject-fixed-typographic-heuristics`.

### `LGW-C326` — resizing must preserve heading relationships and usable proportion

- **Locator:** Chapter 9, printed page 184; PDF page 214.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Headings and body text should enlarge together so low-vision users do not receive readable paragraphs with unreadable labels.
- **Candidate implication:** Validate browser zoom, text-only zoom, OS scaling, font overrides, reflow, clipping, overlays, anchors, and complete tasks at supported extremes.
- **Boundary:** Proportional scaling alone may produce headings that dominate small viewports or still fail minimum readability; responsive typography must preserve relation, not a fixed ratio.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-coherent-text-resizing; runtime-reflow-validation-required`.

### `LGW-C327` — a same-page contents list can support overview and direct section access

- **Locator:** Chapter 9, printed pages 184–185; PDF pages 214–215.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Listing section headings as links near the top can show page coverage and let visitors jump directly to one section.
- **Candidate implication:** Generate in-page navigation from stable section identities with current-location feedback, meaningful labels, and parity with the document outline.
- **Boundary:** A contents list can duplicate navigation, overwhelm short pages, become stale, or obscure required introductory context.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-in-page-overview-and-jump; applicability-and-sync-required`.

### `LGW-C328` — anchor navigation requires more than matching an ID

- **Locator:** Chapter 9, printed pages 184–185; PDF pages 214–215.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Same-page links take visitors from a list to the corresponding heading without leaving the page.
- **Candidate implication:** Specify unique stable fragment, target focus, heading semantics, URL/history, back behavior, sticky-header offset, motion preference, announcement, deep linking, and no-script behavior.
- **Boundary:** Scrolling visually to a target may leave keyboard or assistive focus behind; dynamic content and duplicate IDs can break the contract.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-anchor-navigation-intent; focus-history-and-offset-contract-required`.

### `LGW-C329` — same-page versus off-page placement expectations are contextual

- **Locator:** Chapter 9, printed page 185; PDF page 215.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The chapter recommends same-page links directly under the headline, avoiding off-page links there and reserving left navigation for other topics.
- **Candidate implication:** Distinguish same-page, sibling-page, external, download, action, and disclosure destinations through grouping, labels, semantics, and design-system conventions.
- **Boundary:** Responsive layouts, documentation sites, apps, sidebars, sticky tables of contents, and mixed navigation invalidate universal top/left placement rules.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-destination-type-distinction; reject-universal-placement-rule`.

### `LGW-C330` — persona-channeling is a heuristic review, not user evaluation

- **Locator:** Chapter 9, printed page 186 and summary continuation PDF page 217.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Writers can read only the headline and headings “as” a persona to judge standalone meaning, flow, gist, and section findability before usability testing.
- **Candidate implication:** Use heading-only expert review with evidence maps and explicit reviewer assumptions, then test representative users and access modes.
- **Boundary:** A writer cannot become a persona or participant; a fictional perspective cannot validate comprehension, terminology, disability experience, or population coverage.
- **Promotion status:** `candidate-heading-only-expert-review; direct-user-validation-required`.

### `LGW-C331` — Chapter 9 offers structural writing guidance, not a complete document-outline runtime

- **Locator:** Chapter 9, printed pages 165–186/PDF pages 195–216 and unnumbered summary continuation PDF page 217.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The chapter integrates outlining, question/statement/action/category forms, vocabulary, parallelism, hierarchy, visual distinction, in-page navigation, and review.
- **Candidate implication:** Retain these within a document-structure model covering semantic section identity, contextual heading rank, component composition, locale, responsive presentation, anchors, focus/history, search, print, and evaluation.
- **Boundary:** Medical, public-health, privacy, SEO, level-count, typography, placement, and assistive-technology claims are historical or incomplete. Current external contracts remain unverified because the Developer Index quota is exhausted.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-heading-structure-foundation; modern-document-outline-and-runtime-contract-required`.

### `LGW-C332` — a press release is now a persistent public information object

- **Locator:** Interlude 3, printed pages 189–190; PDF pages 219–220.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Releases originally aimed at same-day media coverage now appear on organization sites, search, links, and third-party copies for reporters and the public over a much longer life.
- **Candidate implication:** Model release identity, public purpose, media purpose, source, publication state, canonical location, audience, retention, archive, syndication, and correction lifecycle.
- **Boundary:** “Everyone” and “forever” are rhetorical overstatements; access, indexing, retention, legal duty, platform persistence, and audience vary.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-persistent-publication-object; lifecycle-and-audience-specificity-required`.

### `LGW-C333` — external copies make correction propagation a first-class requirement

- **Locator:** Interlude 3, printed page 190; PDF page 220.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Releases can be copied or quoted on other sites and remain available even after the source archives or removes them.
- **Candidate implication:** Publish stable canonical identity, version, correction/retraction notice, supersession link, machine-readable update, syndication inventory, and outreach procedure.
- **Boundary:** The publisher cannot guarantee downstream correction or deletion; removal at source may worsen orphaned copies unless status remains resolvable.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-distributed-copy-risk; correction-propagation-and-tombstone-required`.

### `LGW-C334` — archive policy must distinguish preservation from current authority

- **Locator:** Interlude 3, printed page 190; PDF page 220.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Teams should decide whether releases remain indefinitely, move to an archive, or leave the site after a period.
- **Candidate implication:** Define retention basis, archive state, current/obsolete status, effective period, legal hold, citation persistence, search treatment, redirect, and removal authority.
- **Boundary:** Age alone does not determine usefulness or deletion; old releases may be records, evidence, misinformation risks, or still-current basic information.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-release-retention-governance; no-age-only-disposition`.

### `LGW-C335` — SEO attention is not a publication-quality outcome

- **Locator:** Interlude 3, printed page 190; PDF page 220.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Incoming links and social curation are described as beneficial attention that can improve search visibility.
- **Candidate implication:** Treat discovery as one bounded outcome alongside accuracy, correct audience, comprehension, source use, correction reach, accessibility, and public impact.
- **Boundary:** Links, ranking, traffic, and amplification can spread false, stale, harmful, or promotional claims and do not validate public benefit.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-seo-amplification-boundary; quality-and-harm-measures-required`.

### `LGW-C336` — releases may function as summaries that must link to primary evidence

- **Locator:** Interlude 3, Story 1, printed pages 190–191; PDF pages 220–221.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Some researchers first use a release to understand key findings and decide whether to read the full report.
- **Candidate implication:** Type a release summary as a derivative projection with direct primary-source link, citation, authors, date, methods, scope, uncertainty, limitations, funding, and corrections.
- **Boundary:** A promotional summary can overstate a study and is not a substitute for the report; one historical test scenario does not establish universal research behavior.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-release-as-summary; primary-evidence-lineage-required`.

### `LGW-C337` — releases mistaken for fact sheets require explicit genre and currency

- **Locator:** Interlude 3, Story 2, printed page 191; PDF page 221.
- **Documented practitioner position:** General-public searchers selected old releases as topic information, did not recognize the genre, and expected ordinary fact-sheet structure.
- **Candidate implication:** Display content type, publication date/year, modified/corrected date, historical status, current guidance link, source organization, and intended scope prominently.
- **Boundary:** Matching site templates can further obscure genre; a release must not impersonate evergreen or authoritative current guidance.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-genre-and-currency-disclosure; no-release-as-evergreen-by-default`.

### `LGW-C338` — contact identity must distinguish spokesperson, author, researcher, and service owner

- **Locator:** Interlude 3, Story 2, printed page 191; PDF page 221.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Participants assumed an unnamed-role contact was the researcher rather than a media-relations specialist.
- **Candidate implication:** Type every named contact by role, affiliation, department, purpose, availability, channel, authority, and privacy; link to verified profiles where appropriate.
- **Boundary:** Publishing personal contact details can create harassment or privacy risk, and a media contact may not answer public, participant, support, or subject-matter questions.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-contact-role-clarity; purpose-and-safety-required`.

### `LGW-C339` — old releases can conceal the only available topic content

- **Locator:** Interlude 3, Story 3, printed page 191; PDF page 221.
- **Documented practitioner position:** A content inventory found that much of an organization's topic information existed only in old releases with poor web structure.
- **Candidate implication:** Inventory releases by underlying proposition, current authority, duplication, evidence, topic owner, update need, and candidate migration into canonical content.
- **Boundary:** Extracting current content must not erase the historical record or silently change what was claimed at publication time.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-release-proposition-inventory; canonicalization-with-record-preservation-required`.

### `LGW-C340` — a newsroom may serve the public more than working reporters

- **Locator:** Interlude 3, Story 4, printed page 192; PDF page 222.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Reporters in one usability test preferred calling known contacts, suggesting site news might primarily serve other visitors.
- **Candidate implication:** Research reporter, editor, analyst, researcher, investor, employee, affected community, and public tasks separately; provide role-appropriate paths.
- **Boundary:** A few historical reporters do not establish newsroom audience priority, and direct calls may reflect relationships unavailable to others.
- **Promotion status:** `candidate-multiple-newsroom-audiences; no-public-or-reporter-default`.

### `LGW-C341` — public-facing releases should use web information structure

- **Locator:** Interlude 3, printed pages 192–193; PDF pages 222–223.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Releases should use full dates, headings, short sections, lists, media, relevant links, site templates, and clearly identified contacts rather than paper-document conventions.
- **Candidate implication:** Specify release schema for title, dek/summary, date/time/zone, source, body sections, quotations, claims, evidence links, media, contacts, related content, and status.
- **Boundary:** Format cannot compensate for inaccurate claims, missing disclosures, inaccessible media, embargo breaches, or absent correction policy.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-web-release-structure; editorial-governance-required`.

### `LGW-C342` — publication dates need complete temporal semantics

- **Locator:** Interlude 3, printed pages 191–193; PDF pages 221–223.
- **Documented practitioner position:** A full date including year and unambiguous month matters because releases persist.
- **Candidate implication:** Store publication, modification, correction, embargo, event, effective, and expiry timestamps with timezone and locale-specific display.
- **Boundary:** A visible date alone does not reveal what changed, whether guidance remains current, or which jurisdictional time controls.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-temporal-metadata; multi-date-state-model-required`.

### `LGW-C343` — links should express typed evidence and relationship

- **Locator:** Interlude 3, printed pages 191–193; PDF pages 221–223.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Releases should link to full reports, relevant pages, people, and further information.
- **Candidate implication:** Type links as primary evidence, dataset, methods, person, organization, background, related release, correction, media asset, or action with destination verification.
- **Boundary:** Related links can be promotional, stale, paywalled, inaccessible, permission-limited, or circular and do not themselves prove a claim.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-typed-release-links; destination-and-authority-verification-required`.

### `LGW-C344` — quotations need speaker authority and quotation provenance

- **Locator:** Interlude 3, NSF example, printed page 193; PDF page 223.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The example uses named researcher quotations and links to relevant people and research.
- **Candidate implication:** Record exact speaker, role, affiliation, approval, source event/transcript, date, editing, translation, context, and permitted use for every quotation.
- **Boundary:** A quote can be promotional, selectively edited, outdated, translated inaccurately, or attributed to someone without authority over the claim.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-quotation-lineage; speaker-context-and-rights-required`.

### `LGW-C345` — visual news assets require evidentiary and rights metadata

- **Locator:** Interlude 3, printed page 193; PDF page 223.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Images, video, and audio can make news more engaging and reduce text density.
- **Candidate implication:** For each asset, record purpose, subject, source, creator, capture date, edit, caption, credit, rights/license, consent, authenticity, accessibility alternative, transcript, and download variants.
- **Boundary:** Visuals can sensationalize, fabricate context, expose people, violate rights, or misrepresent evidence; decoration is not inherently informative.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-news-asset-governance; rights-authenticity-and-access-required`.

### `LGW-C346` — social and mobile previews are lossy projections, not source replacements

- **Locator:** Interlude 3, printed page 194; PDF page 224.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Headline, opening sentence, and image should be planned to work when shared on mobile and social platforms.
- **Candidate implication:** Generate preview title, summary, image, alternative text, source, date, canonical URL, and locale from governed fields; validate cropping, truncation, cache, and correction propagation.
- **Boundary:** The cited platforms and character limits are dated; third-party previews can strip qualification, retain stale caches, or add tracking.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-preview-projection-model; semantic-loss-and-cache-controls-required`.

### `LGW-C347` — third-party curation can improve framing while severing source context

- **Locator:** Interlude 3, Figure Interlude 3-2, printed page 194; PDF page 224.
- **Documented practitioner position:** A curator's clearer headline and summary may outperform the source headline when sharing research news.
- **Candidate implication:** Monitor authorized and influential derivative framing, preserve canonical attribution, and publish correction-friendly share metadata without surrendering source truth.
- **Boundary:** Curators may oversimplify, sensationalize, alter causality, or omit limitations; popularity does not grant editorial authority.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-curated-framing-boundary; canonical-source-and-correction-required`.

### `LGW-C348` — sensitive research news needs participant and population protections

- **Locator:** Interlude 3, social autism example, printed page 194; PDF page 224.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The example presents early autism prediction research in a headline, social summary, and article preview.
- **Candidate implication:** Require current scientific review, population scope, uncertainty, nonstigmatizing language, participant/community input, clinical limits, privacy, and help context for sensitive health or identity research.
- **Boundary:** Historical headlines about diagnosis, treatment, infants, and autism are not current medical evidence and may stigmatize or overpromise.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-sensitive-research-communication; no-medical-reuse`.

### `LGW-C349` — newsroom metadata must support machine distribution without overclaiming

- **Locator:** Interlude 3, printed pages 189–194; PDF pages 219–224.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Search, syndication, social links, and other sites distribute releases beyond the original newsroom.
- **Candidate implication:** Govern canonical URL, content type, source organization, dates, author/contact roles, language, image, summary, keywords/topics, license, and status across feeds and structured metadata.
- **Boundary:** Metadata can amplify misclassification or false freshness; current schemas and provider contracts remain unverified.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-machine-distribution-metadata; current-schema-validation-required`.

### `LGW-C350` — embargo and publication state are missing from the chapter's lifecycle

- **Locator:** Interlude 3, printed pages 189–195; PDF pages 219–225.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The interlude contrasts short historical release timing with persistent web publication but does not develop prepublication states.
- **Candidate implication:** Model draft, review, approved, embargoed, scheduled, published, corrected, superseded, retracted, archived, and removed states with role-based access and audit.
- **Boundary:** Accidental indexing, cached previews, feeds, timezone errors, or permission leaks can break embargoes or expose sensitive information.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-publication-state-gap; controlled-release-workflow-required`.

### `LGW-C351` — preference evidence does not validate the redesigned release

- **Locator:** Interlude 3, usability-test continuation, printed page 195; PDF page 225.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Fifteen of 16 participants reportedly preferred a reformatted release with headings and links to a different unchanged release and commented that it was easier to scan.
- **Candidate implication:** Treat the result as formative evidence and measure matched-topic comprehension, fact accuracy, source use, task success, accessibility, print use, and delayed recall in controlled comparisons.
- **Boundary:** The releases differed by topic, participants saw them in different scenarios, preference was prompted at the end, and no inferential detail supports a causal or population claim.
- **Promotion status:** `limited-formative-preference-evidence; controlled-outcome-testing-required`.

### `LGW-C352` — print preference is a legitimate mode, not an exception to dismiss

- **Locator:** Interlude 3, usability-test continuation, printed page 195; PDF page 225.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The one participant who preferred the traditional release said they printed desired web pages.
- **Candidate implication:** Preserve a coherent, accessible, source-identified print projection alongside web structure, including full URLs or references, dates, status, media captions, and corrections.
- **Boundary:** A web redesign that improves scanning but degrades print, archiving, citation, or offline study is not universally better.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-print-mode-counterevidence; multimode-parity-required`.

### `LGW-C353` — Interlude 3 reframes releases but does not supply modern publication governance

- **Locator:** Interlude 3, printed pages 189–195; PDF pages 219–225.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The interlude reframes press releases as persistent summaries, fact sheets, basic information, visual stories, and social/mobile sources for broad audiences.
- **Candidate implication:** Retain public-web structure, primary-evidence links, role clarity, full dates, media, previews, and print while adding canonical identity, state, embargo, correction, retraction, rights, accessibility, syndication, archive, and measurement.
- **Boundary:** Historical SEO, platform, “forever,” audience, preference, and health-research examples are incomplete or unsafe as current rules. External technical contracts remain unverified because the Developer Index quota is exhausted.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-public-news-content-foundation; modern-editorial-provenance-and-lifecycle-model-required`.

### `LGW-C354` — conversational plain language is an interaction strategy, not reduced intellectual content

- **Locator:** Chapter 10, printed pages 197–198; PDF pages 227–228.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Clear informal prose can preserve complex meaning while making a site feel like a direct exchange with a person pursuing a task.
- **Candidate implication:** Model conversation through explicit actor, purpose, context, question, answer, action, and response rather than through casual tone alone.
- **Boundary:** A system is not a person and may not have listened, understood, remembered, or exercised judgment. Friendly language cannot mask automation, authority, uncertainty, or unavailable help.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-direct-interaction-frame; no-human-understanding-implication`.

### `LGW-C355` — imagined conversation is a drafting aid, not user evidence

- **Locator:** Chapter 10, printed pages 197–198; PDF pages 227–228.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Writers are asked to picture a persona and hear the exchange as if speaking by phone or message.
- **Candidate implication:** Use scenario dialogue to expose missing questions, sequence, vocabulary, and transitions, then validate them with representative people and operational evidence.
- **Boundary:** Persona-authored questions and simulated dialogue remain hypotheses; they cannot establish population language, consent, capability, risk, or need.
- **Promotion status:** `limited-conversation-prototyping-technique; direct-evidence-required`.

### `LGW-C356` — second person can clarify relevance and responsibility

- **Locator:** Chapter 10, printed pages 198–201; PDF pages 228–231.
- **Documented practitioner position:** “You” addresses visitors directly and can replace distant third-person constructions, including in serious and regulatory material.
- **Candidate implication:** Use second person when the addressed role, applicable condition, action, and consequence are unambiguous.
- **Boundary:** “You” can falsely assign fault, obligation, eligibility, ownership, or knowledge when multiple people, organizations, delegates, or affected parties are involved.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-direct-address-option; role-and-liability-resolution-required`.

### `LGW-C357` — imperatives make procedural actors and actions compact

- **Locator:** Chapter 10, printed pages 198–203; PDF pages 228–233.
- **Documented practitioner position:** In instructions, the imperative supplies an understood “you” and puts the action first.
- **Candidate implication:** Type each step with actor, verb, object, prerequisite, condition, expected result, error, recovery, and authority.
- **Boundary:** A grammatical command does not prove the action is available, authorized, safe, reversible, or mandatory; protective context may need to precede it.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-procedural-clarity-pattern; executable-action-contract-required`.

### `LGW-C358` — a licensing rewrite demonstrates decomposition, not verified policy

- **Locator:** Chapter 10, Case Study 10-1, printed pages 199–203; PDF pages 229–233.
- **Documented practitioner position:** A physician-licensing passage becomes easier to follow when direct address, active steps, short sections, and bullets replace third person, passive voice, and dense prose.
- **Candidate implication:** Decompose regulated procedures into canonical requirements, conditions, steps, evidence, deadlines, contacts, exceptions, and outcomes, with proposition-level source mapping.
- **Boundary:** Editorial clarity cannot determine legal scope or current licensing procedure; the responsible authority must verify every transformed proposition and omission.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-regulated-content-decomposition; authoritative-semantic-review-required`.

### `LGW-C359` — the chapter's gender-neutrality guidance is historically bounded

- **Locator:** Chapter 10, printed pages 203–204; PDF pages 233–234.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The chapter recommends “you,” plurals, verbs, or articles to avoid generic “he,” while describing specific people with gendered pronouns.
- **Candidate implication:** Prefer direct role language and use a specific person's self-identified name and pronouns when relevant and permitted.
- **Boundary:** The 2012 discussion assumes a binary model and does not govern current gender-inclusive language, neopronouns, translation, privacy, unknown identity, or jurisdiction-specific records.
- **Promotion status:** `limited-historical-inclusivity-guidance; current-inclusive-language-contract-required`.

### `LGW-C360` — pronouns require stable referents and interaction roles

- **Locator:** Chapter 10, printed pages 204–208; PDF pages 234–238.
- **Documented practitioner position:** “I,” “you,” and “we” should be used consistently according to who asks, answers, authors, or acts.
- **Candidate implication:** Represent speaker, addressee, actor, affected party, accountable entity, and source explicitly for each content state.
- **Boundary:** A visitor-facing “I” can invent a belief or choice; organizational “we” can obscure the legal entity, automated system, vendor, team, or individual responsible.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-referent-consistency; typed-participant-roles-required`.

### `LGW-C361` — first person should expose real authorship or agency

- **Locator:** Chapter 10, printed pages 205–208; PDF pages 235–238.
- **Documented practitioner position:** “I” is presented as appropriate for personal articles, blogs, and social posts, while “we” is natural for organizations.
- **Candidate implication:** Bind first-person copy to a named or typed speaker with authority, provenance, channel, and review responsibility.
- **Boundary:** Voice does not establish authorship, representation, endorsement, institutional authority, or accountability; synthetic and team-produced content require honest attribution.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-authorship-voice-option; provenance-and-authority-required`.

### `LGW-C362` — active voice can expose the responsible actor

- **Locator:** Chapter 10, printed pages 208–211; PDF pages 238–241.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Active voice usually makes prose clearer by placing the actor before the action and object.
- **Candidate implication:** Prefer active constructions when people need to know who decides, performs, verifies, pays, receives, retains, or remedies an action.
- **Boundary:** Surface grammar is not accountability: an active sentence may name the wrong actor or conceal authority behind “we,” while a passive construction may be accurate when the actor is unknown or the affected object is the legitimate focus.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-actor-clarity-default; semantic-accountability-over-grammar`.

### `LGW-C363` — passive voice is a governed option, not an automatic defect

- **Locator:** Chapter 10, printed pages 208–211; PDF pages 238–241.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The author recommends active voice “most of the time” and acknowledges cases where passive voice is appropriate.
- **Candidate implication:** Record why the actor is omitted or deferred and test whether the sentence still preserves responsibility, cause, remedy, and safety.
- **Boundary:** Passive voice may protect a harmed person from blame or foreground a critical state; it may also deliberately conceal institutional action. Context controls.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-contextual-voice-choice; actor-omission-review-required`.

### `LGW-C364` — think-aloud observations support diagnosis but not universal cognition claims

- **Locator:** Chapter 10, printed pages 209–210; PDF pages 239–240.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Historical regulation studies are used to show readers constructing stories and struggling when passive prose omits actors.
- **Candidate implication:** Use think-aloud sessions to locate interpretation gaps, then triangulate with comprehension, action, error, access, and domain review.
- **Boundary:** Small historical observations do not establish universal reading behavior, causal effect, disability coverage, locale parity, or production success.
- **Promotion status:** `limited-formative-reading-evidence; triangulated-validation-required`.

### `LGW-C365` — procedural rewriting can reveal missing operational facts

- **Locator:** Chapter 10, Case Study 10-2, printed pages 210–212; PDF pages 240–242.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Rewriting laboratory-order instructions into active steps exposes who buys supplies, who receives them, and what the user must do.
- **Candidate implication:** Treat unresolved actors, handoffs, channels, dependencies, and confirmations as content defects that block publication until an owner supplies verified facts.
- **Boundary:** A writer must not infer fulfillment, contact, privacy, safety, payment, or delivery behavior from grammar or an incomplete source.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-missing-fact-diagnostic; operational-verification-required`.

### `LGW-C366` — sentence length is a diagnostic signal, not a quality threshold

- **Locator:** Chapter 10, printed pages 212–214; PDF pages 242–244.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The chapter suggests roughly 10–20 words, one thought or two closely connected thoughts, and rewriting sentences that require rereading.
- **Candidate implication:** Measure proposition density, dependency, ambiguity, comprehension, speech, translation, rendering, and task outcome instead of enforcing a universal word limit.
- **Boundary:** Short sentences can be vague or choppy; longer sentences may be necessary to preserve conditions, comparisons, scope, or legal relations.
- **Promotion status:** `limited-editing-heuristic; semantic-and-outcome-validation-required`.

### `LGW-C367` — telegraphic language is channel- and audience-dependent

- **Locator:** Chapter 10, printed page 212; PDF page 242.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Abbreviations and compressed grammar may suit constrained social or message channels when sender and recipients understand them and the style fits.
- **Candidate implication:** Govern abbreviations by canonical expansion, audience recognition, locale, speech pronunciation, ambiguity, urgency, archival meaning, and channel constraints.
- **Boundary:** Historical character limits are not durable rules; compression can exclude language learners, cognitive disabilities, assistive technology, search, translation, and future reuse.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-channel-context-principle; current-access-and-locale-validation-required`.

### `LGW-C368` — fragments can serve presentation but must preserve semantic completeness

- **Locator:** Chapter 10, printed pages 212–214; PDF pages 242–244.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Sentence fragments may work when the site's voice and surrounding structure make their meaning clear.
- **Candidate implication:** Type fragments as labels, values, answers, statuses, steps, or rhetorical units and preserve actor, action, object, negation, condition, time, and scope where material.
- **Boundary:** Visual proximity may not survive extraction, responsive layouts, screen-reader navigation, notifications, search, reuse, or translation; punctuation also affects speech and localization.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-typed-fragment-option; standalone-meaning-and-projection-tests-required`.

### `LGW-C369` — voice is a governed system property, not individual flourish

- **Locator:** Chapter 10, printed pages 213–214; PDF pages 243–244.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Clear writing can retain character, while organizational content strategy governs personality and channel variation.
- **Candidate implication:** Define voice constraints by content type, speaker, relationship, risk, state, locale, accessibility, and channel rather than one global adjective set.
- **Boundary:** Brand personality cannot override truth, dignity, urgency, legal meaning, trauma awareness, or the conventions of a safety-critical task.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-governed-voice-principle; context-and-risk-controls-required`.

### `LGW-C370` — complexity increases the need for clarity, not permission to simplify facts away

- **Locator:** Chapter 10, printed page 214; PDF page 244.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Legal, scientific, technical, medical, and other complex topics benefit especially from clear, coherent prose.
- **Candidate implication:** Separate plain expression from substantive reduction and retain technical precision, evidence, uncertainty, contraindications, exceptions, and authoritative terminology.
- **Boundary:** The historical Mayo Clinic example is not current clinical authority and cannot substantiate health advice or universal presentation choices.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-complex-domain-clarity; domain-authority-and-safety-required`.

### `LGW-C371` — cutting should remove friction without deleting propositions

- **Locator:** Chapter 10, printed pages 214–215; PDF pages 244–245.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Writers should revise inflated phrases and remove words that do not contribute to meaning.
- **Candidate implication:** Require proposition-level diff for actors, actions, objects, conditions, negation, time, exceptions, evidence, uncertainty, rights, and consequences before accepting a shorter version.
- **Boundary:** Fewer words do not establish clarity, truth, accessibility, completeness, or lower cognitive load.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-concision-edit; semantic-loss-control-required`.

### `LGW-C372` — keep grammatical dependencies together and relocate typed detail

- **Locator:** Chapter 10, printed pages 215–217; PDF pages 245–247.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Material inserted between subject, verb, and object can interrupt comprehension; key messages and separate details should follow the order of user questions.
- **Candidate implication:** Keep core propositions intact and move deadlines, addresses, channels, qualifications, and alternatives into explicitly related fields or components.
- **Boundary:** Relocation must preserve programmatic association, reading order, print, translation, legal scope, and the point at which information is needed.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-dependency-and-detail-structure; association-and-timing-required`.

### `LGW-C373` — Case Study 10-3 turns a sentence into a decision-and-submission structure

- **Locator:** Chapter 10, Case Study 10-3, printed pages 215–217; PDF pages 245–247.
- **Documented practitioner position:** A public-comment sentence becomes an invitation, deadline, and channel-specific submission options ordered by likely questions.
- **Candidate implication:** Model participation content with proposal identity, eligibility, deadline and timezone, accepted channels, address or endpoint, format, accessibility, privacy, confirmation, late/failed submission, and contact.
- **Boundary:** The visual rewrite does not by itself prove legal sufficiency, channel availability, accommodation, records handling, or successful submission.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-public-process-decomposition; end-to-end-participation-contract-required`.

### `LGW-C374` — paragraph length should follow semantic units and reading context

- **Locator:** Chapter 10, printed pages 218–219; PDF pages 248–249.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Short paragraphs, including one-sentence paragraphs, are presented as easier to use on web and mobile surfaces.
- **Candidate implication:** Break at coherent shifts in topic, state, action, evidence, exception, or speaker and validate continuity across viewport and access modes.
- **Boundary:** Paragraph count or sentence count is not a readability measure; excessive fragmentation can destroy argument, relationship, rhythm, and screen-reader context.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-semantic-chunking-option; no-fixed-paragraph-limit`.

### `LGW-C375` — lists and tables are semantic alternatives, not decorative scanning devices

- **Locator:** Chapter 10, printed page 218; PDF page 248.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Information may become more scannable when removed from sentences and expressed as lists or tables.
- **Candidate implication:** Choose prose, list, or table according to whether meaning is narrative, sequential, set-based, comparative, or relational, with corresponding semantics.
- **Boundary:** Fragmentation can lose qualifiers and relationships; visual columns, bullets, or alignment do not provide list or table semantics, responsive behavior, or speech comprehension.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-structure-selection-principle; semantic-form-contract-required`.

### `LGW-C376` — context-first ordering supports comprehension and safe action timing

- **Locator:** Chapter 10, printed pages 219–222; PDF pages 249–252.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Paragraphs and sentences should begin with their topic or known context before introducing new information, especially when later qualifications constrain action.
- **Candidate implication:** Order condition before consequence and prerequisite or applicability before an executable instruction when premature action could cause error or harm.
- **Boundary:** “Known” is audience- and state-dependent; urgency, warnings, conclusions, evidence, or decisions may legitimately precede background, and no universal order follows from the cited historical study.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-given-new-and-safety-ordering; context-specific-validation-required`.

### `LGW-C377` — Case Study 10-4 shows that clarity work can require new facts

- **Locator:** Chapter 10, Case Study 10-4, printed pages 219–221; PDF pages 249–251.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Reordering a yam-import rule exposes the unresolved question of who must perform the fumigation and suggests separating importer-facing requirements from specialist procedure.
- **Candidate implication:** Block final copy when the responsible actor, qualification, timing, jurisdiction, or procedure owner is unknown; route the gap to an authoritative subject-matter owner.
- **Boundary:** Placeholder actors such as “[person]” are evidence of incompleteness, not publishable content, and editorial inference could change a legal obligation.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-actor-gap-diagnostic; publication-blocking-authority-resolution`.

### `LGW-C378` — nominalizations can conceal action, obligation, and responsibility

- **Locator:** Chapter 10, printed pages 221–223; PDF pages 251–253.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Turning nouns such as “recommendation” or “retention” back into verbs can produce shorter, more active sentences.
- **Candidate implication:** Inspect abstract nouns for a hidden actor, action, modality, object, time, and consequence, then use the precise governing verb.
- **Boundary:** Mechanical suffix replacement can alter domain meaning; “must,” “may,” “should,” “recommend,” and “require” carry different authority and legal force.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-hidden-action-diagnostic; modality-and-domain-precision-required`.

### `LGW-C379` — user vocabulary must come from research and map to canonical concepts

- **Locator:** Chapter 10, printed pages 223–224; PDF pages 253–254.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Familiar audience terms can create recognition, while insiders commonly overestimate visitors' knowledge of organizational or specialist jargon.
- **Candidate implication:** Maintain researched aliases, search terms, definitions, locale variants, and canonical domain labels with provenance and scope.
- **Boundary:** Popular vocabulary can be ambiguous, stigmatizing, inaccurate, adversarial, or unsafe; expert terms may be necessary for precision, records, interoperability, and professional audiences.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-researched-vocabulary-principle; canonical-concept-mapping-required`.

### `LGW-C380` — localization is acknowledged but remains an explicit chapter gap

- **Locator:** Chapter 10, printed pages 223–224; PDF pages 253–254.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The chapter notes global, multilingual, and culturally diverse audiences and says localization is important but outside the book's scope.
- **Candidate implication:** Govern source content for translation with locale, concept, register, grammar, gender, expansion, direction, format, terminology, jurisdiction, review, and fallback metadata.
- **Boundary:** English pronoun, imperative, sentence-length, fragment, given-new, and “short word” advice cannot be projected mechanically across languages and cultures.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-explicit-localization-gap; locale-specific-design-and-review-required`.

### `LGW-C381` — plain-language evidence is promising but bundled and historically bounded

- **Locator:** Chapter 10, printed pages 224–226; PDF pages 254–256.
- **Documented practitioner position:** A cited medicine-site study reports higher task success and substantially lower task time for both lower- and higher-literacy groups after a broad plain-language redesign.
- **Candidate implication:** Evaluate plain-language transformations with comprehension, accuracy, time, error, recovery, confidence, accessibility, burden, and downstream outcomes across relevant literacy and language groups.
- **Boundary:** The intervention bundled organization, sentence, paragraph, list, and vocabulary changes; the chapter does not establish which caused the results, statistical uncertainty, current replicability, clinical safety, or production accessibility.
- **Promotion status:** `moderate-bundled-task-evidence; causal-and-current-validation-required`.

### `LGW-C382` — Chapter 10 supplies sentence diagnostics, not a complete language runtime

- **Locator:** Chapter 10, printed pages 197–226; PDF pages 227–256.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The chapter joins pronouns, imperatives, active voice, short sentences and paragraphs, decomposition, context-first order, action verbs, audience vocabulary, and plain language into a conversational editing method.
- **Candidate implication:** Retain actor-action-context diagnostics while adding typed participants, modality, executable state, authoritative meaning, proposition diff, inclusive language, localization, speech, cognitive access, safety, legal review, and outcome testing.
- **Boundary:** Examples and evidence are from 2012 or earlier, gender treatment is binary, localization is deferred, and external technical contracts remain unverified because the required Developer Index is quota-limited.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-sentence-design-foundation; modern-language-runtime-and-governance-required`.

### `LGW-C383` — lists and tables can remove repetition without removing meaning

- **Locator:** Chapter 11, printed page 227; PDF page 257.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Lists add space around items, while tables remove repeated wording and make related information easier to scan.
- **Candidate implication:** Transform prose only after identifying its canonical items, sequence, categories, relationships, qualifiers, and reading purpose.
- **Boundary:** Visual brevity can hide syntax, scope, exceptions, or relationships; semantic equivalence must survive speech, extraction, translation, print, and responsive projection.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-structural-concision-principle; semantic-equivalence-required`.

### `LGW-C384` — bulleted lists represent sets or alternatives

- **Locator:** Chapter 11, printed pages 227–229; PDF pages 257–259.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Bullets help people recognize, count, scan, and mentally check items or options that do not imply a required order.
- **Candidate implication:** Type a bulleted structure as a set, choice, feature group, material list, prerequisite set, or other unordered relation and state its selection rule.
- **Boundary:** Bullets do not communicate whether people need all, any, exactly one, at least one, or none; introductory text or controls must make that logic explicit.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-unordered-list-foundation; selection-cardinality-required`.

### `LGW-C385` — list conversion must preserve item boundaries and category logic

- **Locator:** Chapter 11, museum and camp examples, printed pages 227–229; PDF pages 257–259.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Dense enumerations become easier to scan when each museum, packing item, or instruction receives its own visible list item and related items are nested.
- **Candidate implication:** Identify atomic items, groups, nesting, introductory scope, and exceptions before converting prose to a semantic list.
- **Boundary:** Editorial splitting can falsely make dependent phrases independent, flatten hierarchy, or detach qualifications from the items they govern.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-itemization-pattern; proposition-and-nesting-review-required`.

### `LGW-C386` — decorative markers must not imply undocumented categories

- **Locator:** Chapter 11, printed pages 229–231; PDF pages 259–261.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Custom bullets may express site personality, but differences in color or shape should not make people wonder whether they encode a grouping.
- **Candidate implication:** Separate the list's semantic marker from optional decoration and document every visual distinction that carries meaning.
- **Boundary:** Brand expression cannot replace list semantics; color, motion, images, and custom glyphs need nonvisual equivalence, contrast, forced-colors behavior, localization, and reduced-motion review.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-decoration-versus-semantics-boundary; multimodal-equivalence-required`.

### `LGW-C387` — numbered lists should signal required sequence

- **Locator:** Chapter 11, printed pages 231–233; PDF pages 261–263.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Instructions imply sequence, and numbers help people see step count, order, completeness, progress, and their place after leaving the page to act.
- **Candidate implication:** Use an ordered structure when order changes validity or outcome and model stable step identity, prerequisites, completion, resumption, branches, and recovery.
- **Boundary:** Numbers alone do not make instructions executable or prove that every person must perform every step in one linear pass.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-ordered-procedure-foundation; stateful-flow-contract-required`.

### `LGW-C388` — procedural lists expose omitted and misordered steps

- **Locator:** Chapter 11, printed pages 231–233; PDF pages 261–263.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Converting procedural paragraphs into numbered steps helps authors notice missing actions, incorrect order, and serious consequences.
- **Candidate implication:** Treat procedural decomposition as a diagnostic and validate it by domain review, walkthrough, representative task performance, error injection, and recovery testing.
- **Boundary:** A tidy list can still encode an unsafe, inaccessible, unauthorized, obsolete, or operationally impossible process.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-procedure-diagnostic; end-to-end-operational-validation-required`.

### `LGW-C389` — separate rationale, materials, and procedure

- **Locator:** Chapter 11, concrete-preparation example, printed pages 232–233; PDF pages 262–263.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The suggested rewrite distinguishes why the task matters, what materials are needed, and how to perform twelve grouped steps.
- **Candidate implication:** Model rationale, prerequisites, equipment or materials, hazards, ordered actions, expected results, and disposal or follow-up as related but distinct content units.
- **Boundary:** Separating information must not defer safety, eligibility, protective equipment, or irreversible consequences until after action begins.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-procedure-content-typing; safety-timing-required`.

### `LGW-C390` — one visible step should have one primary action

- **Locator:** Chapter 11, concrete-preparation example, printed page 233; PDF page 263.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The revision begins steps with verbs and generally gives each step one action.
- **Candidate implication:** Give each step a primary verb while preserving inseparable subactions, conditions, expected results, and verification.
- **Boundary:** Mechanical atomization can create excessive interaction, lose simultaneity, or separate a warning from the action it controls.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-primary-action-per-step; procedural-coherence-required`.

### `LGW-C391` — branches require explicit decisions and mutually intelligible outcomes

- **Locator:** Chapter 11, printed pages 233–235; PDF pages 263–265.
- **Documented practitioner position:** When the next instruction depends on a condition, the decision should be explicit and its “if, then” alternatives can be shown beneath the step.
- **Candidate implication:** Model decision variable, possible values, evidence needed, branch destination, exclusivity, default, unknown case, re-entry, and recovery.
- **Boundary:** A two-column presentation is insufficient when conditions overlap, are unknown, require diagnosis, or have safety-critical consequences.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-explicit-branching-pattern; decision-logic-contract-required`.

### `LGW-C392` — condition-before-action can prevent premature execution

- **Locator:** Chapter 11, printed page 235; PDF page 265.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The chapter resolves conflict between action-first steps and context-first conditions by making review of the condition an action, then presenting branch outcomes.
- **Candidate implication:** Put applicability and protective conditions before the governed action while keeping the decision itself operationally explicit.
- **Boundary:** Not every branch belongs in a table, and no layout substitutes for validated eligibility, diagnosis, permissions, or real-time system state.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-condition-action-ordering; validated-decision-state-required`.

### `LGW-C393` — illustrations in instructions require equivalent procedural information

- **Locator:** Chapter 11, Twitter example, printed page 235; PDF page 265.
- **Documented practitioner position:** A screenshot can help show where to find a control during a step.
- **Candidate implication:** Associate each visual with the relevant step, version, platform, state, caption, text equivalent, zoom behavior, and update owner.
- **Boundary:** Screenshots age quickly, may expose data, and cannot be the only way to identify a control, state, spatial location, gesture, or outcome.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-show-and-tell-option; maintained-nonvisual-equivalence-required`.
