### `LGW-C394` — numeric rhetoric must remain distinct from procedural order

- **Locator:** Chapter 11, printed pages 236–238; PDF pages 266–268.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Numbered tips may be valid, but numbers should not simultaneously represent instructions and alternatives in a way that confuses expectations.
- **Candidate implication:** Reserve ordered-list semantics for order-bearing content within mixed tasks and label enumerated collections by their actual relation.
- **Boundary:** Familiar “N tips” formatting does not prove completeness, importance, order, evidence, or user value and may manufacture engagement.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-numeric-semantics-distinction; no-count-based-quality-claim`.

### `LGW-C395` — Case Study 11-1 separates return choices from the steps within each choice

- **Locator:** Chapter 11, Case Study 11-1, printed pages 236–238; PDF pages 266–268.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Return methods are alternatives suited to bullets, while the actions inside each method are ordered steps; disqualifying notes should appear before unusable instructions.
- **Candidate implication:** Model choice eligibility, cost, channel, prerequisites, ordered actions, refund result, confirmation, timing, exceptions, and fallback for each return path.
- **Boundary:** The historical commerce example does not establish current policy, carrier availability, accessibility, jurisdictional rights, refund timing, or fee lawfulness.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-choice-versus-procedure-model; current-service-and-rights-verification-required`.

### `LGW-C396` — list length depends on recognition, organization, and task

- **Locator:** Chapter 11, printed pages 238–239; PDF pages 268–269.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Roughly five to ten unfamiliar items are easier to handle, while longer familiar, predictably ordered lists may work.
- **Candidate implication:** Evaluate recognition, search strategy, ordering, grouping, filtering, viewport, input method, memory burden, and task success instead of enforcing a universal count.
- **Boundary:** The five-to-ten range is a heuristic, not a cognitive or accessibility threshold; even familiar lists vary across locale, expertise, naming, disability, and state.
- **Promotion status:** `limited-list-length-heuristic; task-and-population-validation-required`.

### `LGW-C397` — long lists need meaningful order, grouping, and alternate retrieval

- **Locator:** Chapter 11, state and territory examples, printed pages 238–239; PDF pages 268–269.
- **Documented practitioner position:** A long familiar list can work when people know the target and its likely alphabetical position; visible grouping may support scanning and print.
- **Candidate implication:** Provide governed ordering, headings, search or typeahead, keyboard navigation, full option exposure, print behavior, and an unknown or not-listed path where needed.
- **Boundary:** Familiarity and alphabetical order are locale-specific; dropdowns, maps, multicolumn visual wrapping, and long option sets have distinct access and interaction costs.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-long-list-conditions; multimodal-retrieval-contract-required`.

### `LGW-C398` — placing a list in an image alt attribute is not an accessible map alternative

- **Locator:** Chapter 11, Figure 11-10 commentary, printed page 238; PDF page 268.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The chapter suggests making a map accessible by placing the list in an “ALT-tag.”
- **Candidate implication:** Reject this as implementation guidance; provide the same selectable destinations as semantic links or controls with names, states, keyboard access, focus, and equivalent outcomes.
- **Boundary:** An alt attribute describes an image; it does not create navigable options, preserve interactive relationships, or provide equivalent map functionality. Current primary contracts remain unverified because the Developer Index interface is unavailable.
- **Promotion status:** `rejected-outdated-accessibility-advice; operable-equivalent-interface-required`.

### `LGW-C399` — parallel list grammar supports prediction but must not flatten meaning

- **Locator:** Chapter 11, printed pages 239–240; PDF pages 269–270.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Starting list items with the same grammatical form creates a recognizable pattern and can speed reading.
- **Candidate implication:** Use parallel syntax when items share a semantic type and expose deliberate exceptions when their type or consequence differs.
- **Boundary:** Forced parallelism can change modality, agency, chronology, emphasis, or truth; surface consistency is subordinate to semantic accuracy.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-parallelism-default; meaning-preservation-required`.

### `LGW-C400` — visual list grouping must follow semantic association

- **Locator:** Chapter 11, printed pages 240–242; PDF pages 270–272.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Spacing, indentation, marker prominence, and aligned wrapping should connect an introduction with its list and distinguish individual items.
- **Candidate implication:** Keep DOM, reading, focus, visual, responsive, and print grouping aligned, with list introductions and notes programmatically associated where needed.
- **Boundary:** Fixed point spacing and visual indentation are historical styling examples, not universal tokens or semantics; zoom, reflow, text spacing, user styles, and long translations can change layout.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-semantic-visual-alignment; responsive-access-validation-required`.

### `LGW-C401` — expected results and notes need typed relationships to steps

- **Locator:** Chapter 11, smoke-detector example, printed page 241; PDF page 271.
- **Documented practitioner position:** A result can appear on its own indented line beneath a step, while notes are visually subordinate but associated.
- **Candidate implication:** Type content as instruction, expected result, verification, warning, exception, note, troubleshooting, or recovery and associate it with the exact step it governs.
- **Boundary:** Visual indentation alone does not convey relation or priority; critical warnings must not be styled as optional notes or announced after the action.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-step-support-typing; semantic-association-and-safety-priority-required`.

### `LGW-C402` — lists and tables differ by relationship, not apparent column count

- **Locator:** Chapter 11, printed pages 242–243; PDF pages 272–273.
- **Documented practitioner position:** A list contains one category even when visually wrapped into columns; a table contains at least two categories and expresses relationships across them.
- **Candidate implication:** Select list or table semantics from the data relation, not the visual layout, and preserve that relation under responsive transformation.
- **Boundary:** Some structures are better represented as description lists, cards, forms, trees, or charts; the chapter's binary does not cover every semantic relation.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-relation-based-form-selection; broader-structure-taxonomy-required`.

### `LGW-C403` — repeated conditional sentences can become a relational table

- **Locator:** Chapter 11, printed pages 242–243; PDF pages 272–273.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Repeated “if this category, then this answer” prose can become rows whose shared language moves into column headings.
- **Candidate implication:** Normalize repeated statements into typed dimensions, headers, values, units, scope, provenance, and exceptions, then verify row-level semantic equivalence.
- **Boundary:** Removing repeated words can also remove qualifiers or create false uniformity when rows differ in authority, timing, conditions, or exceptions.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-relational-normalization-pattern; row-level-semantic-diff-required`.

### `LGW-C404` — numerical comparison needs units, periods, conditions, and definitions

- **Locator:** Chapter 11, rental-rate example, printed pages 243–245; PDF pages 273–275.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Tables support comparison of related numbers, while missing date ranges, mislabeled fields, and unexplained categories undermine the comparison.
- **Candidate implication:** Model value, unit, currency, period, basis, range boundaries, inclusivity, precision, source, effective date, and category definitions.
- **Boundary:** Alignment and shading cannot correct stale, incomparable, selectively framed, or misleading numbers.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-comparison-table-foundation; quantitative-provenance-required`.

### `LGW-C405` — a table should answer a defined question for a defined situation

- **Locator:** Chapter 11, printed pages 244–245; PDF pages 274–275.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Tables are useful when the answer is “it depends,” with the determining situation leading to the relevant answer across the row.
- **Candidate implication:** Record the question, decision variable, audience state, answer dimensions, and intended comparison or lookup task for every table.
- **Boundary:** A table can imply deterministic answers where uncertainty, multiple simultaneous conditions, interaction effects, judgment, or exceptions exist.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-question-to-relation-model; uncertainty-and-exception-support-required`.

### `LGW-C406` — first-column priority is reading-direction and task dependent

- **Locator:** Chapter 11, printed page 245; PDF page 275.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The first column should contain what people know or what the answer depends on, so they can scan down and then across to the answer.
- **Candidate implication:** Choose row headers from the primary lookup dimension and adapt visual order, markup, and speech behavior for locale, direction, and task.
- **Boundary:** “First” is not universally leftmost; users may compare columns, navigate headers, search values, or approach with a different known variable.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-lookup-dimension-principle; locale-and-task-specific-order-required`.

### `LGW-C407` — multidimensional decisions may need filters or guided questions instead of a table

- **Locator:** Chapter 11, printed page 245; PDF page 275.
- **Documented practitioner position:** When an answer depends on many variables, a large matrix may be less useful than sequential questions or filters.
- **Candidate implication:** Choose comparison table, filters, calculator, decision tree, or guided flow based on dimensionality, reversibility, privacy, transparency, and need to compare the full set.
- **Boundary:** Guided questions can conceal options, collect unnecessary data, infer sensitive traits, impose defaults, and make comparison or backtracking harder.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-complexity-routing-option; transparency-privacy-and-recovery-required`.

### `LGW-C408` — Case Study 11-2 converts shipping bands into explicit range relationships

- **Locator:** Chapter 11, Case Study 11-2, printed pages 246–247; PDF pages 276–277.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Shipping-charge bullets repeat language and put the unknown price before the known order value; a two-column table makes the ranges and charges directly comparable.
- **Candidate implication:** Model destination scope, carrier, order-value interval, boundary inclusivity, charge, currency, effective date, exclusions, military addresses, taxes, surcharges, and fallback.
- **Boundary:** The suggested table retains possible gaps and ambiguity unless every boundary and destination category is verified; historical prices and carriers are not current evidence.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-range-table-pattern; exhaustive-boundary-and-service-verification-required`.

### `LGW-C409` — table simplicity is a task outcome, not a column cap

- **Locator:** Chapter 11, printed pages 248–249; PDF pages 278–279.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Two columns are preferred, more columns may work when visible together, and large tables may be divided or layered.
- **Candidate implication:** Evaluate comparison integrity, header persistence, navigation, zoom, reflow, small screens, speech, keyboard use, print, and export before splitting or transforming a table.
- **Boundary:** A two-column default and avoidance of horizontal scrolling are heuristics; splitting can destroy cross-row comparison, conceal context, or force repeated navigation.
- **Promotion status:** `limited-two-column-heuristic; relationship-preserving-responsive-validation-required`.

### `LGW-C410` — responsive table design must preserve relationships, not merely fit width

- **Locator:** Chapter 11, printed pages 248–249; PDF pages 278–279.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The chapter recognizes small screens, magnification, nonmaximized windows, and difficulty noticing horizontal scrolling.
- **Candidate implication:** Define narrow-view behavior for every table—reflow, scrolling, prioritized columns, transposed data, cards, or alternate views—while retaining headers, units, scope, comparison, focus, and position.
- **Boundary:** Hiding columns or converting rows to cards can make material information unavailable or comparisons impossible; visual fit is not semantic or functional parity.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-responsive-constraint-recognition; explicit-transformation-contract-required`.

### `LGW-C411` — long tables require persistent context and navigable headers

- **Locator:** Chapter 11, printed page 249; PDF page 279.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Long tables are difficult because users must remember column headings; the chapter suggests smaller tables and points to historical screen-reader tagging guidance.
- **Candidate implication:** Provide semantic caption and headers, scoped associations, repeated or sticky visual context, keyboard and screen-reader navigation, position awareness, and equivalent export or print behavior.
- **Boundary:** Breaking a table into smaller tables can duplicate, drift, or obscure comparison; the cited 2012 link does not establish current accessibility conformance.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-context-persistence-need; current-table-accessibility-contract-unverified`.

### `LGW-C412` — table styling should support row tracking without becoming the semantics

- **Locator:** Chapter 11, printed pages 249–250; PDF pages 279–280.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Light rules, restrained borders, alternating row shading, left-aligned text, and decimal alignment can reduce visual interference and aid scanning.
- **Candidate implication:** Use styling as redundant row and column support while preserving explicit table markup, contrast, forced-colors behavior, zoom, focus, selection, and numeric semantics.
- **Boundary:** Alternating color, alignment, and light borders may disappear or fail under user styles and cannot replace headers, units, row identity, or relationships.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-supportive-table-styling; semantics-and-user-style-resilience-required`.

### `LGW-C413` — Chapter 11 supplies a structural grammar, not a complete accessible data runtime

- **Locator:** Chapter 11 and summary, printed pages 227–251; PDF pages 257–281.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The chapter distinguishes unordered items, ordered instructions, branches, and relational comparisons, then adds grouping, parallelism, simplicity, and visual formatting guidance.
- **Candidate implication:** Retain relation-first structure selection while adding typed list cardinality, flow state, decision logic, captions and headers, data provenance, responsive transformation, localization, programmatic association, keyboard and speech journeys, export, and validation.
- **Boundary:** Fixed counts and column preferences are heuristics; several examples are historical; the map alt-text suggestion is rejected; and current primary technical contracts remain unverified because the Developer Index interface is unavailable.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-list-table-structural-foundation; modern-semantic-data-and-access-runtime-required`.

### `LGW-C414` — legal information is still user-facing content

- **Locator:** Interlude 4, printed page 253; PDF page 283.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Terms, privacy notices, accessibility policies, and other legal material should be written as clearly as other web content and treated as communication between an organization and its visitors.
- **Candidate implication:** Include legal content in the same discovery, architecture, accessibility, localization, testing, governance, and maintenance system as product content while preserving its distinct authority.
- **Boundary:** Calling a legal document a conversation must not imply equal bargaining power, negotiation, comprehension, assent, or that a person chose the terms freely.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-integrated-legal-content-principle; power-and-authority-boundary-required`.

### `LGW-C415` — accuracy, sufficiency, and clarity are separate validation dimensions

- **Locator:** Interlude 4, printed page 253; PDF page 283.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Legal information can be correct, cover relevant situations, and remain understandable; clarity need not be traded against accuracy or sufficiency.
- **Candidate implication:** Require independent factual, legal, coverage, comprehension, accessibility, localization, operational, and affected-party review rather than treating legal approval as proof of clarity.
- **Boundary:** Clear prose can still be false, incomplete, unlawful, unfair, unenforceable, inaccessible, or inconsistent with actual system behavior.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-three-dimension-frame; multidimensional-assurance-required`.

### `LGW-C416` — clarity may support compliance but does not guarantee behavior or prevent disputes

- **Locator:** Interlude 4, printed page 253; PDF page 283.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The interlude argues that understandable terms and policies help people follow them and reduce conflict or litigation.
- **Candidate implication:** Measure comprehension, informed choice, correct action, complaint, remedy, and dispute outcomes separately while examining whether the underlying rule is feasible and fair.
- **Boundary:** Clear wording cannot cause compliance when terms are coercive, hidden, impractical, unlawful, contradicted by design, or unsupported by service operations; litigation avoidance is an organizational outcome, not proof of user benefit.
- **Promotion status:** `limited-behavioral-benefit-claim; fairness-and-causal-evidence-required`.

### `LGW-C417` — plain-language transformation requires proposition-level legal verification

- **Locator:** Interlude 4, personal-data example, printed page 253; PDF page 283.
- **Documented practitioner position:** A concrete direct-address version is presented as more explanatory and equally legal compared with abstract language about processing identifiable-person data.
- **Candidate implication:** Map every actor, data subject, data category, source, purpose, action, recipient, legal basis, retention rule, right, and exception from source to rewrite.
- **Boundary:** The rewrite adds examples and shifts scope; editorial confidence cannot establish equivalence, legal effect, jurisdictional validity, or completeness.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-plain-legal-language-goal; authoritative-semantic-diff-required`.

### `LGW-C418` — trust must follow trustworthy facts and conduct

- **Locator:** Interlude 4, printed page 253; PDF page 283.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The interlude asks whether plain language is more likely to inspire trust and comfort.
- **Candidate implication:** Treat trust as an earned outcome of accurate disclosure, fair practice, meaningful control, security, remedy, and observed conduct rather than a tone target.
- **Boundary:** Friendly legal copy can increase misplaced trust, lower scrutiny, or conceal harmful terms; comfort is not informed consent or comprehension.
- **Promotion status:** `limited-trust-hypothesis; trustworthy-operation-and-no-manipulation-required`.

### `LGW-C419` — archaic legal diction is not legal authority

- **Locator:** Interlude 4, printed pages 254–255; PDF pages 284–285.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Terms such as “pursuant to,” “hereinafter,” and “to wit” are historical language, not necessary markers of legality.
- **Candidate implication:** Replace obsolete diction with current precise language when authoritative reviewers confirm that scope, modality, definitions, and precedent are preserved.
- **Boundary:** Some terms of art have jurisdiction-specific meaning that a shorter synonym may not preserve; age or unfamiliarity alone does not authorize replacement.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-archaic-diction-diagnostic; jurisdictional-meaning-review-required`.

### `LGW-C420` — legal citation and user-facing explanation are distinct related fields

- **Locator:** Interlude 4, legislative-decree example, printed page 254; PDF page 284.
- **Documented practitioner position:** A plain statement of why information is provided can be separated from the formal citation that establishes its legal source.
- **Candidate implication:** Model obligation summary, authoritative source, jurisdiction, section, effective date, applicability, version, and canonical link separately but keep them visibly and programmatically related.
- **Boundary:** A summary does not replace governing text, and a citation does not prove the summary is accurate, current, applicable, or complete.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-summary-source-separation; authority-lineage-required`.

### `LGW-C421` — acronyms and defined terms need durable retrieval support

- **Locator:** Interlude 4, printed page 254; PDF page 284.
- **Documented practitioner position:** A one-use acronym and distant “hereinafter” definition create memory burden; repeating a meaningful name may be clearer.
- **Candidate implication:** Store canonical term, short label, definition, jurisdiction, scope, aliases, and contextual expansion, then validate extraction, speech, search, and translation.
- **Boundary:** Repetition can be verbose and translated abbreviations differ; formal defined terms may be necessary when they delimit legal scope.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-term-memory-diagnostic; canonical-definition-system-required`.

### `LGW-C422` — official drafting guidance is evidence only within its authority and date

- **Locator:** Interlude 4, Federal Register example, printed page 255; PDF page 285.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The interlude cites a U.S. Federal Register list of expressions to avoid as evidence that lawyers can draft regulations without archaic forms.
- **Candidate implication:** Bind every legal-language rule to issuing authority, document type, jurisdiction, effective date, version, and applicability.
- **Boundary:** A historical U.S. federal drafting resource does not govern private contracts, other jurisdictions, later law, court interpretation, or every legal genre.
- **Promotion status:** `moderate-authoritative-historical-example; current-scope-verification-required`.

### `LGW-C423` — technical privacy jargon should resolve to observable practices

- **Locator:** Interlude 4, printed page 255; PDF page 285.
- **Documented practitioner position:** “Understand how people use our web site” is offered as plainer than references to traffic patterns, navigation, and clickstream data.
- **Candidate implication:** Disclose concrete event categories, data fields, sources, purposes, recipients, retention, legal basis, controls, and consequences instead of substituting a broad euphemism.
- **Boundary:** The plain version is less specific than “clickstream data” and may conceal surveillance scope; familiar wording is not sufficient disclosure.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-jargon-diagnostic; practice-specific-privacy-disclosure-required`.

### `LGW-C424` — legal headings should expose material questions and rights

- **Locator:** Interlude 4, printed page 256; PDF page 286.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Question and statement headings using words such as collect, use, share, and choices can make privacy content more findable than vague noun headings.
- **Candidate implication:** Derive headings from material practices, obligations, rights, choices, consequences, exceptions, complaints, and remedies, with canonical policy coverage beneath each.
- **Boundary:** User-language headings can omit legally required or unasked information; “choices” is misleading when no meaningful refusal or alternative exists.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-material-question-headings; coverage-and-rights-parity-required`.

### `LGW-C425` — glossaries supplement but cannot defer essential meaning

- **Locator:** Interlude 4, printed page 256; PDF page 286.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Linked glossary definitions can support necessary technical terms such as “cookie.”
- **Candidate implication:** Provide contextual definitions at first material reliance and maintain glossary links as optional deeper support with stable anchors, locale, version, and source.
- **Boundary:** People may not open a glossary; a definition cannot cure deceptive naming, missing disclosure, inaccessible interaction, or inconsistent use.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-contextual-definition-support; no-essential-meaning-deferral`.

### `LGW-C426` — familiar content patterns can improve legal readability without changing authority

- **Locator:** Interlude 4, printed page 256; PDF page 286.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Pronouns, short headed sections, active sentences, lists, tables, and examples can be used in legal information.
- **Candidate implication:** Apply ordinary content structures while retaining explicit modality, scope, conditions, exceptions, source, effective date, hierarchy, and governing-text status.
- **Boundary:** Readability patterns do not themselves establish legal sufficiency, assent, enforceability, current law, or accessibility.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-shared-content-patterns; legal-semantics-remain-controlling`.

### `LGW-C427` — a model policy is a structural illustration, not reusable legal text

- **Locator:** Interlude 4, Case Study 4-1, printed pages 257–258; PDF pages 287–288.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The interlude offers a model privacy policy and says organizations must modify or add information for their actual site or app.
- **Candidate implication:** Use only the structural questions as discovery prompts; generate policy text from a verified data inventory, system behavior, vendors, jurisdictions, legal bases, retention, rights, and operations.
- **Boundary:** Copying model language can create false disclosures and legal exposure. The 2012 sample predates major current privacy regimes and contemporary tracking, profiling, biometrics, AI, mobile, and cross-device practices.
- **Promotion status:** `limited-structural-example; no-template-reuse-authority`.

### `LGW-C428` — legal documents require explicit temporal and version identity

- **Locator:** Interlude 4, model-policy opening, printed page 257; PDF page 287.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The model policy prominently includes the date it was last changed.
- **Candidate implication:** Track effective, published, modified, accepted, superseded, and archived dates; version identity; change summary; notice method; affected population; and preserved prior versions.
- **Boundary:** An “updated” date alone does not identify what changed, when terms take effect, which version governed an event, or whether renewed notice or consent is required.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-policy-date-foundation; full-version-and-change-lifecycle-required`.

### `LGW-C429` — in-page policy navigation requires accessible state and destination behavior

- **Locator:** Interlude 4, model-policy opening, printed page 257; PDF page 287.
- **Documented practitioner position:** A linked question outline at the top gives an overview and direct access to specific policy sections.
- **Candidate implication:** Preserve meaningful link text, heading hierarchy, fragment identity, focus movement, URL and history, sticky offset, reduced motion, print outline, and synchronized policy changes.
- **Boundary:** Same-page links do not compensate for missing coverage or an unusably long policy, and historical screenshots do not prove accessible implementation.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-policy-outline-pattern; anchor-navigation-contract-required`.

### `LGW-C430` — privacy claims must match actual collection across every state and channel

- **Locator:** Interlude 4, model-policy collection section, printed page 257; PDF page 287.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The sample distinguishes browsing from named interactions and lists personal details collected for orders, accounts, newsletters, surveys, contests, and events.
- **Candidate implication:** Verify collection at page load, consent state, authentication, forms, purchases, support, stores, phone, SDKs, logs, security systems, vendors, and inferred-data pipelines.
- **Boundary:** “We do not collect” is a high-risk universal claim; network metadata, cookies, logs, fraud tools, analytics, embeds, and processors may contradict it.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-practice-parity-requirement; runtime-data-inventory-required`.

### `LGW-C431` — purpose, contact, personalization, and promotion require separate controls

- **Locator:** Interlude 4, model-policy use section, printed page 257; PDF page 287.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The sample groups order processing, order questions, personalization, promotional email, and preference research, with “contact us” as an opt-out.
- **Candidate implication:** Separate necessary service purposes from optional personalization, research, and marketing; state legal basis, channel, frequency, profiling, recipients, controls, effect, and withdrawal path for each.
- **Boundary:** Contact-based opt-out may be burdensome or legally insufficient; purchase and service data do not automatically authorize promotion, profiling, or preference inference.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-purpose-separation-gap; consent-and-preference-contract-required`.

### `LGW-C432` — absolute sharing claims and vague partner classes require substantiation

- **Locator:** Interlude 4, model-policy sharing section, printed page 257; PDF page 287.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The sample says email and phone are never disclosed while postal information and interests may be shared with “carefully selected” companies, subject to contact-based refusal.
- **Candidate implication:** Enumerate recipient categories and roles, data categories, purposes, legal basis, transfers, onward use, contracts, retention, sale/share status, safeguards, and accessible opt-out.
- **Boundary:** “Never,” “anyone,” and “carefully selected” are vague or absolute claims that may be contradicted by processors, legal demands, security events, affiliates, or actual practice.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-recipient-disclosure-gap; substantiated-sharing-and-control-required`.

### `LGW-C433` — cookie explanations must distinguish storage, access, purpose, parties, and control

- **Locator:** Interlude 4, model-policy cookie section, printed page 258; PDF page 288.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The sample explains a cookie as a small piece of information used for recognition, cart state, personalization, and understanding site use, and claims it does not provide name or email.
- **Candidate implication:** Disclose cookie or storage name, party, data, identifier relation, purpose, duration, access, recipients, consent category, control, dependency, and consequence of refusal.
- **Boundary:** The sample is technically and legally incomplete: identifiers may be personal data, cookies can be linked to profiles, and “our computers” obscures browsers, devices, vendors, and cross-context processing.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-historical-cookie-model-gap; current-storage-and-tracking-disclosure-required`.

### `LGW-C434` — children's privacy cannot be reduced to an age-threshold promise

- **Locator:** Interlude 4, model-policy children section, printed page 258; PDF page 288.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The sample says the organization does not knowingly sell to or collect from children under 13 and asks guardians to contact it for removal.
- **Candidate implication:** Model service audience, jurisdictional age rules, age assurance, guardian roles, data minimization, profiling and advertising restrictions, safeguarding, child-accessible notice, deletion, preservation duties, and remedy.
- **Boundary:** “Knowingly,” “sell to,” a single under-13 threshold, and contact-based removal do not cover current global children's rights, accidental collection, teens, school contexts, safety, verification, or erasure limits.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-outdated-children-privacy-model; jurisdictional-safeguarding-contract-required`.

### `LGW-C435` — a contact path is not a complete rights or remedy system

- **Locator:** Interlude 4, model-policy closing, printed page 258; PDF page 288.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The sample ends with contact information so visitors can continue the conversation and ask questions.
- **Candidate implication:** Provide purpose-specific accessible channels for questions, rights requests, complaints, appeal, regulator contact, urgent security or safety reports, status, confirmation, deadlines, identity verification, and escalation.
- **Boundary:** Generic “contact us” may lack staffing, authority, privacy, language access, response commitment, case continuity, or an alternative for people unable to use the channel.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-human-contact-foundation; operated-rights-and-remedy-workflow-required`.

### `LGW-C436` — Interlude 4 establishes a clarity obligation but not current legal-content governance

- **Locator:** Interlude 4, printed pages 253–258; PDF pages 283–288.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The interlude argues that legal content can be accurate, sufficient, plain, conversational, structured, and approachable, using privacy policy examples to demonstrate the method.
- **Candidate implication:** Retain plain-language structure while adding authoritative proposition lineage, jurisdiction, legal basis, actual-system parity, consent, rights, fairness, versioning, accessibility, localization, security, retention, enforcement, and remedy.
- **Boundary:** The model policy and cited requirements are from 2012 or earlier and are not reusable legal advice. Current primary legal and implementation contracts remain unverified because the Developer Index interface is unavailable.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-legal-clarity-foundation; current-jurisdictional-governance-and-counsel-required`.

### `LGW-C437` — links are route contracts, not decorated phrases

- **Locator:** Chapter 12, printed pages 259–260; PDF pages 289–290.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Links connect pages within and beyond a site, and their wording should help people understand where they can go.
- **Candidate implication:** Model source context, purpose, destination identity, route type, state, access requirements, expected result, return path, and owner for every link.
- **Boundary:** Clear words cannot make an unavailable, unsafe, stale, unauthorized, deceptive, or inaccessible destination valid.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-route-contract-foundation; runtime-destination-governance-required`.

### `LGW-C438` — unfamiliar branded names need descriptive context

- **Locator:** Chapter 12, printed pages 260–261; PDF pages 290–291.
- **Documented practitioner position:** A program or product name alone may be meaningless to people who have not already learned the organization's vocabulary.
- **Candidate implication:** Pair proprietary names with scope, audience, purpose, outcome, or task language and maintain aliases to the canonical entity.
- **Boundary:** A descriptive replacement can erase a legally required, culturally important, or recognized name; test recognition across new and returning audiences, locales, and search contexts.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-brand-plus-purpose-pattern; canonical-name-preservation-required`.

### `LGW-C439` — link and destination labels should preserve one promise

- **Locator:** Chapter 12, printed pages 261–262; PDF pages 291–292.
- **Documented practitioner position:** People check the destination headline after activating a link to confirm that they arrived where expected; exact or close wording supports confidence.
- **Candidate implication:** Intentionally map route label, link text, accessible name, document title, primary heading, breadcrumb, preview, and destination state to one canonical proposition.
- **Boundary:** Literal duplication is not always appropriate across compact navigation, translated surfaces, stateful actions, or contextual labels; semantic parity matters more than string identity.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-launch-land-parity; intentional-cross-surface-mapping-required`.

### `LGW-C440` — route confirmation must include identity, state, and scope

- **Locator:** Chapter 12, printed pages 261–262; PDF pages 291–292.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Matching names helps visitors answer whether they reached the expected page.
- **Candidate implication:** Confirm destination identity together with tenant, account, role, environment, locale, record, step, filter, permissions, and current state where material.
- **Boundary:** A matching headline can conceal wrong data, wrong account, wrong jurisdiction, stale state, or a deceptive destination.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-label-confirmation-base; full-destination-state-contract-required`.

### `LGW-C441` — historical SEO claims do not govern semantic parity

- **Locator:** Chapter 12, printed page 261; PDF page 291.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The chapter says matching linked and destination headlines may help search-engine optimization.
- **Candidate implication:** Preserve route parity for human prediction and machine consistency, then evaluate current search behavior separately from primary documentation.
- **Boundary:** The chapter offers no current algorithm evidence, and search ranking cannot define link quality, truth, accessibility, or user benefit.
- **Promotion status:** `limited-historical-seo-claim; human-route-integrity-controls`.

### `LGW-C442` — action links should expose the available action and object

- **Locator:** Chapter 12, printed page 262; PDF page 292.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Verb phrases such as “Look up a company” can communicate action more readily than isolated nouns.
- **Candidate implication:** Type action label, object, preconditions, authorization, commitment, expected result, reversibility, progress, failure, and recovery.
- **Boundary:** Verb-first grammar does not prove the element is a link rather than a button, that the action occurs immediately, or that it is safe and available.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-action-object-labeling; semantic-role-and-state-required`.

### `LGW-C443` — noun links are contextual, not inherently unusable

- **Locator:** Chapter 12, printed page 262; PDF page 292.
- **Documented practitioner position:** A historical study is cited in which action phrases reduced hesitation compared with single-noun links, especially among older adults.
- **Candidate implication:** Test noun and verb labels within the actual navigation hierarchy, task, population, language, and component role.
- **Boundary:** One historical study does not make nouns universally defective or age a sufficient behavior proxy; established destinations and categories often use nouns appropriately.
- **Promotion status:** `limited-formative-label-evidence; contextual-behavioral-validation-required`.

### `LGW-C444` — generic links fail when separated from surrounding prose

- **Locator:** Chapter 12, printed pages 262–265; PDF pages 292–295.
- **Documented practitioner position:** “Click here,” “More,” “Read more,” and repeated “Learn more” do not identify destinations, especially in scanned or extracted link lists.
- **Candidate implication:** Ensure each accessible name communicates distinguishing topic, action, object, destination, file, or outcome within its necessary programmatic context.
- **Boundary:** Visible text need not carry every contextual word when a correctly associated heading or label provides it, but hidden additions must not contradict the visible promise or burden speech users.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-standalone-link-purpose; programmatic-context-and-name-parity-required`.

### `LGW-C445` — device-specific wording should not define the action

- **Locator:** Chapter 12, printed page 263; PDF page 293.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The chapter says “Click here” is unnecessary because visual styling already indicates a link.
- **Candidate implication:** Name the destination or outcome rather than a mouse action and support activation through keyboard, touch, switch, voice, and assistive technology.
- **Boundary:** Visual affordance alone is insufficient; link semantics, focus, contrast, underline or other redundant cues, target size, and voice-disambiguating names still matter.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-input-neutral-labeling; multimodal-affordance-required`.

### `LGW-C446` — repeated action cards need distinct accessible names

- **Locator:** Chapter 12, job-site example, printed pages 263–264; PDF pages 293–294.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Parallel calls to action are useful only when each tells people what they will get; a lone “Learn more” breaks that pattern.
- **Candidate implication:** Derive each card action name from its associated item and outcome while preventing duplicate names that resolve to different destinations.
- **Boundary:** Appending hidden card titles mechanically can create verbose speech or name/visible-label divergence; association should be implemented and tested at component level.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-distinct-card-action-pattern; component-accessible-name-contract-required`.

### `LGW-C447` — “see all” requires a named set and truthful scope

- **Locator:** Chapter 12, printed pages 264–265; PDF pages 294–295.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Links such as “See all help and support” are more informative than “See all” because they name the set.
- **Candidate implication:** Include collection identity, filter or scope, item count when reliable, destination type, and preserved state where those affect the result.
- **Boundary:** “All” is a completeness claim and may be false under permissions, pagination, personalization, inventory, locale, or dynamic availability.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-named-collection-link; completeness-and-state-verification-required`.

### `LGW-C448` — embedded links create optional branches, not automatic distraction

- **Locator:** Chapter 12, printed pages 265–267; PDF pages 295–297.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The chapter generally discourages inline links because they may interrupt reading and move people away before a thought is complete, while allowing them for browsing contexts.
- **Candidate implication:** Choose inline, adjacent, end-of-section, footnote, disclosure, preview, or related-link placement according to dependency, interruption cost, reading purpose, return path, and need for immediate source access.
- **Boundary:** “Always a distraction” is too broad: citations, definitions, evidence, exceptions, and required context may be most useful inline, and moving them away can conceal provenance or increase effort.
- **Promotion status:** `limited-placement-default; task-and-evidence-dependent-linking-required`.

### `LGW-C449` — browse and complete are different link-density modes

- **Locator:** Chapter 12, Wikipedia example, printed page 266; PDF page 296.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Dense embedded linking may suit exploratory browsing because people expect topics to branch and connect.
- **Candidate implication:** Define whether a surface primarily supports exploration, study, decision, procedure, transaction, monitoring, or recovery and govern link density accordingly.
- **Boundary:** A person may switch modes on the same surface; assumed “browsing” does not justify clutter, tracking, inaccessible focus sequences, or loss of task context.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-task-mode-distinction; mixed-mode-and-access-testing-required`.

### `LGW-C450` — definition links require disclosure and stable interaction behavior

- **Locator:** Chapter 12, printed page 266; PDF page 296.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Inline definition links are treated as acceptable when they clarify without navigating away.
- **Candidate implication:** Define whether a term opens inline disclosure, tooltip, popover, dialog, or route, with keyboard and pointer triggers, focus, dismissal, expanded state, relation, announcement, deep link, and fallback.
- **Boundary:** A small window can obscure content, trap focus, fail on touch or zoom, disappear before reading, or keep essential meaning behind interaction.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-contextual-definition-option; disclosure-runtime-contract-required`.

### `LGW-C451` — adjacent related links should preserve source continuity

- **Locator:** Chapter 12, spa example, printed pages 266–267; PDF pages 296–297.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Links placed after a coherent text segment can let people finish the thought before choosing a related path.
- **Candidate implication:** Group related routes by purpose, preserve source position and return behavior, and distinguish prerequisite evidence from optional continuation.
- **Boundary:** End placement can make necessary sources, definitions, warnings, or alternatives invisible at the point of reliance.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-coherent-segment-placement-option; dependency-and-return-contract-required`.

### `LGW-C452` — visual markers and link text should form one target when they express one action

- **Locator:** Chapter 12, printed pages 267–268; PDF pages 297–298.
- **Documented practitioner position:** When bullets, arrows, numbers, thumbnails, or icons visually belong to a link, people may try to activate the marker, so the full apparent unit should be interactive.
- **Candidate implication:** Align visual, pointer, focus, accessible-name, and activation boundaries for a single action and provide an adequate target without overlapping neighbors.
- **Boundary:** Enlarging the target must not nest interactive elements, create accidental activation, hide separate controls, or make screen-reader grouping ambiguous.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-perceived-target-alignment; interaction-boundary-validation-required`.

### `LGW-C453` — link affordance needs redundant cues across states

- **Locator:** Chapter 12, printed pages 268–269; PDF pages 298–299.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Links should be visually obvious, and link color should not be reused for noninteractive status text.
- **Candidate implication:** Define default, hover, focus-visible, active, visited, disabled or unavailable, loading, and error behavior using semantics plus redundant visual cues that survive themes and user settings.
- **Boundary:** Color alone cannot convey interactivity or state, and “disabled link” may be the wrong semantic model; current primary implementation contracts remain unverified.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-distinct-affordance-principle; full-state-and-noncolor-contract-required`.

### `LGW-C454` — visited state can reduce memory burden in exploratory collections

- **Locator:** Chapter 12, printed pages 268–269; PDF pages 298–299.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Distinguishing visited links helps people recognize where they have been and avoid reopening or skipping destinations through memory error.
- **Candidate implication:** Use visited state where navigation history supports comparison or exploration and test meaning, contrast, privacy, history clearing, and route identity.
- **Boundary:** Visited styling is not always useful in transactional apps, can expose sensitive browsing on shared devices, is constrained by browser privacy behavior, and does not prove task completion or content comprehension.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-exploratory-history-support; contextual-privacy-and-state-review-required`.

### `LGW-C455` — a visited link is not a completed task

- **Locator:** Chapter 12, printed page 269; PDF page 299.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Visited color is presented as a cue for paths already tried.
- **Candidate implication:** Keep browser visit history distinct from application states such as viewed, read, completed, submitted, approved, failed, or needs attention.
- **Boundary:** Conflating these states can cause missed obligations, duplicate actions, false progress, or sensitive disclosure.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-visited-versus-domain-state-distinction; typed-progress-model-required`.

### `LGW-C456` — link recovery requires more than remembering color

- **Locator:** Chapter 12, printed page 269; PDF page 299.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Clear writing and visited-state cues help people recover after choosing a wrong path.
- **Candidate implication:** Provide predictable back behavior, breadcrumbs or context, preserved filters and form state, undo where applicable, alternate routes, error explanation, and safe return from external or authenticated transitions.
- **Boundary:** Visited color cannot restore lost input, session, scroll, focus, permissions, or transaction state.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-wrong-route-recovery-goal; state-preserving-navigation-required`.

### `LGW-C457` — external destinations need ownership, risk, and return disclosure

- **Locator:** Chapter 12, general internal/external-link framing, printed pages 259 and 265; PDF pages 289 and 295.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Links connect both within a site and to other sites.
- **Candidate implication:** Identify external ownership when material and govern destination verification, privacy/referrer exposure, authentication, language, availability, accessibility, security, return path, and fallback.
- **Boundary:** The chapter does not provide a modern external-handoff contract; identical styling can hide a consequential change of operator or protection.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-external-handoff-gap; verified-cross-boundary-transition-required`.

### `LGW-C458` — destination type and commitment should be knowable before activation

- **Locator:** Chapter 12, seven-guideline framework, printed pages 259–270; PDF pages 289–300.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Meaningful labels should communicate what happens after activation.
- **Candidate implication:** Disclose when a target is a download, external site, new context, communication channel, authentication gate, payment, submission, destructive action, or unavailable destination, including format and size where useful.
- **Boundary:** Link text alone cannot safely carry every behavior; component semantics, adjacent metadata, confirmation, and runtime state may be required.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-preactivation-predictability; typed-destination-and-commitment-contract-required`.

### `LGW-C459` — localization requires concept parity, not copied keywords

- **Locator:** Chapter 12, launch/land and meaningful-link guidance, printed pages 261–265; PDF pages 291–295.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Link and destination should share recognizable words and meaning.
- **Candidate implication:** Preserve canonical concept, intent, destination identity, grammatical role, and distinguishing terms across locale-specific labels and routes.
- **Boundary:** Exact lexical repetition may be ungrammatical or unclear across languages, scripts, inflection, direction, and cultural naming conventions.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-concept-parity-principle; locale-specific-transcreation-required`.

### `LGW-C460` — link analytics do not establish label quality or user benefit

- **Locator:** Chapter 12, SEO and route-choice claims, printed pages 261 and 265; PDF pages 291 and 295.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The chapter connects meaningful, matching link text with discoverability and reduced hesitation.
- **Candidate implication:** Pair activation data with destination match, wrong-turn rate, return, task success, comprehension, access, recovery, and downstream harm.
- **Boundary:** Click-through can rise because of prominence, ambiguity, curiosity, manipulation, or distribution and does not prove the link fulfilled its promise.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-outcome-measurement-boundary; no-click-proxy-for-quality`.

### `LGW-C461` — link collections require coherent names and differentiated destinations

- **Locator:** Chapter 12, link-list and university-list examples, printed pages 263 and 269; PDF pages 293 and 299.
- **Documented practitioner position:** People visually or aurally scan collections of links, so repeated generic names make comparison and selection difficult.
- **Candidate implication:** Validate each collection as a set: unique accessible names, parallel semantic granularity, predictable ordering, current destinations, manageable focus sequence, and no duplicate-label divergence.
- **Boundary:** Uniqueness alone can produce unnecessarily verbose labels; shared contextual headings may legitimately disambiguate concise visible text.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-link-set-coherence; contextual-brevity-and-distinction-required`.

### `LGW-C462` — Chapter 12 does not cover link security and privacy behavior

- **Locator:** Chapter 12, printed pages 259–270; PDF pages 289–300.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The chapter focuses on wording, placement, target area, and visited-state cues.
- **Candidate implication:** Add URL validation, scheme restrictions, redirect and open-redirect controls, referrer policy, opener isolation, download integrity, phishing resistance, sensitive-query handling, logging minimization, expiry, and incident response.
- **Boundary:** A semantically excellent link can still leak data, execute an unsafe scheme, impersonate a destination, deliver malware, or expose private history.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-security-and-privacy-gap; secure-link-runtime-required`.

### `LGW-C463` — Chapter 12 supplies link-writing heuristics, not a complete navigation runtime

- **Locator:** Chapter 12 and summary, printed pages 259–270; PDF pages 289–300.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The chapter recommends descriptive branded links, launch/land parity, verb-led actions, meaningful names, deliberate placement, aligned targets, obvious affordance, and visited-state cues.
- **Candidate implication:** Retain preactivation prediction and postnavigation confirmation while adding semantic role, accessible name, route state, destination type, external handoff, focus, keyboard and voice behavior, security, privacy, localization, analytics, expiry, and recovery.
- **Boundary:** Embedded-link and visited-color advice is contextual, examples are historical, SEO claims are unverified, and current primary browser and accessibility contracts remain unknown because the Developer Index interface is unavailable.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-link-content-foundation; modern-navigation-security-and-access-runtime-required`.

### `LGW-C464` — every illustration needs a declared communication purpose

- **Locator:** Chapter 13, printed pages 271–273; PDF pages 301–303.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Illustrations should be planned around purpose, audience, conversation, type, and the specific value they add.
- **Candidate implication:** Type each asset as exact representation, selection aid, procedural support, concept explanation, data display, orientation, evidence, identity, or mood, with an owner and success criterion.
- **Boundary:** Persona imagination and organizational intent do not establish user value, interpretation, accessibility, truth, or permission to publish.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-purpose-first-media-model; evidence-and-governance-required`.

### `LGW-C465` — media purpose determines its semantic equivalent

- **Locator:** Chapter 13, purpose continuum, printed pages 272–273; PDF pages 302–303.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The chapter distinguishes exact-item images, self-service diagrams, process illustrations, charts or maps, and mood pictures along a representational-to-emotional continuum.
- **Candidate implication:** Derive caption, alternative, transcript, data table, controls, provenance, and interaction behavior from the asset's actual purpose rather than its file type.
- **Boundary:** One asset may serve several purposes, and emotional or decorative intent can still affect meaning, inclusion, trust, and choice.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-purpose-to-equivalent-mapping; multi-purpose-analysis-required`.

### `LGW-C466` — video is a compound time-based content type

- **Locator:** Chapter 13, printed pages 271–272; PDF pages 301–302.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Video can tell stories and demonstrate tasks; the chapter recommends concise, simple, interesting segments with informative titles, transcripts, and captions.
- **Candidate implication:** Model video with title, purpose, chapters, transcript, captions, audio description, language, speaker identity, source, controls, poster, duration, file or stream variants, rights, and fallback.
- **Boundary:** The book's three-to-five-minute and bandwidth limits are dated heuristics; brevity cannot replace complete instructions, evidence, safety, or an accessible alternative.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-time-based-media-foundation; current-multimodal-runtime-required`.

### `LGW-C467` — transcript and captions serve different access needs

- **Locator:** Chapter 13, accessibility note, printed page 272; PDF page 302.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The chapter calls for a transcript for people who cannot see and captions for people who cannot hear.
- **Candidate implication:** Provide synchronized captions for speech and meaningful sound, a transcript for searchable and asynchronous text access, and audio description or equivalent visual narration when visuals carry meaning.
- **Boundary:** A transcript alone does not describe visual action, and captions alone do not serve every deaf, blind, deafblind, cognitive, language, or situational need.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-caption-transcript-base; audio-description-and-mode-parity-required`.

### `LGW-C468` — historical video SEO guidance is not a media-quality contract

- **Locator:** Chapter 13, printed page 272; PDF page 302.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The chapter recommends keywords in filenames and descriptions and cites hosted-view counts as an SEO benefit.
- **Candidate implication:** Maintain descriptive metadata for discovery and asset identity, then verify current search contracts independently.
- **Boundary:** Keywords, distribution, and views do not prove accuracy, accessibility, comprehension, consent, rights, or user benefit; platform advice from 2012 is not current authority.
- **Promotion status:** `limited-historical-discovery-advice; no-seo-or-view-proxy-for-quality`.

### `LGW-C469` — exact-item media should answer decision-relevant questions

- **Locator:** Chapter 13, product example, printed pages 273–274; PDF pages 303–304.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Product views should show what prospective buyers need to know, such as appearance, capacity, compartments, or fit.
- **Candidate implication:** Derive an asset shot list from observed comparison questions and include scale, dimensions, variants, states, materials, limitations, and representative conditions where material.
- **Boundary:** Images can conceal defects, exaggerate scale or color, omit included items, show unavailable variants, or imply performance they do not substantiate.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-decision-relevant-product-media; representation-and-claim-verification-required`.

### `LGW-C470` — indirect customer signals are discovery evidence, not final requirements

- **Locator:** Chapter 13, printed page 274; PDF page 304.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The chapter suggests customer-facing staff, social conversations, and competitor imagery as sources for learning what people want to see.
- **Candidate implication:** Triangulate support, returns, search, reviews, interviews, observation, accessibility needs, and product evidence with provenance and privacy controls.
- **Boundary:** Staff reports, competitors, and social posts can be biased, unrepresentative, promotional, manipulated, or collected without appropriate purpose and consent.
- **Promotion status:** `limited-discovery-inputs; triangulated-and-governed-research-required`.

### `LGW-C471` — self-service illustrations must expose selectable relationships

- **Locator:** Chapter 13, seating example, printed pages 274–275; PDF pages 304–305.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Seating charts and similar diagrams can help people compare options and complete tasks without assistance.
- **Candidate implication:** Represent item identity, location, availability, price, view, accessibility features, selection, focus, zoom, legend, update state, and equivalent nonvisual comparison.
- **Boundary:** A visual map may exclude blind, low-vision, motor, cognitive, or small-screen users and may overstate real-time availability or accessibility.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-visual-self-service-goal; operable-equivalent-selection-required`.

### `LGW-C472` — accessibility claims in diagrams require verified attributes

- **Locator:** Chapter 13, seating-chart example, printed page 275; PDF page 305.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Showing wheelchair-accessible seating is presented as making patrons welcome.
- **Candidate implication:** Provide verified feature definitions, dimensions, companion seating, route, transfer, sightline, hearing support, restroom and entrance access, availability, booking method, and assistance contact.
- **Boundary:** A wheelchair symbol alone cannot establish suitability, route accessibility, current inventory, or the needs of all disabled patrons.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-accessible-option-visibility; verified-feature-and-journey-detail-required`.

### `LGW-C473` — source-document illustrations can reveal upstream content defects

- **Locator:** Chapter 13, PIN-document example, printed pages 274–276; PDF pages 304–306.
- **Documented practitioner position:** A picture can show where to find a value on a paper document, but redesigning the paper source may be the better solution.
- **Candidate implication:** Use visual help as a bounded bridge while tracking the upstream artifact, field identity, version, redesign owner, migration, and retirement condition.
- **Boundary:** Screenshots of documents can become stale, expose personal data, fail magnification, and normalize a broken source rather than fixing it.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-upstream-defect-diagnostic; temporary-bridge-and-source-remediation-required`.

### `LGW-C474` — process imagery should preserve sequence and state

- **Locator:** Chapter 13, loan-process example, printed pages 276–277; PDF pages 306–307.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Simple pictures paired with words can make a process noticeable and memorable.
- **Candidate implication:** Model step identity, order, actor, action, duration, prerequisites, branch, current status, expected result, exception, and recovery in both visual and textual forms.
- **Boundary:** A three-stage diagram may oversimplify eligibility, underwriting, cost, delay, rejection, obligations, and recourse, particularly in financial services.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-process-overview-pattern; material-stage-and-risk-completeness-required`.

### `LGW-C475` — progressive visual layers need explicit state and equivalence

- **Locator:** Chapter 13, weather-map example, printed page 277; PDF page 307.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Letting people choose which map layers to display can keep a complex visual simple.
- **Candidate implication:** Define available layers, default and persisted state, legend, source, timestamp, units, keyboard controls, focus, announcements, contrast, combined-state validity, and nonvisual data access.
- **Boundary:** User control cannot justify hiding required hazards, uncertainty, attribution, or essential context; layer combinations may become inaccessible or misleading.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-progressive-visual-detail; stateful-equivalent-layer-contract-required`.

### `LGW-C476` — chart titles can state the main proposition

- **Locator:** Chapter 13, beverage comparison, printed pages 277–279; PDF pages 307–309.
- **Documented practitioner position:** A message-led title, clear axes, and a chart can make a numerical comparison more apparent than dense prose.
- **Candidate implication:** Store chart question, key proposition, measure, unit, population, period, source, uncertainty, categories, transformations, and supporting data.
- **Boundary:** A title can overstate causality, certainty, representativeness, or importance and must remain consistent with the full data and limitations.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-message-led-data-display; evidence-and-claim-governance-required`.

### `LGW-C477` — chart alternatives need the data and relationships, not a vague description

- **Locator:** Chapter 13, accessibility note and chart example, printed pages 277–279; PDF pages 307–309.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The chapter says chart information should be explained in alternative text for people listening to the page.
- **Candidate implication:** Provide a concise text alternative for the main point and a programmatically accessible data table or structured description for values, comparisons, trends, uncertainty, and source.
- **Boundary:** An image alt attribute is usually too limited for complex charts; duplicating all data into one accessible name creates unusable speech and poor navigation.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-equivalent-data-intent; layered-chart-alternative-required`.

### `LGW-C478` — zero-baseline advice is contextual, while truthful scale is mandatory

- **Locator:** Chapter 13, printed pages 278–279; PDF pages 308–309.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The chapter calls starting at zero critical for truthful comparisons and warns that partial axes can exaggerate differences.
- **Candidate implication:** Justify axis domain, scale, transformation, truncation, normalization, aggregation, and annotation according to the measure and analytical question.
- **Boundary:** Zero is appropriate for many magnitude comparisons but not universal for intervals, changes, distributions, logarithmic data, or other legitimate analyses; disclosure and proportional representation control.
- **Promotion status:** `limited-zero-baseline-rule; strong-truthful-scale-requirement`.

### `LGW-C479` — mood images make claims even when they show no product fact

- **Locator:** Chapter 13, printed pages 279–280; PDF pages 309–310.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Photos intended to evoke emotion communicate brand messages such as belonging, warmth, trustworthiness, or impact.
- **Candidate implication:** Treat mood imagery as a claim-bearing content unit with intended message, affected audiences, evidence, provenance, consent, rights, expiry, and interpretation testing.
- **Boundary:** Emotional imagery can manipulate, stereotype, tokenize, falsely imply endorsement or outcomes, or substitute affect for evidence.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-mood-as-meaning-principle; claim-and-manipulation-review-required`.

### `LGW-C480` — donation and impact imagery requires causal and rights substantiation

- **Locator:** Chapter 13, conservation example, printed page 279; PDF page 309.
- **Documented practitioner position:** A wildlife photograph is shown as reinforcing the message that financial support makes a difference.
- **Candidate implication:** Link fundraising media to verified program, location, subject, photographer, license, consent where applicable, impact evidence, restrictions, and current use of funds.
- **Boundary:** A compelling image does not prove that a donation caused the depicted outcome or that the subject, place, and program are connected.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-fundraising-media-risk; causal-provenance-and-rights-required`.

### `LGW-C481` — representation must be truthful, not aspirational theater

- **Locator:** Chapter 13, printed pages 282–284; PDF pages 312–314.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Images of people influence whether visitors believe content is for them; the chapter recommends broad representation but recounts a failed attempt to depict diversity the organization did not have.
- **Candidate implication:** Govern representation with current demographic and role evidence, participant consent, non-tokenizing context, affected-community review, local relevance, and a truthful relationship to the organization.
- **Boundary:** Demographic mirroring cannot define belonging, and visual diversity can conceal unequal power, access, outcomes, or internal conditions.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-truthful-representation-principle; anti-tokenism-and-equity-review-required`.

### `LGW-C482` — identity inference from images is probabilistic and sensitive

- **Locator:** Chapter 13, printed pages 282–284; PDF pages 312–314.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The chapter frames diversity through perceived ethnicity, gender, age, culture, and location.
- **Candidate implication:** Describe only relevant observable context and self-identified roles where authorized; avoid assigning protected traits, health, family status, or identity from appearance.
- **Boundary:** Viewers and metadata systems can misgender, racialize, age, diagnose, or stereotype people; representation research must protect privacy and dignity.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-sensitive-inference-boundary; self-identification-and-data-minimization-required`.

### `LGW-C483` — image interpretation must be tested in full context

- **Locator:** Chapter 13, printed pages 283–284; PDF pages 313–314.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The chapter recommends showing complete prototype pages to representative participants and asking what messages or adjectives images convey rather than whether they are liked.
- **Candidate implication:** Test interpretation, relevance, trustworthiness, inclusion, action, recall, and harm with the image, copy, layout, state, and destination together.
- **Boundary:** Small qualitative tests reveal possible readings but do not prove population meaning, causal effect, cultural parity, or absence of harm.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-contextual-interpretation-testing; bounded-formative-evidence`.

### `LGW-C484` — ad-like styling can hide legitimate content

- **Locator:** Chapter 13, printed pages 284–285; PDF pages 314–315.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Colorful graphic boxes, especially in conventional ad positions, may be ignored when people assume they lead away from the requested content.
- **Candidate implication:** Distinguish first-party tasks, notices, recommendations, sponsorship, and advertising through explicit semantics, labels, ownership, placement, and behavior.
- **Boundary:** “Banner blindness” varies by layout, population, task, and time; legitimate content should not imitate editorial material to evade ad recognition.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-ad-recognition-risk; transparent-content-and-sponsorship-typing-required`.

### `LGW-C485` — motion competes for attention and can cause access harm

- **Locator:** Chapter 13, printed pages 284–286; PDF pages 314–316.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Blinking, rolling, waving, or wandering media distracts from the user's task and is especially difficult for slow readers and people using magnification.
- **Candidate implication:** Avoid nonessential motion and provide pause, stop, hide, reduced-motion, focus, timing, persistence, and no-loss alternatives for moving content.
- **Boundary:** The chapter's savannah explanation is rhetorical, not evidence; motion risks also include vestibular effects, seizures, cognition, attention, screen magnification, and automated timing.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-nonessential-motion-warning; current-motion-access-contract-required`.

### `LGW-C486` — animation is justified by information change, not novelty

- **Locator:** Chapter 13, printed page 286; PDF page 316.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Animation is recommended only when motion helps explain content, especially a process.
- **Candidate implication:** Record what temporal relation motion conveys and provide user-controlled play, pause, replay, step, speed, scrub, text, static sequence, and status.
- **Boundary:** A useful animation can still be inaccessible, too fast, unlocalizable, unsafe, or impossible to study; motion should not be the only carrier of sequence or change.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-informational-animation-test; user-control-and-static-equivalence-required`.

### `LGW-C487` — the knot example demonstrates synchronized multimodal steps

- **Locator:** Chapter 13, knot-animation example, printed page 286; PDF page 316.
- **Documented practitioner position:** A process animation is praised for supporting continuous playback or stepwise control while synchronizing the visual state with progressively emphasized text.
- **Candidate implication:** Synchronize stable step IDs, visuals, text, controls, focus, current step, replay, speed, and alternate static instructions across modes.
- **Boundary:** Darkening text alone may not convey current state nonvisually, and historical screenshots do not prove keyboard, screen-reader, touch, zoom, or reduced-motion support.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-synchronized-process-pattern; implemented-state-equivalence-required`.

### `LGW-C488` — alternative text depends on image purpose and surrounding context

- **Locator:** Chapter 13, printed pages 286–287; PDF pages 316–317.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Meaningful images should be annotated according to the substantive or emotional purpose they serve.
- **Candidate implication:** Decide whether an image is informative, functional, complex, decorative, redundant, or part of a group, then author the smallest context-complete equivalent.
- **Boundary:** Not every image should be described, and describing a mood image can add unwanted interpretation, identity inference, redundancy, or verbosity.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-purpose-based-alternative; decorative-and-context-decision-required`.

### `LGW-C489` — the telephone test is useful but insufficient for image accessibility

- **Locator:** Chapter 13, printed page 287; PDF page 317.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The chapter cites imagining the page read aloud over a telephone as a test for what an image description should convey.
- **Candidate implication:** Use the exercise to draft meaning, then test semantics, reading order, navigation, name and description, verbosity, interaction, braille, speech, zoom, and user journeys.
- **Boundary:** A hypothetical spoken description cannot validate programmatic implementation, complex data exploration, controls, touch, cognition, deafblind access, or participant preference.
- **Promotion status:** `limited-authoring-heuristic; assistive-technology-and-user-validation-required`.

### `LGW-C490` — decorative images need explicit nonannouncement behavior

- **Locator:** Chapter 13, purpose range and accessibility section, printed pages 272–273 and 286–287; PDF pages 302–303 and 316–317.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The chapter emphasizes meaningful alternatives but does not separately govern decorative or redundant images.
- **Candidate implication:** Mark genuinely decorative assets so assistive technology ignores them, while ensuring CSS backgrounds, icons, generated content, and redundant thumbnails carry no missing function or information.
- **Boundary:** An image is not decorative merely because a writer calls it mood or brand; if it changes interpretation, trust, identity, or action, its effect remains part of the content.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-decorative-media-gap; explicit-semantic-suppression-and-review-required`.

### `LGW-C491` — media assets require rights, consent, and provenance records

- **Locator:** Chapter 13, examples and credited wildlife photo, printed pages 273–286; PDF pages 303–316.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The chapter uses many third-party screenshots and notes permission for at least one credited photograph.
- **Candidate implication:** Track creator, subject, capture date, source, license, consent or release, edits, synthetic status, expiry, territories, accessibility assets, attribution, and permitted channels.
- **Boundary:** Permission granted for this book does not transfer to the repository, product, model training, marketing, or derivative reuse.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-rights-provenance-need; no-transferred-reuse-authority`.

### `LGW-C492` — screenshots require privacy, state, and maintenance governance

- **Locator:** Chapter 13, paper-document and web-interface examples, printed pages 274–286; PDF pages 304–316.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Screenshots and document images are used to orient people and demonstrate interfaces.
- **Candidate implication:** Record product and version, locale, account state, data classification, redaction, capture date, crop, annotation, alternative, owner, review trigger, and replacement path.
- **Boundary:** Screenshots can expose personal or confidential data, preserve unsafe examples, drift from the current interface, and become unusable under zoom, reflow, translation, or theme changes.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-screenshot-content-type; privacy-and-lifecycle-controls-required`.

### `LGW-C493` — responsive media needs art direction, not indiscriminate shrinking

- **Locator:** Chapter 13, size guidance, printed pages 281–282; PDF pages 311–312.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Large images can displace critical content and small thumbnails can become unintelligible; the chapter recommends testing across representative screens.
- **Candidate implication:** Define responsive source, crop, focal point, resolution, density, intrinsic dimensions, reading order, layout shift, zoom, caption relation, and alternate presentation for each viewport and mode.
- **Boundary:** “Above the fold,” monitor resolution, and desktop/mobile assumptions are historical proxies; user zoom, text enlargement, orientation, split screen, and reflow create many effective viewports.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-size-and-clarity-principle; responsive-art-direction-contract-required`.

### `LGW-C494` — media performance is an end-to-end user constraint

- **Locator:** Chapter 13, video and image-size guidance, printed pages 271–272 and 281; PDF pages 301–302 and 311.
