- **Documented practitioner position:** The chapter warns that media size and download time can impede access.
- **Candidate implication:** Budget bytes, requests, decode, dimensions, format, preload priority, lazy loading, connection variability, data cost, energy, caching, offline behavior, and failure fallback by purpose.
- **Boundary:** The fixed 2012 megabyte guidance is obsolete; aggressive optimization can degrade legibility, evidence, color, captions, or critical-task availability.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-performance-awareness; current-purpose-based-media-budget-required`.

### `LGW-C495` — media must be localized beyond translated captions

- **Locator:** Chapter 13, global representation guidance, printed pages 282–283; PDF pages 312–313.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The chapter recommends culturally and geographically appropriate imagery for different site versions.
- **Candidate implication:** Govern locale-specific symbols, gestures, people, clothing, settings, maps, reading direction, embedded text, units, date and number forms, narration, captions, audio description, and rights.
- **Boundary:** Swapping images by locale can stereotype populations, create unequal product evidence, or change material meaning; one “diverse” global asset is not automatically appropriate everywhere.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-cultural-context-recognition; locale-specific-equivalence-and-anti-stereotype-review-required`.

### `LGW-C496` — synthetic and edited media require authenticity disclosure and evidence controls

- **Locator:** Chapter 13, exact-item, mood, diversity, and credibility examples, printed pages 273–284; PDF pages 303–314.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The chapter assumes photographs and illustrations can communicate products, real participants, organizational character, and credibility.
- **Candidate implication:** Track capture or generation method, edits, compositing, model or tool, source inputs, authenticity evidence, disclosure, approval, and prohibited representational uses.
- **Boundary:** Contemporary generated or heavily edited media can depict nonexistent products, people, events, evidence, diversity, or outcomes; the 2012 chapter does not address this risk.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-modern-authenticity-gap; synthetic-media-provenance-and-disclosure-required`.

### `LGW-C497` — Chapter 13 supplies purpose-led media critique, not a complete multimodal runtime

- **Locator:** Chapter 13 and summary, printed pages 271–287; PDF pages 301–317.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The chapter organizes illustrations by purpose and recommends clarity, appropriate size, truthful diversity, non-ad-like presentation, restrained motion, useful animation, and meaningful alternatives.
- **Candidate implication:** Retain purpose-first selection while adding structured equivalents, captions, audio description, controls, semantics, responsive art direction, performance, data provenance, rights, consent, privacy, authenticity, localization, security, lifecycle, and multimodal testing.
- **Boundary:** File-size, SEO, zero-axis, demographic, and alt-text advice is incomplete or contextual; examples are historical; and current technical contracts remain unverified because the Developer Index interface is unavailable.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-media-content-foundation; modern-multimodal-governance-and-runtime-required`.

### `LGW-C498` — revision is part of authorship, not a cleanup after writing

- **Locator:** Chapter 14, printed pages 289–290; PDF pages 319–320.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Writing proceeds through drafting, rereading, editing, revising, checking, and proofreading rather than a perfect first attempt.
- **Candidate implication:** Model draft, substantive revision, copy edit, verification, review, approval, release, correction, and archive as explicit states with owners and evidence.
- **Boundary:** Repeated revision does not guarantee truth, inclusion, accessibility, legal validity, or completion; exit criteria and accountable release authority remain necessary.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-iterative-writing-foundation; governed-content-lifecycle-required`.

### `LGW-C499` — self-review checks intention against plausible interpretation

- **Locator:** Chapter 14, printed pages 290–291; PDF pages 320–321.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Writers should reread for intended meaning, alternate interpretation, consistency, tone, clarity, vocabulary, grammar, and spelling.
- **Candidate implication:** Use a structured self-review covering proposition, actor, audience, purpose, source, state, tone, terminology, access, locale, consequence, and unresolved questions.
- **Boundary:** Authors share their own assumptions and cannot serve as independent evidence that representative people, domain experts, or assistive technologies will interpret the content correctly.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-self-review-baseline; independent-validation-required`.

### `LGW-C500` — link checking requires resolved-route verification

- **Locator:** Chapter 14, printed page 291; PDF page 321.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Authors should activate every link and confirm that its destination matches the link's words.
- **Candidate implication:** Verify final URL, redirects, status, destination identity, route state, permissions, locale, authentication, fragment, external ownership, download metadata, focus, return path, and expiry.
- **Boundary:** A successful click in one reviewer session does not prove availability, safety, accessibility, or parity across roles, devices, locales, states, and future publication time.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-link-verification-base; automated-and-journey-level-checks-required`.

### `LGW-C501` — fact checking begins with provenance and contradiction analysis

- **Locator:** Chapter 14, printed pages 291–292; PDF pages 321–322.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Repetition, popularity, search prominence, community editing, or print publication does not prove truth; writers should examine authority, authorship, date, bias, and consistency.
- **Candidate implication:** Record each claim's source, authority, method, date, scope, uncertainty, conflicts, transformation, reviewer, and next review date.
- **Boundary:** Institutional type does not predict impartiality, and apparent consistency may reflect copied errors or shared incentives; source evaluation must be claim- and domain-specific.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-provenance-skepticism; claim-level-evidence-governance-required`.

### `LGW-C502` — dates and ownership metadata support scrutiny but do not prove trustworthiness

- **Locator:** Chapter 14, CDC example, printed page 292; PDF page 322.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Reviewed, updated, source, maintainer, and canonical URL metadata can help people assess currency and responsibility.
- **Candidate implication:** Expose published, reviewed, modified, effective, corrected, superseded, and next-review dates together with source, author, accountable owner, jurisdiction, canonical identity, and status.
- **Boundary:** Fresh dates can be automated or cosmetic, and named owners can lack authority; metadata is evidence to inspect, not a trust badge.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-content-provenance-fields; substantive-currency-verification-required`.

### `LGW-C503` — a rest interval is a cognitive aid, not a release control

- **Locator:** Chapter 14, printed pages 292–293; PDF pages 322–323.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Time away from a draft can help an author notice logic gaps, missing information, and typographical errors.
- **Candidate implication:** Allow reflection where risk and schedule permit while using independent review and automated gates for defects that familiarity may hide.
- **Boundary:** Rest is infeasible for some incidents and insufficient for high-consequence content; urgency requires predefined fast-path roles, evidence, approval, monitoring, and correction.
- **Promotion status:** `limited-cognitive-editing-technique; risk-based-release-controls-required`.

### `LGW-C504` — online correction needs explicit change propagation

- **Locator:** Chapter 14, printed page 293; PDF page 323.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Online content can be fixed and reposted, though repeated updates may annoy subscribers or expose errors.
- **Candidate implication:** Classify silent typo fixes, material corrections, updates, supersession, and retractions; preserve diffs, dates, reasons, approvals, affected channels, notifications, caches, feeds, and downstream copies.
- **Boundary:** Editability does not make errors harmless; silent changes can alter records, evidence, consent, legal effect, or decisions already made.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-online-correction-recognition; correction-and-propagation-lifecycle-required`.

### `LGW-C505` — reading aloud is a useful prose diagnostic, not accessibility testing

- **Locator:** Chapter 14, printed page 293; PDF page 323.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Reading aloud can reveal stumbling, excessive length, awkward rhythm, and unclear wording.
- **Candidate implication:** Use read-aloud review for rhythm and syntax, then test screen-reader pronunciation, navigation, names, descriptions, punctuation, language metadata, and interaction separately.
- **Boundary:** Author speech is not synthesized speech, braille, cognitive access, comprehension, localization, or a disabled-user journey.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-oral-editing-heuristic; no-substitute-for-access-validation`.

### `LGW-C506` — dictionaries and style guides are governed references, not final authorities

- **Locator:** Chapter 14, printed pages 293–294; PDF pages 323–324.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Writers should use dictionaries, handbooks, and an organizational style guide to avoid misspelling and misuse.
- **Candidate implication:** Bind reference source, edition, locale, domain, owner, precedence, exceptions, and review date, and distinguish language convention from product fact or legal rule.
- **Boundary:** Reference works can conflict, lag language change, encode exclusion, or be inapplicable to a community, jurisdiction, identity, or technical domain.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-reference-use; scoped-precedence-and-maintenance-required`.

### `LGW-C507` — automated spelling checks produce candidates, not decisions

- **Locator:** Chapter 14, printed page 294; PDF page 324.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Spell checkers accept valid but contextually wrong words and reject valid names or terms missing from their dictionaries.
- **Candidate implication:** Treat spelling, grammar, terminology, lint, link, accessibility, and policy tools as explainable findings with locale, confidence, suppression, ownership, and human review.
- **Boundary:** Automation can introduce false corrections, erase identities, mishandle code or domain terms, and create false confidence when checks pass.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-automation-limit; human-reviewed-multicheck-pipeline-required`.

### `LGW-C508` — proofreading is a distinct pass from substantive review

- **Locator:** Chapter 14, printed page 294; PDF page 324.
- **Documented practitioner position:** A backward line-by-line pass can help isolate individual words and catch typographical errors missed while reading for meaning.
- **Candidate implication:** Separate semantic, factual, structural, interaction, localization, accessibility, and legal review from final character-level and rendered-artifact proofreading.
- **Boundary:** Reverse reading can break context and cannot validate meaning, order, behavior, layout, truncation, or cross-surface consistency.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-distinct-proofreading-pass; multilayer-quality-review-required`.

### `LGW-C509` — additional reviewers reduce blind spots only when roles are defined

- **Locator:** Chapter 14, printed pages 289 and 294–296; PDF pages 319 and 324–326.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Sharing drafts with colleagues or early readers provides perspectives the author may miss.
- **Candidate implication:** Select reviewers by evidence need and affected perspective, declare their authority and conflicts, and record what each review can and cannot establish.
- **Boundary:** “Two sets of eyes” is not assurance; peers can share assumptions, defer to authority, overlook harms, or lack domain, accessibility, language, and lived-experience expertise.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-collaborative-review; competence-and-role-mapping-required`.

### `LGW-C510` — partial drafts support early alignment but can conceal system effects

- **Locator:** Chapter 14, printed page 295; PDF page 325.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Reviewing a small portion early can reveal problems in message, detail, tone, organization, and style before more work accumulates.
- **Candidate implication:** Use partial reviews for targeted hypotheses while scheduling whole-journey, cross-component, responsive, localized, and stateful validation before release.
- **Boundary:** An isolated fragment cannot prove route coherence, cumulative burden, interaction behavior, policy coverage, or consistency across the complete experience.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-early-slice-review; whole-system-validation-required`.

### `LGW-C511` — observed hesitation is diagnostic evidence, not a standalone rewrite rule

- **Locator:** Chapter 14, printed page 295; PDF page 325.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Relevant readers can read a draft aloud, and hesitation, stumbling, or rereading may identify wording to revise.
- **Candidate implication:** Record observed location, behavior, interpretation, task context, participant characteristics, and alternative causes before changing content.
- **Boundary:** Hesitation can reflect unfamiliarity, disability, pronunciation, language, fatigue, content complexity, or the artificial read-aloud task rather than defective copy.
- **Promotion status:** `limited-formative-reading-signal; contextual-diagnosis-required`.

### `LGW-C512` — message recall tests perceived meaning, not factual or causal validity

- **Locator:** Chapter 14, printed page 295; PDF page 325.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Asking readers what message they received is presented as more useful than asking whether they liked the draft.
- **Candidate implication:** Compare perceived proposition, actor, action, condition, evidence, uncertainty, consequence, and next step against the intended canonical content.
- **Boundary:** Correct recall does not prove the claim is true, the action is usable, the tone is appropriate, or the experience causes a beneficial outcome.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-message-comprehension-check; broader-validity-and-task-testing-required`.

### `LGW-C513` — post-publication comments are inputs, not representative truth

- **Locator:** Chapter 14, printed pages 295–296; PDF pages 325–326.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Comments and social responses after publication can inform future writing.
- **Candidate implication:** Triage feedback by provenance, representativeness, severity, urgency, evidence, abuse risk, privacy, response owner, correction path, and closure.
- **Boundary:** Responders are self-selected; visible feedback can be manipulated, harmful, sensitive, or unrelated to content quality and cannot replace monitoring or structured research.
- **Promotion status:** `limited-post-publication-signal; governed-feedback-and-incident-triage-required`.

### `LGW-C514` — organizational consistency must not erase accountable authorship

- **Locator:** Chapter 14, printed pages 295–296; PDF pages 325–326.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Most organizational content should feel consistent rather than reveal individual writer differences, while signed articles may preserve a personal voice.
- **Candidate implication:** Separate surface voice consistency from source authorship, accountable owner, contributor credit, approval authority, and version history.
- **Boundary:** A seamless “organizational voice” can obscure who made a claim, who has authority, whose perspective is missing, and who can correct harm.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-style-consistency-goal; provenance-and-accountability-preservation-required`.

### `LGW-C515` — feedback disposition should follow evidence, not ego or hierarchy

- **Locator:** Chapter 14, printed pages 294–296 and 301; PDF pages 324–326 and 331.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Authors should receive feedback openly without automatically accepting every suggestion.
- **Candidate implication:** Record each finding, evidence, affected requirement, severity, owner, decision, rationale, change, verification, and unresolved dissent.
- **Boundary:** “Put your ego away” can suppress legitimate expertise or affected-party objection; psychological safety and escalation matter when power is unequal.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-evidence-led-feedback; power-aware-disposition-required`.

### `LGW-C516` — persona walkthroughs are expert inspections, not participant evidence

- **Locator:** Chapter 14, printed pages 296–297; PDF pages 326–327.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Teams can simulate persona scenarios through a site to identify likely trouble before usability testing.
- **Candidate implication:** Use persona walkthroughs as hypothesis-generating inspections with named assumptions, evidence links, route and state coverage, and follow-up research.
- **Boundary:** A fictional persona cannot consent, act, experience disability, reveal unexpected interpretation, or validate real behavior and outcomes.
- **Promotion status:** `limited-pretest-inspection; no-substitute-for-representative-participation`.

### `LGW-C517` — copy editing and developmental editing answer different questions

- **Locator:** Chapter 14, printed pages 297–298; PDF pages 327–328.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Copy editors focus on grammar, spelling, punctuation, and style details; developmental editors focus on purpose, audience, selection, hierarchy, organization, and usability.
- **Candidate implication:** Type review scopes so detailed correctness cannot substitute for content strategy and structural validity, and vice versa.
- **Boundary:** Neither role alone covers domain facts, law, policy, accessibility, localization, security, privacy, operations, affected-party outcomes, or runtime behavior.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-review-scope-separation; multidisciplinary-assurance-required`.

### `LGW-C518` — independent copy review is valuable but not infallible

- **Locator:** Chapter 14, printed page 297; PDF page 327.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Another copy editor is recommended because authors rarely catch every typographical problem in their own work.
- **Candidate implication:** Require independent review proportionate to risk and combine source, diff, rendered, localized, data, and interaction checks.
- **Boundary:** Editors can introduce errors, normalize disputed usage, or review the wrong version; change attribution and post-edit verification are necessary.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-independent-detail-review; versioned-verification-required`.

### `LGW-C519` — review planning belongs at project initiation

- **Locator:** Chapter 14, printed pages 298–299; PDF pages 328–329.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Teams should identify reviewers, roles, schedules, and content plans at the beginning rather than presenting an unexpected final draft.
- **Candidate implication:** Create a review matrix mapping content type and risk to required evidence, reviewer, authority, sequence, deadline, fallback, and release gate.
- **Boundary:** Early agreement can still exclude affected groups, lock in bad assumptions, or become stale as scope, law, systems, and risk change.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-early-review-design; adaptive-risk-based-matrix-required`.

### `LGW-C520` — “no surprise” should mean transparent collaboration, not avoidance of challenge

- **Locator:** Chapter 14, printed page 299; PDF page 329.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Reviewers should understand the planned purpose, audience, scenarios, scope, and style before receiving a draft.
- **Candidate implication:** Share decision context, evidence, proposed changes, risks, unknowns, and review criteria early while retaining a route for dissent and changed evidence.
- **Boundary:** Avoiding surprise must not become preclearance, conformity pressure, concealed findings, or reluctance to surface urgent harm.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-early-context-sharing; dissent-and-escalation-protection-required`.

### `LGW-C521` — review requests need stage, scope, authority, and deadline

- **Locator:** Chapter 14, printed page 300; PDF page 330.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Each reviewer should receive the draft stage, requested help, return date, and a way to renegotiate or ask questions.
- **Candidate implication:** Package review with immutable version, scope, required questions, sources, unresolved items, reviewer role, decision authority, due date, escalation, and acceptance criteria.
- **Boundary:** Individual email is not a durable review system and can fragment comments, expose sensitive content, lose provenance, and produce version conflict.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-specific-review-request; centralized-versioned-review-record-required`.

### `LGW-C522` — reviewer prompts should be semantic and accessible, not color-coded alone

- **Locator:** Chapter 14, printed page 300; PDF page 330.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Brackets, bold names, and different colors are suggested for directing specific questions to reviewers.
- **Candidate implication:** Use structured assignments with reviewer identity, question type, status, priority, source location, response field, notification, and noncolor cues.
- **Boundary:** Color-only distinctions fail for color vision, forced colors, printing, speech, and extraction; embedded prompts can accidentally ship in production.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-targeted-review-question; accessible-structured-comment-system-required`.

### `LGW-C523` — factual conflicts require authoritative resolution, not comment aggregation

- **Locator:** Chapter 14, printed page 301; PDF page 331.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Conflicting factual comments should trigger investigation into the actual answer rather than automatic acceptance.
- **Candidate implication:** Record competing claims, sources, authority, scope, date, uncertainty, resolver, decision, dissent, and verification before release.
- **Boundary:** Consensus, seniority, majority, or stylistic preference cannot resolve empirical, legal, policy, or operational truth.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-conflict-resolution-principle; evidence-and-authority-adjudication-required`.

### `LGW-C524` — reviewer misunderstanding may reveal copy or context failure

- **Locator:** Chapter 14, printed page 301; PDF page 331.
- **Documented practitioner position:** If a reviewer misunderstands a passage, the writer should revise and ask the reviewer to try it again.
- **Candidate implication:** Diagnose whether the cause is wording, missing context, incorrect assumption, structural relation, specialized knowledge, accessibility, localization, or reviewer scope, then retest the changed proposition.
- **Boundary:** One misunderstanding does not prove the text is defective, and one successful reread does not establish broad comprehension.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-misinterpretation-diagnostic; cause-analysis-and-revalidation-required`.

### `LGW-C525` — compromise cannot alter controlled meaning without accountable approval

- **Locator:** Chapter 14, printed page 301; PDF page 331.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Successful legal, policy, technical, and editorial review may require negotiation toward accurate, reliable, and clear content.
- **Candidate implication:** Identify which fields are negotiable presentation, which are controlled facts or obligations, and who can approve scope, risk, exception, and residual uncertainty.
- **Boundary:** Team compromise is not evidence and cannot lawfully average incompatible requirements, conceal risk, or trade away rights, safety, accessibility, or truth.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-cross-functional-negotiation; controlled-meaning-and-authority-boundary-required`.

### `LGW-C526` — review dispositions need persistent rationale and verification

- **Locator:** Chapter 14, printed page 301; PDF page 331.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Authors should summarize changes made and not made across rounds, especially for legal, policy, and technical reviewers.
- **Candidate implication:** Maintain finding ID, version, reviewer, requested change, evidence, decision, rationale, approver, resulting diff, verification, carried risk, and round-to-round lineage.
- **Boundary:** A narrative summary can omit silent edits, unresolved dissent, lost comments, superseded facts, or whether the published artifact matches the approved version.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-review-disposition-foundation; auditable-diff-to-release-lineage-required`.

### `LGW-C527` — publication requires automated and manual release gates absent from the chapter

- **Locator:** Chapter 14, printed pages 289–302; PDF pages 319–332.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The chapter covers human revision, tools, peer feedback, editing, and specialist review but does not define a production gate.
- **Candidate implication:** Gate release on required approvals, unresolved severity, source currency, links, terminology, accessibility, localization, data and media integrity, security, privacy, rendering, metadata, redirects, rollback, and monitoring.
- **Boundary:** A checklist or green automation result proves only the checks it actually covers; exceptions need authority, expiry, mitigation, and audit.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-release-gate-gap; risk-based-mixed-assurance-required`.

### `LGW-C528` — Chapter 14 supplies collaborative revision practice, not complete content operations

- **Locator:** Chapter 14 and summary, printed pages 289–302; PDF pages 319–332.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The chapter combines self-revision, fact and link checking, early reader feedback, persona walkthroughs, editing roles, planned specialist review, negotiation, and change summaries.
- **Candidate implication:** Retain iterative and role-specific review while adding canonical versions, structured findings, evidence lineage, access and locale reviews, automated checks, release authority, correction propagation, monitoring, rollback, retention, and audit.
- **Boundary:** Personal diligence, multiple eyes, read-aloud exercises, personas, compromise, and review summaries do not prove production correctness; current workflow contracts remain unverified because the Developer Index interface is unavailable.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-editorial-collaboration-foundation; modern-content-operations-and-release-runtime-required`.

### `LGW-C529` — a style guide is an operated content product

- **Locator:** Interlude 5, printed pages 303–306; PDF pages 333–336.
- **Documented practitioner position:** A guide should begin with existing decisions, grow from author and editor questions, and remain a small, findable online resource rather than a long book.
- **Candidate implication:** Give the guide users, jobs, canonical entries, search, navigation, ownership, workflow, versions, feedback, analytics, accessibility, localization, and service commitments.
- **Boundary:** Publishing a guide does not create adoption, correctness, enforceability, or consistent outcomes; it must be integrated into authoring and review work.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-style-guide-as-service; operated-product-model-required`.

### `LGW-C530` — consistency is a coordination benefit, not a governing quality

- **Locator:** Interlude 5, printed pages 303–304; PDF pages 333–334.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Shared decisions about formality, usage, spelling, and words in transition can make content consistent across sites, apps, and social channels.
- **Candidate implication:** Use consistency to reduce unnecessary variation while allowing truth, accessibility, user vocabulary, legal scope, locale, community preference, and task context to override a general pattern.
- **Boundary:** Content can be consistently wrong, exclusionary, ambiguous, inaccessible, deceptive, or stale.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-coordination-value; consistency-subordinate-to-semantic-quality`.

### `LGW-C531` — language variation needs explicit scope and rationale

- **Locator:** Interlude 5, printed pages 303–304; PDF pages 333–334.
- **Documented practitioner position:** A guide can settle choices such as contractions, singular “they,” and evolving compounds for an organization or publication.
- **Candidate implication:** Record locale, audience, content type, channel, register, effective date, rationale, examples, exceptions, and source for every scoped language decision.
- **Boundary:** Organizational preference does not control a person's self-identified language, legal record, quoted speech, technical identifier, proper name, community term, or another locale's grammar.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-scoped-language-decision; identity-and-locale-boundaries-required`.

### `LGW-C532` — words in transition require versioned, locale-specific policy

- **Locator:** Interlude 5, printed page 304; PDF page 334.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Organizations choose among changing forms such as hyphenated and closed compounds to maintain consistency during language change.
- **Candidate implication:** Track accepted, preferred, deprecated, prohibited, and legacy forms by locale and date, with migration, search aliases, redirects, pronunciation, and record-preservation rules.
- **Boundary:** Language change is not uniform, linear, or organization-owned; premature enforcement can erase recognition, identity, history, or interoperability.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-language-change-recognition; versioned-variant-governance-required`.

### `LGW-C533` — guide entries should answer observed decisions, not accumulate trivia

- **Locator:** Interlude 5, printed pages 304–306; PDF pages 334–336.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The organization-specific guide should focus on issues contributors question or dispute instead of reproducing a general reference work.
- **Candidate implication:** Require each entry to identify triggering evidence, decision risk, intended users, scope, source, examples, owner, and review or retirement condition.
- **Boundary:** Frequency of questions does not determine importance; rare legal, safety, accessibility, localization, or identity issues may require entries before they generate volume.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-demand-led-scope; risk-and-obligation-inputs-required`.

### `LGW-C534` — examples should expose application and counterexamples

- **Locator:** Interlude 5, “affect/effect” example, printed pages 304–305; PDF pages 334–335.
- **Documented practitioner position:** A topic page explains a distinction, provides examples, and suggests clearer rewrites.
- **Candidate implication:** Store preferred, acceptable, discouraged, prohibited, exception, and before/after examples with rationale, scope, semantic diff, locale, and test fixtures.
- **Boundary:** Examples can be copied outside their scope, become de facto rules, or alter facts and modality when shortened.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-example-led-guidance; typed-scope-and-semantic-verification-required`.

### `LGW-C535` — external reference guides need declared precedence and applicability

- **Locator:** Interlude 5, printed page 305; PDF page 335.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Teams should adopt an existing general guide where possible and keep their own guide focused on local decisions.
- **Candidate implication:** Define the reference hierarchy among law, standards, domain rules, inclusive-language guidance, locale guides, brand guidance, product terminology, and local exceptions.
- **Boundary:** A general guide may be outdated, inaccessible, culturally narrow, commercially licensed, or in conflict with governing authority and community language.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-reference-reuse; explicit-precedence-and-rights-required`.

### `LGW-C536` — inherited organizational rules should be challenged through evidence

- **Locator:** Interlude 5, printed page 305; PDF page 335.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Existing organizational guidance should be considered and collaboratively changed when too formal, old, or incompatible with online content strategy.
- **Candidate implication:** Provide a change proposal with evidence, affected entries and surfaces, stakeholders, compatibility impact, migration, deprecation, approval, and rollout plan.
- **Boundary:** Organizational culture and owner preference must not block corrections required by law, accessibility, safety, inclusion, factual accuracy, or user harm.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-governed-change-principle; protected-escalation-for-mandatory-correction-required`.

### `LGW-C537` — a style-guide owner needs bounded authority and accountability

- **Locator:** Interlude 5, printed page 306; PDF page 336.
- **Documented practitioner position:** A named owner should write and maintain the guide and may convene representatives from different contributor groups.
- **Candidate implication:** Define owner duties, decision rights, consultation requirements, conflicts, response times, succession, audit, and escalation.
- **Boundary:** One owner or committee cannot speak for every locale, disability, identity, discipline, legal authority, product, or affected community.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-accountable-ownership; federated-expertise-and-escalation-required`.

### `LGW-C538` — sponsorship should resource adoption, not manufacture correctness

- **Locator:** Interlude 5, printed page 306; PDF page 336.
- **Documented practitioner position:** A management champion with organizational influence is recommended to endorse the guide and encourage compliance.
- **Candidate implication:** Use sponsorship for resources, integration, training, remediation, and accountability while keeping rule evidence, appeals, exceptions, and independent review visible.
- **Boundary:** Executive “clout” cannot make a rule true, inclusive, lawful, accessible, or appropriate and can suppress dissent or local expertise.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-resource-sponsorship; no-authority-substitution-for-evidence`.

### `LGW-C539` — modular entries need canonical identity and typed relations

- **Locator:** Interlude 5, printed page 306; PDF page 336.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Each style topic should be a small, database-like card reachable through search and links.
- **Candidate implication:** Give each entry a stable ID, title, rule type, scope, rationale, examples, sources, related and superseding entries, status, version, owner, and machine-readable representation.
- **Boundary:** Atomization can fragment qualifications, hierarchy, exceptions, or related concepts and create contradictory cards.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-modular-guide-model; canonical-relation-and-consistency-validation-required`.

### `LGW-C540` — channel variants need semantic parity and explicit constraints

- **Locator:** Interlude 5, printed page 306; PDF page 336.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Different styles may be allowed for different media and situations, such as compressed social messages versus email.
- **Candidate implication:** Define channel-specific register, length, abbreviation, formatting, speech, truncation, permanence, publicness, urgency, and accessibility while preserving canonical meaning.
- **Boundary:** Channel convention cannot authorize loss of material facts, dignity, accessibility, legal meaning, localization, or archive value.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-channel-variation-option; canonical-semantic-parity-required`.

### `LGW-C541` — style-guide search requires aliases and context, not exact-term recall

- **Locator:** Interlude 5, printed pages 304 and 306; PDF pages 334 and 336.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Contributors should be able to find topics through both search and linked navigation.
- **Candidate implication:** Index preferred and nonpreferred forms, common questions, misspellings, synonyms, locale variants, content types, and related concepts while preserving canonical entry identity.
- **Boundary:** Search logs may expose sensitive work and are biased toward current vocabulary; zero results need a feedback or escalation route.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-multi-route-findability; governed-alias-and-query-evidence-required`.

### `LGW-C542` — the guide itself must meet the standards it teaches

- **Locator:** Interlude 5, printed page 306; PDF page 336.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The guide should use clear web writing, examples, and usability testing so authors and editors can find and apply it.
- **Candidate implication:** Validate guide comprehension, task success, accessibility, localization, search, navigation, examples, code integrations, print or export, and feedback workflows.
- **Boundary:** A usable guide can still contain unsound rules, and testing existing contributors alone may miss novice, contractor, global, disabled, or cross-functional needs.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-self-applying-quality-principle; diverse-user-and-rule-validity-testing-required`.

### `LGW-C543` — feedback needs a governed proposal and disposition workflow

- **Locator:** Interlude 5, printed pages 304 and 306; PDF pages 334 and 336.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The sample exposes “Suggest a change,” and the chapter recommends an easy mechanism for questions and new topics.
- **Candidate implication:** Capture proposal, evidence, affected scope, proposer, privacy, urgency, owner, review, decision, rationale, version, migration, notification, and appeal.
- **Boundary:** Easy submission without moderation, service levels, transparency, and protection from retaliation can create a suggestion graveyard or expose sensitive issues.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-feedback-channel; operated-change-disposition-required`.

### `LGW-C544` — style rules need machine-enforcement metadata

- **Locator:** Interlude 5, database-card and example guidance, printed pages 304–306; PDF pages 334–336.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The chapter describes human-readable modular guidance but not automated enforcement.
- **Candidate implication:** Mark each rule as advisory, review-required, autofixable, blocking, or nonautomatable, with detector, locale, confidence, safe fix, exclusions, suppression, and test cases.
- **Boundary:** Not every linguistic or inclusive-language judgment is safely lintable, and automatic fixes can change meaning, names, identity, code, or legal scope.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-enforcement-metadata-gap; explainable-human-reviewed-automation-required`.

### `LGW-C545` — terminology governance is broader than spelling preference

- **Locator:** Interlude 5, printed pages 303–306; PDF pages 333–336.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The interlude centers grammar, spelling, punctuation, usage, and style decisions.
- **Candidate implication:** Connect approved terminology to canonical concepts, definitions, aliases, prohibited or harmful terms, product IDs, legal names, UI strings, translations, pronunciation, search, and records.
- **Boundary:** A preferred word cannot silently rename a product, policy, identity, data field, API, legal entity, or record without coordinated migration and authority.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-language-guidance-base; enterprise-terminology-lineage-required`.

### `LGW-C546` — inclusive-language rules need affected-community and self-identification authority

- **Locator:** Interlude 5, singular-they example, printed page 303; PDF page 333.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The chapter recognizes that organizations make usage decisions, including pronoun conventions.
- **Candidate implication:** Base identity-language guidance on self-identification, affected-community expertise, privacy, context, current sources, locale, records constraints, and a rapid correction path.
- **Boundary:** A style committee or sponsor cannot override a person's name or pronouns, flatten community disagreement, force disclosure, or project English identity categories across languages.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-inclusive-language-authority; self-identification-and-community-review-required`.

### `LGW-C547` — style-guide versions need migration, deprecation, and historical access

- **Locator:** Interlude 5, organic-growth and language-transition guidance, printed pages 303–306; PDF pages 333–336.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The guide is expected to evolve as language and contributor needs change.
- **Candidate implication:** Publish entry and guide versions, effective dates, change logs, supersession, deprecation windows, migration queries, automated checks, notifications, exceptions, and archived historical rules.
- **Boundary:** Immediate global replacement can corrupt quotations, records, legal text, APIs, search, translations, and content whose historical form must remain fixed.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-evolution-principle; controlled-migration-and-record-preservation-required`.

### `LGW-C548` — Interlude 5 supplies a lightweight guide model, not complete language governance

- **Locator:** Interlude 5, printed pages 303–306; PDF pages 333–336.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The interlude recommends a consistent, owned, sponsored, modular, searchable, example-rich, tested, feedback-driven guide that grows organically from real work.
- **Candidate implication:** Retain small findable entries and accountable maintenance while adding rule provenance, authority, scope, precedence, inclusive language, localization, canonical terminology, machine enforcement, versions, migration, deprecation, appeal, adoption, and outcome measurement.
- **Boundary:** Consistency, ownership, sponsorship, usage frequency, and organic growth do not establish correctness; current technical tooling contracts remain unverified because the Developer Index interface is unavailable.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-lightweight-style-guide-foundation; full-language-governance-system-required`.

### `LGW-C549` — usability testing is one research method, not every encounter with content

- **Locator:** Chapter 15, printed page 307; PDF page 337.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The chapter broadly calls any attempt by someone to use or explain developed content a usability test.
- **Candidate implication:** Classify sessions precisely as moderated usability, comprehension study, interview, observation, accessibility evaluation, prototype test, field study, survey, experiment, or another method.
- **Boundary:** Broad labeling obscures method assumptions, consent, sampling, evidence strength, analysis, and what conclusions are valid.
- **Promotion status:** `limited-inclusive-method-definition; precise-research-typing-required`.

### `LGW-C550` — formative testing diagnoses a design before release

- **Locator:** Chapter 15, printed page 307; PDF page 337.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Most web-team usability testing is framed as formative work used to identify what succeeds or fails early enough to improve it.
- **Candidate implication:** State formative questions, prototype fidelity, target decisions, severity criteria, iteration owner, retest plan, and release consequence before sessions begin.
- **Boundary:** Formative findings do not estimate population prevalence, production performance, long-term outcomes, or causal impact without additional design and evidence.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-formative-diagnostic-purpose; bounded-inference-required`.

### `LGW-C551` — team preference is not evidence of user success

- **Locator:** Chapter 15, printed page 307; PDF page 337.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Teams are not typical users, and observation of intended users should replace internal arguments about what works.
- **Candidate implication:** Convert disputed assumptions into researchable questions and define whose tasks, capabilities, contexts, risks, and outcomes the evidence must cover.
- **Boundary:** A few observed participants are not automatically representative, and “what users want” cannot override law, safety, rights, factual truth, or affected nonusers.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-external-evidence-principle; sampling-and-obligation-boundary-required`.

### `LGW-C552` — respectful terminology locates failure in the design

- **Locator:** Chapter 15, printed page 308; PDF page 338.
- **Documented practitioner position:** People should be called participants rather than subjects, and teams should test how the site works rather than test the users.
- **Candidate implication:** Use language that preserves agency, avoids blame, and distinguishes participant, researcher, observer, customer, employee, patient, child, guardian, and affected party roles.
- **Boundary:** Terminology alone does not create ethical practice, informed consent, power balance, privacy, compensation, accessibility, or safety.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-nonblaming-language; substantive-participant-protection-required`.

### `LGW-C553` — participant-facing framing should be accurate, not euphemistic

- **Locator:** Chapter 15, printed page 308; PDF page 338.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The author avoids “test” and “evaluation” with participants and instead invites them to try or work with the site so they do not feel judged.
- **Candidate implication:** Explain purpose, activities, observation, recording, data use, risks, voluntariness, withdrawal, limits, and contact in understandable nonblaming language.
- **Boundary:** Reducing anxiety must not conceal research, evaluation, monitoring, recording, sponsorship, or how findings affect people.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-nonjudgmental-framing; transparent-informed-consent-required`.

### `LGW-C554` — research questions must precede method and tasks

- **Locator:** Chapter 15, six attributes, printed pages 308–309; PDF pages 338–339.
- **Documented practitioner position:** “Real issues” means deciding what the team needs to learn and planning sessions to answer those questions.
- **Candidate implication:** Record decision, uncertainty, research question, construct, population, method rationale, task, measure, analysis, limitation, and stopping rule.
- **Boundary:** A realistic-looking activity can still be incapable of answering the research question or can invite post-hoc interpretation.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-question-first-research; explicit-design-and-analysis-plan-required`.

### `LGW-C555` — recruitment must represent relevant variation rather than a generic user

- **Locator:** Chapter 15, “real people,” printed pages 308–309; PDF pages 338–339.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Participants should represent at least some intended visitors or users.
- **Candidate implication:** Build a sampling matrix across task, expertise, role, access need, device, language, locale, environment, risk, service history, and marginalized or high-consequence groups.
- **Boundary:** Convenience recruits, customers alone, or primary personas can erase affected nonusers, rare obligations, disability, low-connectivity contexts, and people excluded by the current design.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-relevant-participant-principle; explicit-sampling-and-coverage-required`.

### `LGW-C556` — realistic tasks need provenance and safe conditions

- **Locator:** Chapter 15, “real tasks,” printed pages 308–309; PDF pages 338–339.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Scenarios should reflect tasks people genuinely want or find realistic.
- **Candidate implication:** Source tasks from observed or verified needs and specify starting state, goal, data, constraints, stakes, dependencies, success, failure, recovery, and prohibited deception.
- **Boundary:** Artificial tasks can cue wording, presume eligibility, demand sensitive disclosure, trigger distress, or test an action a participant would never choose.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-realistic-task-principle; provenance-safety-and-state-required`.

### `LGW-C557` — observation, self-report, and telemetry are different data types

- **Locator:** Chapter 15, “real data,” printed pages 308–309; PDF pages 338–339.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Moderated sessions combine watching, listening, neutral questions, and notes, whereas unmoderated tools may capture only actions or clickstreams.
- **Candidate implication:** Preserve event, observation, quote, interpretation, facilitator prompt, artifact, system log, and inference as distinct evidence with timestamps and provenance.
- **Boundary:** Behavior does not reveal motive by itself, think-aloud changes behavior, self-report can be inaccurate, and telemetry can be privacy-invasive or context-poor.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-evidence-type-distinction; provenance-and-method-effect-required`.

### `LGW-C558` — researchers cannot simply put bias away

- **Locator:** Chapter 15, “real insights,” printed page 309; PDF page 339.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Teams are urged to set aside assumptions and biases when reviewing data.
- **Candidate implication:** Use reflexivity, preregistered questions where appropriate, independent observation, structured notes, counterevidence, triangulation, dissent, and traceable inference.
- **Boundary:** Bias cannot be removed by intention; recruitment, facilitation, tasks, instrumentation, analysis, sponsorship, and reporting all shape findings.
- **Promotion status:** `limited-debiasing-advice; explicit-reflexive-analysis-controls-required`.

### `LGW-C559` — research value requires implemented and revalidated change

- **Locator:** Chapter 15, “real changes,” printed page 309; PDF page 339.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Teams should preserve what works and change what does not based on findings.
- **Candidate implication:** Link finding to severity, evidence, decision, owner, change, diff, verification, retest, release, monitoring, and residual risk.
- **Boundary:** Acting on every observation can overfit a small sample, introduce regressions, or optimize one group at another's expense.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-findings-to-change-loop; disposition-and-revalidation-required`.

### `LGW-C560` — low-fidelity prototypes enable early learning but constrain inference

- **Locator:** Chapter 15, printed pages 309–310; PDF pages 339–340.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Testing need not wait for a finished product; paper and informal prototypes can expose issues early and cheaply.
- **Candidate implication:** Match fidelity to the question and record which content, interaction, data, performance, accessibility, state, and system behaviors are real, simulated, or absent.
- **Boundary:** Paper cannot validate browser semantics, assistive technology, timing, responsive layout, input behavior, security, personalization, or production performance.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-early-prototype-testing; fidelity-limited-claims-required`.

### `LGW-C561` — informal locations still require ethical and operational controls

- **Locator:** Chapter 15, informal-session example, printed page 309; PDF page 339.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Labs are optional; sessions can occur in offices, homes, conferences, break rooms, or other convenient settings.
- **Candidate implication:** Assess privacy, confidentiality, safety, accessibility, power relationships, interruptions, recording visibility, data security, travel, and participant comfort for each setting.
- **Boundary:** Convenience can create coercion, expose coworkers or household members, reveal sensitive environments, or exclude people who need accommodations.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-location-flexibility; setting-specific-ethics-and-access-required`.

### `LGW-C562` — research tools are optional, but secure evidence handling is not

- **Locator:** Chapter 15, printed pages 309–310; PDF pages 339–340.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Specialized software, video, and formal reports are not always necessary; paper notes and concise findings may suffice.
- **Candidate implication:** Choose the minimum data needed while governing consent, capture, access, encryption, transfer, retention, redaction, deletion, audit, and breach response for every medium.
- **Boundary:** “Informal” does not exempt notes, recordings, screen shares, or observer access from privacy, security, research, employment, or records obligations.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-tool-minimalism; invariant-data-governance-required`.

### `LGW-C563` — observer access changes the research environment

- **Locator:** Chapter 15, printed pages 309–311; PDF pages 339–341.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Project members are encouraged to observe sessions directly or remotely, with recordings used when direct observation is impractical.
- **Candidate implication:** Disclose observer identity and purpose, limit access, prevent participant identification or intervention, secure channels, manage notes, and debrief observations against evidence.
- **Boundary:** Large or senior observer groups can intimidate participants, expose sensitive data, amplify voyeurism, and privilege memorable anecdotes over analysis.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-stakeholder-observation-value; consent-power-and-access-controls-required`.

### `LGW-C564` — reporting depth should match decision, risk, and traceability needs

- **Locator:** Chapter 15, printed pages 310–311; PDF pages 340–341.
- **Documented practitioner position:** A quick agreed list may replace a formal report in some formative settings.
- **Candidate implication:** Define minimum evidence for each decision: question, participants, method, tasks, findings, counterevidence, severity, limitations, recommendations, disposition, and artifact links.
- **Boundary:** Team agreement and speed can erase dissent, uncertainty, sampling limitations, participant voice, and the evidence future reviewers need.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-proportionate-reporting; minimum-auditable-research-record-required`.

### `LGW-C565` — research is a full lifecycle from planning through use

- **Locator:** Chapter 15, printed page 310; PDF page 340.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Usability work proceeds through planning, conducting, analyzing, reporting, and using results.
- **Candidate implication:** Add intake, ethics and risk review, recruitment, consent, accommodation, piloting, data governance, analysis, disposition, revalidation, publication, retention, deletion, and impact monitoring.
- **Boundary:** A five-phase sequence does not itself allocate authority, protect participants, or ensure findings change the released product.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-research-lifecycle-base; expanded-governance-required`.

### `LGW-C566` — fixed participant counts are conventions, not universal sufficiency rules

- **Locator:** Chapter 15, printed pages 310–311; PDF pages 340–341.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Typical formative testing is described as five to fifteen individual sessions; a recurring quick method uses three.
- **Candidate implication:** Choose sample and iteration based on question, heterogeneity, severity, saturation, comparison design, confidence needs, access groups, resources, and decision risk.
- **Boundary:** Three, five, or fifteen participants do not guarantee coverage, saturation, statistical power, accessibility, locale parity, or detection of low-frequency high-harm failures.
- **Promotion status:** `limited-practice-convention; question-and-risk-based-sampling-required`.

### `LGW-C567` — think-aloud is a method with reactivity and access constraints

- **Locator:** Chapter 15, printed pages 310–312; PDF pages 340–342.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Participants commonly perform tasks while verbalizing thoughts, with facilitators asking questions before and after.
- **Candidate implication:** Specify concurrent or retrospective protocol, prompts, silence handling, language, cognitive and speech accommodations, facilitator training, and how reactivity limits inference.
- **Boundary:** Think-aloud can slow tasks, change attention, exclude some participants, expose private reasoning, and produce rationalization rather than natural cognition.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-formative-protocol-option; reactivity-and-accommodation-required`.

### `LGW-C568` — facilitators need neutrality plus safeguarding and escalation capability

- **Locator:** Chapter 15, printed pages 308–314; PDF pages 338–344.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Experienced or trained facilitators should ask neutral questions, make people comfortable, manage discussion, and avoid domination.
- **Candidate implication:** Train facilitators in method, bias, consent, accessibility, language, trauma, distress, sensitive disclosures, safeguarding, incident response, and boundaries.
- **Boundary:** “Neutral” facilitation is not value-free and may be impossible when safety, misinformation, discrimination, or mandatory reporting arises.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-skilled-facilitation; ethics-and-incident-capability-required`.

### `LGW-C569` — continuous lightweight testing needs coverage and governance, not ritual cadence

- **Locator:** Chapter 15, “morning a month,” printed page 311; PDF page 341.
- **Documented practitioner position:** A regular three-person monthly cadence can help teams focus on selected issues and act quickly on observed problems.
- **Candidate implication:** Maintain a coverage map, backlog, sampling rotation, severity rule, evidence record, change verification, and escalation so cadence does not narrow attention.
- **Boundary:** Monthly ritual can repeatedly sample easy participants and low-risk flows while missing rare, seasonal, multilingual, disabled, adversarial, or high-consequence journeys.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-continuous-research-cadence; coverage-and-risk-portfolio-required`.

### `LGW-C570` — disability inclusion must be designed, not appended as “special needs”

- **Locator:** Chapter 15, accessibility note, printed page 311; PDF page 341.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The chapter says to include people with “special needs” to ensure the product works for them.
- **Candidate implication:** Recruit disabled participants across relevant access technologies and contexts, co-design accommodations, pay equitably, test with their own setups where appropriate, and separate conformance from lived usability.
- **Boundary:** The terminology is dated; one disabled participant cannot represent disability, and inclusion in a mainstream sample does not replace targeted accessibility research or standards evaluation.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-disabled-participation-intent; modern-disability-led-research-required`.

### `LGW-C571` — remote moderated research expands reach but changes the task environment

- **Locator:** Chapter 15, printed page 312; PDF page 342.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Remote facilitation can recruit across locations and allow observers to join without travel.
- **Candidate implication:** Govern platform access, installation, permissions, bandwidth, device control, accessibility, language, privacy, recording, technical support, fallback, compensation, and environmental context.
- **Boundary:** Remote reach does not guarantee diversity and can exclude people with limited connectivity, devices, privacy, digital confidence, or platform accessibility.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-remote-reach-option; digital-access-and-platform-bias-controls-required`.

### `LGW-C572` — unmoderated research trades explanatory depth for scale and standardization

- **Locator:** Chapter 15, printed page 312; PDF page 342.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Unmoderated tests may measure whether many people find a fact but are weaker for understanding whether content is sufficient or understood.
- **Candidate implication:** Use unmoderated methods for clearly operationalized behaviors, pilot instructions, instrument errors and abandonment, include support and withdrawal, and pair with qualitative work when interpretation matters.
- **Boundary:** Larger samples do not cure invalid tasks, bots, inattentive responses, device bias, inaccessible tools, privacy risks, or absent explanation.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-scale-depth-tradeoff; validated-instrument-and-triangulation-required`.

### `LGW-C573` — global research requires local language and cultural authority

- **Locator:** Chapter 15, printed page 312; PDF page 342.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Global products should be tested in multiple contexts, cultures, and languages with local or remote facilitators who understand cross-cultural work.
- **Candidate implication:** Govern locale-specific questions, recruitment, translation and interpretation, facilitator authority, consent, incentives, infrastructure, law, analysis, data transfer, synthesis, and decision parity.
- **Boundary:** Research conducted from one country can reproduce colonial power, mistranslate constructs, export data unlawfully, or treat national culture as homogeneous.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-global-context-recognition; local-partnership-and-data-sovereignty-required`.

### `LGW-C574` — simultaneous sessions trade consistency for speed

- **Locator:** Chapter 15, group-setting story, printed pages 312–313; PDF pages 342–343.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Multiple one-to-one sessions can run concurrently with several facilitators, followed by collective discussion, to obtain rapid feedback.
- **Candidate implication:** Standardize core protocol, train facilitators, record deviations, preserve individual evidence before group discussion, and analyze facilitator and room effects.
- **Boundary:** Parallel facilitation increases variance; shared space and later discussion reduce privacy and independence and may contaminate responses.
- **Promotion status:** `limited-rapid-parallel-method; consistency-privacy-and-contamination-controls-required`.

### `LGW-C575` — A/B tests estimate outcome differences under an allocation system

- **Locator:** Chapter 15, printed page 313; PDF page 343.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Fielding alternative versions can show which produces more of a measurable outcome in real use, though it often does not explain why.
- **Candidate implication:** Predefine hypothesis, variants, unit, population, allocation, exposure, metric, guardrails, duration, power, exclusions, interference, novelty, analysis, stopping, and rollout.
- **Boundary:** “Real-world” data can be confounded by implementation, traffic, season, instrumentation, selection, carryover, or concurrent changes; one changed feature does not automatically establish causal mechanism.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-controlled-field-comparison-base; experimental-design-and-inference-controls-required`.

### `LGW-C576` — conversion is not a sufficient definition of “best”

- **Locator:** Chapter 15, A/B section, printed page 313; PDF page 343.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The chapter describes sales, completed forms, pages viewed, and conversion as common winning metrics.
- **Candidate implication:** Pair organization outcomes with comprehension, suitability, accessibility, privacy, burden, error, regret, cancellation, complaints, rights, safety, long-term effects, and affected-party harm.
- **Boundary:** A variant can increase conversion through pressure, confusion, omission, defaults, surveillance, or unsuitable action and still be worse for people.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-conversion-metric-boundary; multidimensional-benefit-and-harm-guardrails-required`.

### `LGW-C577` — qualitative explanation and experiments answer complementary questions

- **Locator:** Chapter 15, printed page 313; PDF page 343.
- **Documented practitioner position:** A/B testing can show outcome differences but not why a version worked, limiting generalization.
- **Candidate implication:** Triangulate experimental effects with interviews, observation, content comprehension, accessibility, logs, and mechanism-focused follow-up without treating post-hoc stories as proof.
- **Boundary:** Qualitative accounts do not automatically explain causal effects, and experimental averages can hide subgroup harm or mechanism differences.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-method-complementarity; disciplined-mixed-method-inference-required`.

### `LGW-C578` — focus groups elicit social discussion, not individual task usability

- **Locator:** Chapter 15, printed pages 313–315; PDF pages 343–345.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Focus groups are suited to opinions, attitudes, desires, and group discussion, while usability sessions observe individual interaction and task behavior.
- **Candidate implication:** Select group discussion for socially constructed meanings or reactions and individual task methods for behavior, preserving each method's evidence boundaries.
- **Boundary:** The chapter's fixed people, time, and “best time” table is conventional rather than universal; individual interviews and participatory group methods span more purposes.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-talk-versus-task-distinction; no-rigid-method-binary`.

### `LGW-C579` — group dynamics are both a limitation and sometimes the phenomenon of interest

- **Locator:** Chapter 15, printed pages 313–315; PDF pages 343–345.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Dominance, conformity, and discrepancy between talk and action make focus groups weak substitutes for usability testing.
- **Candidate implication:** Design group methods around interaction effects, facilitation, turn equity, confidentiality, dissent, power, and whether social negotiation itself matters.
- **Boundary:** Group influence does not make discussion data worthless; it changes the construct being observed and may be essential for collaborative or community decisions.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-group-dynamics-warning; method-purpose-contextualization-required`.

### `LGW-C580` — mixed individual and group sessions need contamination controls

- **Locator:** Chapter 15, printed pages 314–315; PDF pages 344–345.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Individual product use can precede a group discussion so teams receive both behavior and shared reflection.
- **Candidate implication:** Preserve individual results before discussion, disclose confidentiality limits, manage hierarchy and sensitive topics, distinguish evidence types, and analyze changes introduced by the group.
- **Boundary:** Participants may reveal private session details, conform afterward, or be influenced by others in future tasks; combined methods do not automatically triangulate.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-sequenced-mixed-method-option; independence-and-confidentiality-required`.

### `LGW-C581` — finding content is not understanding or using it

- **Locator:** Chapter 15, final section, printed page 315; PDF page 345.
- **Documented practitioner position:** Teams should continue beyond route finding to observe whether people locate the needed proposition, interpret it correctly, and complete the task.
- **Candidate implication:** Measure find, identify, comprehend, decide, act, verify, recover, retain, and downstream outcome as distinct stages.
- **Boundary:** Reaching a page, looking at text, paraphrasing once, or completing a scripted transaction does not prove durable understanding, informed choice, safety, or real-world success.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-content-outcome-depth; multistage-task-and-comprehension-validation-required`.

### `LGW-C582` — content testing must cover meaning, structure, vocabulary, and media together

- **Locator:** Chapter 15, printed page 315; PDF page 345.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The chapter proposes testing content availability, information design, organization, scanning, reading, vocabulary, and interpretation of images.
- **Candidate implication:** Test canonical propositions and the resolved experience across hierarchy, language, media, interaction, states, accessibility, locale, device, and task consequence.
- **Boundary:** A single usability session cannot validate all dimensions, and observed ease cannot prove fact accuracy, legal sufficiency, operational capacity, security, privacy, or absence of harm.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-integrated-content-testing; multidisciplinary-assurance-boundary-required`.

### `LGW-C583` — business goals and user satisfaction need independent outcome accounting

- **Locator:** Chapter 15, closing paragraph, printed page 315; PDF page 345.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The book closes by linking satisfaction of visitor conversations with achievement of business goals.
- **Candidate implication:** Model organization, user, affected-party, public-interest, accessibility, rights, safety, and operational outcomes separately and expose conflicts rather than assuming alignment.
- **Boundary:** Business success can coexist with coercion, exclusion, surveillance, regret, unsuitable action, externalized harm, or unmet public obligations.
- **Promotion status:** `critical-outcome-alignment-boundary; multidimensional-governance-required`.

### `LGW-C584` — Chapter 15 supplies practical formative methods, not complete research governance

- **Locator:** Chapter 15, printed pages 307–315; PDF pages 337–345.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The chapter advocates early iterative testing with representative people and realistic tasks, supports lightweight and remote variations, distinguishes task observation from group discussion, and insists on testing content beyond findability.
- **Candidate implication:** Retain question-led, iterative, method-fit research while adding sampling logic, ethics, consent, accommodation, language access, participant safety, data governance, reflexivity, analysis, evidence strength, experiment guardrails, disposition, revalidation, and production monitoring.
- **Boundary:** Counts and methods are historical conventions; “special needs” language is dated; conversion is not “best”; informal work is not exempt from ethics; and current research-tool contracts remain unverified because the Developer Index interface is unavailable.
- **Promotion status:** `strong-formative-content-research-foundation; modern-ethical-mixed-method-governance-required`.

### `LGW-C585` — bibliography exposes intellectual lineage without validating currency

- **Locator:** “For More Information — A Bibliography,” printed pages 317–322; PDF pages 347–352.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The bibliography identifies books, articles, research, organizations, and websites that informed or extended the book's 2012 practice context, including usability, accessibility, personas, content strategy, information architecture, plain language, forms, mobile, SEO, testing, and older foundational research.
- **Candidate implication:** Preserve citations as a source-discovery map with dates, source types, and availability checks; verify any consequential claim against the cited work and current primary authority before reuse.
- **Boundary:** Inclusion is not endorsement, independent replication, currentness, availability, jurisdictional fit, or permission to copy. Many URLs, organizations, editions, and technical recommendations may have changed.
- **Promotion status:** `retain-as-historical-source-map; claim-level-verification-required`.

### `LGW-C586` — subject index is a navigation artifact, not a weighting model

- **Locator:** Subject Index, printed pages 323–330; PDF pages 353–360.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The index maps the volume's vocabulary and cross-cutting themes to printed pages, spanning accessibility, conversation, content strategy, structure, language, links, media, marketing, testing, and writing.
- **Candidate implication:** Use the index to audit topic coverage and locate relationships that chapter order may obscure.
- **Boundary:** Entry presence, omission, wording, and page frequency do not establish importance, correctness, completeness, semantic ontology, or implementation priority.
- **Promotion status:** `retain-as-navigation-and-coverage-aid; no-normative-weight`.

### `LGW-C587` — website index identifies historical examples, not reusable patterns

- **Locator:** “Index of Web Sites Shown as Examples,” printed pages 331–332; PDF pages 361–362.
- **Documented practitioner position:** The index names the commercial, nonprofit, government, educational, media, and personal websites shown throughout the book.
- **Candidate implication:** Preserve the list as provenance for the book's example corpus and as a prompt to test principles across organization and service types.
- **Boundary:** The named sites are mutable third-party properties observed around publication; screenshots and evaluations do not establish their present behavior, permission for reuse, representative sampling, or universal pattern quality.
- **Promotion status:** `retain-as-historical-example-provenance; no-current-product-authority`.

### `LGW-C588` — author biography and back cover are publisher-positioning paracontent

- **Locator:** “About Ginny Redish,” printed page 333/PDF page 363; back cover, PDF page 366.
- **Documented practitioner position:** These pages present Redish's professional background, clients, awards, teaching and consulting work, and publisher/endorser claims about the book's usefulness and scope.
- **Candidate implication:** Retain authorship and professional-context metadata when evaluating standpoint and intended audience.
- **Boundary:** Biography, testimonials, feature claims, and marketing language are not independent evidence of efficacy, completeness, universality, or current applicability.
- **Promotion status:** `retain-as-authorship-and-publisher-context; no-independent-evidentiary-weight`.

## Initial comparison with completed books

The opening strongly corroborates the completed books on beginning with people's goals and questions, treating words as part of design, integrating content with navigation and behavior, resolving missing facts before drafting, structuring information for recognition, and testing claims rather than polishing isolated sentences. Its distinctive contribution is the sustained task-oriented web-content frame: destination content, search, navigation, hierarchy, page structure, and prose are treated as one answer-and-action journey.

The combined model must be stricter than the book's metaphors and outcome framing. A site is neither a person on a phone nor always a self-service substitute; “conversation” cannot imply that an institution listened or understood; scanning is not universal; engagement, return, sales, adoption, and support reduction do not prove benefit; SEO and legal/accessibility details from 2012 are not current; and complete records sometimes must remain available alongside decomposed answers. Structured task content remains subordinate to truth, authoritative provenance, accessibility, consent, security, privacy, localization, policy, service capacity, and remedy.

Chapter 2 corroborates the completed books on planning before strings, separating organizational intent from user goals, recognizing interpretive context, preferring research to internal assumption, triangulating qualitative and behavioral evidence, and using scenarios to keep tasks and constraints visible. The combined model is stricter about collection and representation: feedback, search logs, analytics, social listening, surveys, interviews, observation, demographics, emotions, values, quotes, and images all need purpose, consent/privacy, provenance, uncertainty, and permitted-use controls. Personas remain fictional projections, never participants, team members, population truth, or decision authorities; scenarios remain hypotheses, and their absence cannot authorize deletion of records or low-frequency obligations.

Interlude 1 corroborates the completed books on treating content as an operated lifecycle with explicit roles, inventory, cross-channel planning, content-aware design, audit action, testing, maintenance, and realistic resources. The combined model replaces page/URL centrality with semantic and runtime lineage, distinguishes consistency from truth, types ownership and decision authority, preserves records and dependencies through disposition, expands workflow beyond publication, and adds accessibility, localization, privacy, security, rights, retention, incident, migration, and archive controls. Business alignment, traffic, SEO, engagement, and conversion remain bounded organization outcomes rather than governing definitions of content quality.

Chapter 3 corroborates the completed books on integrating content with design from the beginning, using representative content rather than abstract placeholders, supporting resizing and alternate modes, keeping meaning independent of color, aligning visual grouping with semantics, and evaluating the resolved experience. The combined model is stricter than its visual heuristics: native preferences precede page widgets; disability is not “special”; brand, device, age, gender, culture, font family, polarity, line length, alignment, capitalization, underline, and italics are contextual rather than universal rules. Responsive content preserves canonical propositions, and visual redesign must pass semantic diff, factual, accessibility, localization, component, and task validation.

Chapter 4 corroborates the completed books on authentic orientation, explicit purpose, task-first hierarchy, direct actions, meaningful link purpose, discoverable search, progressive data collection, and treating ambiguous labels as possible architecture defects. The combined model replaces a single home-page beginning with route/state entry contracts, separates personality and visual credibility from trustworthiness, prevents brand or primary personas from erasing high-consequence minorities, protects sensitive guided personalization, and treats successful exit and refusal as valid outcomes. Search, mobile priority, screen-reader navigation, authentication, tenant identity, permissions, dynamic incidents, and responsive salience require modern semantic and runtime behavior beyond 2012 placement heuristics.

Chapter 5 corroborates the completed books on user-language labels, task-oriented information architecture, meaningful link purpose, decision-supporting details, multimodal equivalence, behavioral validation, and measuring the whole journey rather than isolated strings. Its distinctive contribution is a compact pathway-page model: visitors need to understand the offered set, predict destinations, choose with minimal avoidable effort, and recover when a route is wrong.
